Strings composed in React from class-adjacent state will drift from the plan the same way the frontend's `kindLabel` map drifts from the server's ActionKind set — for which the repo already has `frontenddrift_test.go`, proving the risk is understood on exactly this seam. And `phraseKeyFor` is the tell: it reverse-engineers intent from a tool name because the intent was never sent, and a blind user's whole model of what the agent is doing would be built on that inference. So: an explicit, translatable, **structured** `announce {key, params}` on the event and plan contracts — not prose — so it goes through i18n, reaches the Arabic user in Arabic (U5) and can be spoken with the right `lang` (AX36). Delete the substring matching: the server knows which tool it called. Then the law gains its natural next term — **a capability that stages an action must name that action in a form a person can hear** — and `render.test.tsx` (AX33) asserts the live-region text per state, so an unannounced capability fails CI exactly as an uncompiled one does.

---

# Corner MO — Mobile, touch, and the agent-first small screen

*Method: the PWA shell, the canvas under touch, every agent surface at phone
width, the capture and offline paths, install/push/share plumbing, and the
strategic question of an agent-primary mobile interface. The single grep that
frames the corner: `ontouch|touchstart|touchmove|touchend|pointerType|
gesturechange` across `frontend/src` returns **one** hit, and it is a CSS line
that turns the browser's own gestures off.*

*Six findings from this territory are filed in Corner AX because they are the
same code seen from the other modality — AX2 (nothing opens on a single
activation), AX8 (hover-only plan controls), AX9 (the row↔ghost binding), AX12
(the context menu and its hover-only submenu), AX25 (one responsive breakpoint)
and AX26 (touch targets). What remains here is what only breaks on a device.*

## T1 — parity and live wrongs

#### MO1 · The canvas cannot be panned by touch — the product is spatially frozen on a phone 🔴
- **Evidence** — `BoardCanvas.tsx:128-138` starts a pan only on `e.button === 1 || spaceDown.current`; `e.button === 0` falls through to clearSelection + marquee. `global.css:248` `.canvas-viewport { touch-action: none; }`. `onWheel` (`:60-70`) is the only other pan path, and touch produces no wheel event.
- **Failure** — Worse than "no touch handlers": `touch-action: none` is an **active instruction** to suppress the browser's own pan and pinch inside the canvas, and nothing replaces them. A phone user sees whatever region the last desktop session left and is architecturally unable to reach anything else — no scrollbars, no swipe, no fallback. A filmmaker opening a shoot board on location can read four cards and cannot reach the fifth. Every other finding in this corner is downstream of this one.
- **Mechanism** — Two-pointer gesture arbitration on the viewport keyed off `e.pointerType` and a live `Map<pointerId,{x,y}>` filled in `onPointerDown/Move`. One touch pointer on empty canvas ⇒ PAN (the inverse of today — MO2); two touch pointers ⇒ pinch-zoom about the midpoint, reusing `applyZoom`'s existing world-space anchor maths with a continuous factor. Mouse and pen keep today's semantics exactly. Invariant: `toCanvas` and `setView` remain the only writers of pan/scale, so the line layer, ghosts, remote cursors and `visibleRegion()` all follow for free. The 3px `moved` threshold (`ElementShell.tsx:100`) becomes pointer-type dependent or every tap becomes a move.
- **Hard clause** — **handler** (client-side). Gesture arbitration must not fork the view-state writers.

#### MO2 · One-finger drag on empty canvas is bound to marquee-select — the exact gesture every touch user makes to pan 🔴
- **Evidence** — `BoardCanvas.tsx:133-138` `if (e.button === 0) { clearSelection(); … setMarquee(…); capture(); }`, committing the selection on pointer-up past a 6px box (`:185-200`).
- **Failure** — The slot pan should occupy is occupied by the most surprising alternative. The one canvas gesture that fully works under touch today is rubber-band selection, and it fires on the universal pan gesture. Drag a finger to look around and you select thirty cards; the floating action bar then appears with a Delete button. The failure is not "nothing happens", it is an unintended destructive multi-select every single time you try to navigate.
- **Mechanism** — Marquee becomes a deliberate mode on coarse pointers: a rail toggle beside Draw (`useView.setDrawMode` is the precedent), or long-press-on-empty-canvas, matching platform convention. Fine pointers keep today's binding, so no desktop behaviour moves. Invariant: `clearSelection()` on tap-empty stays — deselect-on-tap is correct and is what makes an explicit marquee mode legible.
- **Hard clause** — **handler** (client-side).

#### MO3 · The only zoom controls in the product are covered by the agent bar on every phone-width screen 🔴
- **Evidence** — `global.css:678-680` `.zoom-cluster { position:absolute; bottom:16px; right:16px; z-index:30 }` with 34px buttons. `agent-bar.css:12-24` `.agent-bar { position:fixed; bottom:22px; z-index:58 }`; `:386-390` `@media (max-width:720px) { .agent-bar { inset-inline:8px; bottom:12px } .agent-shell { width:100% } }`. The resting pill is ~43px tall, so the shell spans full width from 12px to ~55px above the bottom edge — fully enclosing the zoom cluster's 16–50px band, at z-index 58 over 30.
- **Failure** — With pinch gone (MO1), the zoom cluster and its Fit button are the **entire** remaining navigation surface — and the agent bar's own mobile media query, the single width query in the product, is what buries it. `.hint-pill` was explicitly lifted clear of the bar (`agent-bar.css:384` `bottom:78px`) and the zoom cluster was not: a known collision solved once and missed once. It bites only when the agent is enabled, so the agent's arrival is what took navigation away.
- **Mechanism** — The same fix the hint pill already got: `.zoom-cluster { bottom: 78px }` inside the existing query. Better, make the reserve a token — `--agent-bar-reserve` set by that media query and consumed by `.hint-pill`, `.zoom-cluster` and `useAgentShell.BAR_RESERVE` (a hardcoded 150 — MO7). One variable, three consumers, no new layout.
- **Hard clause** — none; one declaration. T1 because it removes the last navigation control on the device the product installs to.

#### MO4 · No `pointercancel` handling anywhere — a cancelled touch leaves the canvas permanently stuck mid-gesture 🔴
- **Evidence** — `BoardCanvas.tsx:293-300` registers wheel/pointerdown/pointermove/pointerup/dblclick/contextmenu/dragover/drop — no `onPointerCancel`. `ElementShell.tsx:180-181` (drag), `:200-201` (resize) and `:434-435` (drag-to-connect) each bind window pointermove/pointerup and nothing else. Repo-wide grep for `pointercancel` → zero.
- **Failure** — `pointercancel` is a mouse-era non-event and a touch-era certainty — fired whenever the browser takes the gesture over (system edge-swipe, incoming call, Android back gesture, assistive overlay). When it fires, `pointerup` never comes: `panDrag.current`, `marquee`, `drawStroke`, `useView.drag` and `lineDraft` stay set with their window listeners bound, so the canvas paints a frozen marquee or a card stuck under an invisible finger and only a reload recovers. The comment at `BoardCanvas.tsx:115-117` shows pointer capture was already reasoned about for "exotic pointer ids"; cancellation is the case not considered.
- **Mechanism** — Every site that registers `pointerup` also registers `pointercancel` to the same handler with a `cancelled` flag so the up-handler commits nothing: a cancelled drag calls `setDrag(null)` and writes no moveOp, a cancelled marquee clears without selecting, a cancelled stroke discards instead of creating a SKETCH. **Invariant: cancellation never commits a transaction** — the difference between a lost gesture and a phantom edit in the undo history.
- **Hard clause** — **handler** (client-side).

#### MO5 · The context menu has no scroll and no upper clamp — in landscape its top rows render off-screen 🔴
- **Evidence** — `ContextMenu.tsx:59-60` `const y = Math.min(pos.y, window.innerHeight - items.length * 34 - 16);` — a `Math.min` with no matching `Math.max(8, …)`. `global.css:740-745` `.context-menu` sets `position:fixed`, `min-width:200px`, and no `max-height`, no `overflow`.
- **Failure** — A selected BOARD builds a 12–14 row menu: 14 × 34 + 16 = 492px. On 375×667 the clamp yields y=175, survivable. In landscape (667×375) it yields y = **minus 117**: the first rows — Duplicate, Make synced copy — are drawn above the top of the screen with no way to scroll to them. The arithmetic also underestimates height (three dividers add ~11px each), so the clamp is optimistic even in portrait.
- **Mechanism** — `const y = Math.max(8, Math.min(pos.y, window.innerHeight - menuH - 8))` with `menuH` **measured** from the rendered node rather than guessed from `items.length`, plus `max-height: calc(100dvh - 16px); overflow-y:auto; overscroll-behavior:contain`. Measure-then-position is one `useLayoutEffect` against the existing ref.
- **Hard clause** — none.

#### MO6 · No keyboard avoidance and no `visualViewport` — the agent composer sits behind the software keyboard while you type into it 🔴
- **Evidence** — Repo-wide grep for `visualViewport` → zero. `.agent-bar { position:fixed; bottom:22px }` (12px at ≤720px). `global.css:100` `html, body, #root { height:100% }` — no `dvh`, no `svh`. `index.html:5` viewport meta has no `viewport-fit=cover` and no interactive-widget hint. `grep "env(" frontend/src/styles` → zero, so no safe-area insets anywhere.
- **Failure** — A `position:fixed` bottom element is positioned against the **layout** viewport, which iOS Safari does not shrink when the keyboard opens. The composer, the revise input, the plan-row rename field and every Tiptap card are therefore drawn underneath the keyboard the moment they take focus — and `AgentAsk.tsx:70` **auto-focuses** the composer 50ms after opening, so the keyboard rises over the field before the user acts. Android Chrome resizes the layout viewport by default, so this is a total iOS failure and a partial Android one: the worst kind, because it passes a desktop-emulator test. Compounding: no `safe-area-inset-bottom`, so on a notched iPhone the pill sits under the home indicator.
- **Mechanism** — One listener, one variable. `window.visualViewport` `resize`/`scroll` writes `--kb-inset: ${innerHeight - visualViewport.height - visualViewport.offsetTop}px` on `documentElement`; `.agent-bar { bottom: calc(12px + var(--kb-inset,0px) + env(safe-area-inset-bottom)) }`. Add `viewport-fit=cover` so `env()` resolves and `interactive-widget=resizes-content` so Android matches. The same `--kb-inset` feeds MO7.
- **Hard clause** — none; one listener and one token.

#### MO7 · The plan list and the auto-framing budget space with desktop constants, so the review card exceeds the screen and frames the plan behind itself 🔴
- **Evidence** — `agent-bar.css:311-316` `.ac-plan { max-height: 42vh; overflow-y:auto }`. `useAgentShell.ts:13` `export const BAR_RESERVE = 150;` and `:82` `const h = Math.max(240, viewport.clientHeight - BAR_RESERVE)`. Above that list the Decide card stacks a summary, PlanNotes, a quarantine warning, a destructive warning, an optional revise row and a seven-control decision row — anchored at bottom, growing upward, with no `max-height` on `.agent-card`.
- **Failure** — Two compounding miscalibrations. (a) `42vh` is a **layout**-viewport unit, so with the keyboard open on a 667px phone the list still claims 280px inside a ~330px visible strip and the card overflows past the top of the screen where nothing clips or scrolls it — the summary and the destructive-changes warning go first, which is the wrong end to lose. (b) `BAR_RESERVE=150` was measured against a desktop pill; the real Decide card on a phone is 400–500px, so `frameProposal` centres the ghosts in a region the card is sitting on. `useAgentShell.ts:3-6` says framing exists so "a user scrolled elsewhere would watch nothing happen" — on a phone the framing itself hides the plan, and with MO1 in force there is no way to pan out from under it.
- **Mechanism** — `BAR_RESERVE` becomes `document.querySelector('.agent-shell')?.offsetHeight ?? 150`, read at frame time — the bar knows its own height. `.agent-card` gains `max-height: calc(100dvh - var(--topbar-h) - var(--kb-inset,0px) - 24px)` with the plan list flexing inside rather than carrying its own `vh` cap. On coarse pointers the review becomes a sheet rather than a stack inside the bar (MO19).
- **Hard clause** — none.

#### MO8 · The service worker caches failed and hijacked navigations as the offline shell 🔴
- **Evidence** — `frontend/public/sw.js:44-52` — `fetch(request).then(res => { const copy = res.clone(); caches.open(SHELL_CACHE).then(c => c.put('/', copy)); return res; }).catch(() => caches.match('/'))`. No `res.ok` check, no `res.type` check, no redirect check. The asset branch twelve lines above (`:36-37`) **does** guard: `if (res.ok) cache.put(...)`.
- **Failure** — The guarded branch is the cheap one; the branch deciding whether the app can ever open again is not. Any 500 from the origin, any 502 mid-deploy, or — the mobile case — any **captive-portal interstitial returning 200 with someone else's HTML** on hotel, airport or café Wi-Fi is written over `/` in the shell cache. From then on the installed PWA opens to the captive portal's login page forever, offline **and** online, because the shell is network-first-with-cache-fallback and the cache is poisoned; only a good load fixes it and the user has no way to know that. This is the exact failure the offline shell exists to prevent, one condition away from correct, and it is distinct from PL12, which is about queuing writes.
- **Mechanism** — `if (res.ok && res.type === 'basic' && !res.redirected) cache.put('/', copy)` — the same guard the asset branch already uses plus the redirect check that catches the captive portal. Second clause: version the shell cache name off the build so a deploy cannot inherit a poisoned entry, and treat a cached shell older than N days as suspect on the first online boot.
- **Hard clause** — **handler.** One condition; T1 because the failure is total and self-perpetuating.

#### MO9 · The asset cache grows without bound across deploys, and on a phone it evicts the offline board mirror it shares a quota with
- **Evidence** — `sw.js:6-7` defines two fixed cache names; `:16-22` the activate handler deletes only caches **not** named SHELL/ASSET, so `qomranote-assets-v1` survives every deploy; `:33-38` puts every hashed asset fetched, forever, with no eviction, no age cap, no size cap. `lib/boardCache.ts:14-15` opens IndexedDB `qomranote` in the same origin quota; its `MAX_AGE_MS` expires snapshots by age but reclaims nothing under pressure.
- **Failure** — Vite emits content-hashed bundles, so every deploy adds a complete new set and retires nothing. On desktop this is invisible. On a phone the origin quota is a fraction of remaining device storage and browsers evict **per-origin, all-or-nothing** under pressure — so what gets thrown away is not just stale JS but `boardCache`, the entire offline board mirror, which is the only reason the app is useful without signal on a location shoot. Silent, and it looks like "the app forgot my boards".
- **Mechanism** — On activate, enumerate ASSET_CACHE keys and delete anything not referenced by the freshly fetched `index.html` — a two-line diff against the shell's script/link hrefs, the standard precache-manifest trick without adding a build plugin. Additionally call `navigator.storage.estimate()` on boot and trim above ~80% usage before IndexedDB is at risk. `boardCache` should also prune to the N most recent boards, not only by age.
- **Hard clause** — **handler.**

#### MO10 · The manifest and document head are below the installability bar on both mobile platforms *(corrects PL4)*
- **Evidence** — `manifest.webmanifest` in full: name, short_name, description, start_url, display, background_color, theme_color, and one icon `{src:'/icon.svg', sizes:'any', type:'image/svg+xml', purpose:'any'}`. No `scope`, `id`, `lang`, `dir`, `orientation`, `display_override`, maskable icon or raster 192/512. `index.html:2-9`: `<html lang="en">`, viewport meta without `viewport-fit=cover`, `<link rel="icon" href="/icon.svg">` — and **no** `apple-touch-icon`, no `apple-mobile-web-app-capable`, no `apple-mobile-web-app-status-bar-style`, no `apple-mobile-web-app-title`.
- **Failure** — PL4 names the missing share_target/file_handlers/shortcuts and rests on "the PWA already exists". iOS Safari ignores manifest icons and requires `apple-touch-icon`, so Add-to-Home-Screen yields a screenshot-of-the-page icon. No `purpose:maskable` gives the Android launcher a white plinth. **No `scope`** means a LINK card opened inside the standalone window (`cards.tsx:158` `window.open`) can navigate the app window to an arbitrary site with no browser chrome and no back button. And for a bilingual product the manifest carries no `lang`/`dir` at all while `index.html` hardcodes `lang="en"` and `setLanguage` never sets `dir` — so the installed app is English-only at the OS level regardless of the chosen language.
- **Mechanism** — Add `scope:'/'`, `id:'/'`, `lang`, `dir:'auto'`, `orientation:'any'`, `display_override:['standalone','browser']`, and a real icon set: 192/512 PNG `purpose:'any'` plus a 512 `purpose:'maskable'` with safe-zone padding. In `index.html`: `apple-touch-icon` (180×180 PNG), the three `apple-mobile-web-app-*` metas, and `viewport-fit=cover` (which MO6 also needs). Independently, `setLanguage` should set `documentElement.dir` alongside `lang` — it already owns that line (CV9, AX28).
- **Hard clause** — none; asset and metadata work. T1 because PL4, MO12 and MO18 are all blocked behind it.

#### MO11 · No install affordance — `beforeinstallprompt` is never captured, so the PWA installs only for someone who knows the browser menu
- **Evidence** — Grep for `beforeinstallprompt`, `getInstalledRelatedApps`, `display-mode`, `navigator.standalone` across `frontend/src` → zero. `main.tsx:17-22` registers the SW and nothing else. Nothing in the app ever knows whether it runs installed or in a tab.
- **Failure** — Installation is the precondition for everything else that matters here — Web Push on iOS requires home-screen installation (MO12), share targets require installation, and the offline shell only pays off installed. Shipping a manifest and never inviting anyone to install it is why the PWA is a promise rather than a product. The subtler half: because nothing detects `display-mode: standalone`, the app cannot adapt when it **is** installed — a standalone window has no browser back button, which makes MO10's missing `scope` and the `window.open` link path a one-way trip.
- **Mechanism** — Capture `beforeinstallprompt` into a store, suppress the default, and surface a quiet install row in the avatar menu or Settings → Appearance calling `prompt()`; hide it when `matchMedia('(display-mode: standalone)').matches` or `navigator.standalone`. Expose that same match as a `standalone` flag the shell reads so an installed window renders its own back affordance on the breadcrumb. A few dozen lines, and it unblocks MO12 and PL4.
- **Hard clause** — none.

## T2 — collaborator

#### MO12 · Web Push does not exist — an agent run's outcome can never reach the phone, the only device the user carries onto a set
- **Evidence** — Grep across `backend/` for `webpush|vapid|PushSubscription` → zero. `sw.js` has no `push` and no `notificationclick` listener (it registers install, activate, fetch). Grep across `frontend/src` for `Notification.requestPermission`/`showNotification` → zero. `NotificationsBell.tsx:22` and `realtime/socket.ts:126` show the only delivery channel is `notification.new` over the board WebSocket — delivered only to a tab that is open, on that board, right now.
- **Failure** — Composes with rather than restates MP10, which is about **producing** an agent-run notification record and whose etiquette clauses assume "the outcome lands as a notification". MP10 fixes the producer; there is still no transport to a closed phone. Every item in the autonomy wave (A1 schedules, A3 watcher, A4 digest) and LP10's parked-run inbox is defined as landing asynchronously — and asynchronous delivery to someone on location with the browser closed means push or it means nothing.
- **Mechanism** — VAPID keypair in config; a `push_subscriptions` collection keyed by `{sub, endpoint}`; registration behind explicit opt-in in Settings — **never prompt on boot**, since an unprompted dialog is a permanent denial on iOS. A `push` handler in `sw.js` renders `event.data.json()` through `showNotification`, and `notificationclick` focuses an existing client or opens `/?board=<id>&run=<id>` so the run adopts itself into the bar (`agentStore.adopt` exists). Etiquette: push carries MP10's payload verbatim and honours the same per-kind preference switchboard — one transport, one policy, no second notion of what is worth interrupting someone for. Requires MO10/MO11 first.
- **Hard clause** — **handler.** One producer, one policy; a second notification vocabulary is how etiquette rots.

#### MO13 · The phone camera is not an input at all, and an ordinary phone photo is rejected by the agent's own byte budget *(deepens IN5, corrects X1/PL3)*
- **Evidence** — Grep for `capture=` across `frontend/src` → zero; the four file inputs have no capture attribute. `AgentAsk.tsx:248` accepts png/jpeg/webp/gif/pdf — no HEIC/HEIF; `vision.go:42-51 visionTypes` likewise. `vision.go:29` `const maxImageBytes = 4 << 20` and `:96-98` `if att.Size > limit { return … "%s is too large to read" }`. `api/client.ts:196-207 uploadFile` PUTs the raw File — no resize, no re-encode, no `createImageBitmap` anywhere.
- **Failure** — IN5 proposes the whiteboard-photo→elements capability and assumes the photo arrives. It cannot. A modern phone camera writes 3–8 MB per frame, so the **typical** case exceeds `maxImageBytes` and the fetcher refuses it — the digest then falls back to describing the image by filename, so the run proceeds and produces a confident answer about an image it never saw. That silent degradation is the dangerous half. Separately, iPhone photos are HEIC: nothing in the pipeline lists it and the upload service has no allowlist at all, so a HEIC becomes an IMAGE card that renders broken in every non-Safari browser and is invisible to the agent. For a filmmaker photographing a storyboard or a location, the most natural capture is the one the pipeline cannot accept.
- **Mechanism** — Client-side normalisation at the one choke point that already exists: `uploadFile` gains an image branch decoding via `createImageBitmap`, drawing to an `OffscreenCanvas` capped at ~2000px long edge, re-encoding under 3.5 MB before presigning — fixing HEIC, EXIF orientation and the byte budget in one pass, keeping the original as a second attachment where fidelity matters. Add `capture='environment'` as a **second** explicit "Take a photo" input beside the picker (never on the picker itself — that removes the library). Add `image/heic,image/heif` to the composer accept so iOS transcodes rather than greying files out. **Invariant: the agent never silently proceeds on an image it could not read — `vision.go`'s refusal must surface as an `unmet` on the plan, not as a filename in the digest.**
- **Hard clause** — **handler.** The unmet, not the resize, is the clause that stops a confident answer about an unseen image.

#### MO14 · Uploads are one unresumable PUT with no progress, no retry and no abort — the worst possible shape for mobile data
- **Evidence** — `api/client.ts:196-207` — presign → a single `await fetch(uploadUrl, {method:'PUT', body:file})` → completeUpload. No AbortSignal, no retry, no progress, no multipart. Limits are `maxImageSize = 100<<20` and `maxFileSize = 5<<30` (`upload_service.go:29-31`). The canvas drop path swallows failure to the console (`BoardCanvas.tsx:235-237`); the composer path toasts generically and drops the file.
- **Failure** — A 5 GB ceiling reachable only through a single non-resumable request is a desktop-LAN assumption. On mobile data — or on location with one bar — an upload that dies at 90% restarts from zero with no indication it was ever running: there is no spinner on the canvas path at all, so dropping a video onto a board from a phone shows nothing happening and no error. For a filmmaker that is the primary content type. It also gates MO13: even the normalised image path needs to survive a flaky connection.
- **Mechanism** — Thread an `AbortController` and a progress callback through `uploadFile` (XHR `upload.onprogress`, or a streamed fetch body where supported); render a per-upload chip with percentage and cancel on the canvas and in the composer; retry with backoff on network-class failures. True resumability needs multipart from the presigner — a larger change — but progress, cancel and honest failure are local to one function and remove the silent-loss case immediately.
- **Hard clause** — none.

#### MO15 · No lifecycle listeners at all — a backgrounded phone returns to a stale board and a stale run, with no signal that it is stale
- **Evidence** — Grep across `frontend/src` for `visibilitychange`, `'online'`, `'offline'`, `navigator.onLine`, `pagehide`, `freeze`/`resume` → **zero**. Freshness depends entirely on `realtime/socket.ts:133-138`: `ws.onclose` sets `hadDisconnect` and reconnects with backoff `min(delay*2, 15_000)`; only then does `:53-63` refetch the board and `resync()` an in-flight run.
- **Failure** — Mobile browsers suspend background tabs aggressively, and iOS tears down WebSockets almost immediately. The user's actual pattern — open the board, switch to the camera, take a photo, come back — guarantees a suspended socket. On resume the close event fires and the reconnect waits out a backoff that may already have grown to 15 seconds, during which the canvas shows a stale board and the agent bar shows a run state minutes out of date, with nothing on screen saying so. Worse for a run: `loadBoardState` only recovers a run on the board you return to, so a run that finished while the phone was in a pocket leaves the bar sitting on a stale PROPOSED card that a tap could Apply against a fingerprint that has since moved.
- **Mechanism** — A `visibilitychange` handler that, on visible, resets the backoff to its floor, reopens the socket if not OPEN, and calls `refreshBoard()` + `agentStore.resync()` unconditionally — the two functions the reconnect path already calls, triggered by the event that actually indicates staleness. `online`/`offline` drive a one-line connection banner and gate the composer's send honestly, which is also the minimum honest version PL12 asks for. **Invariant: the agent bar never offers Apply on a run whose state has not been confirmed since the app became visible.**
- **Hard clause** — **handler** (client-side); composes with FR11 and FR17.

#### MO16 · A touch drag broadcasts a presence cursor that never leaves — mobile collaborators haunt everyone else's board
- **Evidence** — `BoardCanvas.tsx:141-145` calls `sendCursor(pt.x, pt.y)` for every pointer type, throttled to 50ms. There is no `onPointerLeave`, no `onPointerCancel` and no null-cursor emission anywhere; the cursor clears only on `presence.leave`. It renders as a named arrow at `:323-328`.
- **Failure** — A mouse cursor has a continuous resting position, so "last known position" is a reasonable model. A finger does not exist between touches. Every touch gesture therefore leaves a permanent named arrow frozen at the lift point on every other collaborator's canvas, looking exactly like a person standing still and watching. On a shared production board that is actively misleading about who is present and where — the one thing presence is for. It also means a phone participant streams 20 messages/second of finger position that benefits no one (SC16).
- **Mechanism** — `sendCursor` takes the pointer type: mouse and pen behave as today; touch emits on move and emits a **null** cursor on pointerup/pointercancel, which `presence.cursor` already supports structurally and which `BoardCanvas.tsx:285` already filters on. One extra call at gesture end and the ghost disappears.
- **Hard clause** — none.

## T3 — frontier

#### MO17 · On a phone the agent should be the primary interface, and the architecture already supports it
- **Evidence** — The composer is a bottom-anchored bar that already goes full-width below 720px — the only surface in the product ever laid out for a phone (AX25). `useAgent.setOpen` already opens it from three entry points; `AgentBar.tsx:45-56` is already a complete state machine over ask → work → decide → done; `visibleRegion()` already hands the server a viewport; the server already lays out every canvas a plan writes to. Against that: no pan (MO1), no tap-to-open (AX2), no reachable context menu (AX12), no responsive shell (AX25).
- **Mechanism** — Direct manipulation degrades roughly with the square of the screen shrink — dragging a 300px card around a 375px viewport is miserable in a way no amount of gesture work fixes — while **asking is scale-invariant**, and in fact gets better on a phone, which has dictation, a camera and a share sheet the desktop does not. The honest conclusion is not "make the canvas touch-friendly and keep the agent secondary": at ≤640px the canvas should demote to a **view** (pan/zoom/read/tap-to-open per MO1–MO4, AX2) and the agent should become the **verb** surface. So: a mobile mode, not a mobile app. The rail's 18 creation tools collapse to one `+` and the agent pill becomes the persistent bottom surface, expanded by default; the composer gains three mobile-native inputs — camera (MO13), share-sheet intake (MO18), dictation; scope defaults to `viewport` rather than `board`, because on a phone what you can see is a genuinely meaningful selection and `visibleRegion()` already computes it; presets become thumb-sized verbs. **Invariant: mobile mode adds no capability the desktop lacks and removes none from the desktop** — it is a different projection of the same run lifecycle, so there is one agent, one authority model, one review vocabulary. Nothing on the backend changes.

#### MO18 · The share sheet should hand its payload to the agent, not to a card *(corrects PL4)*
- **Evidence** — PL4 specifies `share_target` → `/capture` → "opens the composer pre-filled with intent and attachmentIds". `App.tsx:78-86` reads exactly two boot params (share, board). `sw.js:27` `if (event.request.method !== 'GET' …) return;` — a share_target POST is explicitly not handled by the service worker.
- **Mechanism** — Two things PL4 does not say. **(a) Mechanically**, the POST form of `share_target` is served by the **service worker**, not the server — the SW must intercept `/capture`, read the FormData, stash the files, and redirect to the SPA. `sw.js`'s non-GET early return means PL4 as written falls through to the network and hits a route that does not exist; that one line has to change for any of it to work. **(b) Conceptually** the destination is wrong: filing a shared link as a LINK card reproduces the desktop's manual model on the device least suited to it — you still have to find the board, place it and title it by finger. The share sheet's real value is that it arrives **with context** the agent can act on: a URL, a photo, a paragraph, and the board you were last on. So: `share_target { action:'/capture', method:'POST', enctype:'multipart/form-data' }` received by a new POST branch in `sw.js` writing to a share-inbox cache and `Response.redirect('/?capture=<id>')`; the SPA opens the composer pre-filled with the files attached and a proposed intent — "file this where it belongs" — **under PREVIEW autonomy always, never auto**, because an intent the user did not type is exactly the case where a ghost preview is the whole safety argument. Target defaults to the last board's Unsorted. Composes with MO13's normalisation and PL12's intent queue.

#### MO19 · Ghost review is spatial by construction, and on a phone the space does not exist
- **Evidence** — `GhostLayer.tsx:141-148` renders ghosts as absolutely-positioned children of `.canvas-layer` at server-computed board coordinates. `frameProposal` is the only mechanism bringing them into view, runs once per run, and after that "the canvas belongs to the person" (`AgentDecide.tsx:129-130`) — which on a phone means unreachable, because there is no pan (MO1) and the framing reserve is wrong (MO7). The plan list is capped at 42vh inside a bottom bar and its hover linkage is dead (AX8, AX9).
- **Mechanism** — The deepest strategic consequence of MO1. QomraNote's central trust claim is that the canvas IS the review surface — and that claim is about a screen large enough to hold a plan and a person able to move around it. On 375×667 a 30-action plan spanning 3,000 board units cannot be seen in place at any zoom that keeps text legible, and cannot be walked; fixing pan does not fix this, it makes review a scavenger hunt. So mobile needs a **second projection of the same plan**, and the data already exists: every action carries kind, title, destination, parentId, position and `because`. **Review-by-sequence** on coarse pointers: one action per screen, full-bleed, showing the row's text, its destination, its `because`, and a **locally framed** canvas thumbnail (`frameProposal`'s maths narrowed to that one box, so the person sees where it lands without navigating there), with three thumb-sized verbs — keep, drop, send elsewhere — from the existing closed Adjustment vocabulary. Swipe advances; a summary screen carries Apply/Revise/Discard. **Nothing new is computed**: a different renderer over `useEffectivePlan()`, which is exactly why it is safe. Composes with LP3's risk banding (show TOUCHES-EXISTING and destructive individually, collapse the additive tail to one screen: a 41-action plan is four screens, not forty-one) and it is AX32's accessible review mode with a different skin.

#### MO20 · Capture and arrangement are different devices, and the product has no notion of a handoff
- **Evidence** — The mobile-capable half is exactly: the Unsorted tray's quick-capture field, file drop, and the agent composer. The arrangement half — marquee, drag, columns, connectors, resize, group-into-column — is entirely mouse-bound. `api.moveElements(movable, crumbBoard)` (`ElementShell.tsx:139`) already implements "file this into another board's Unsorted" as a first-class server operation, and `loadBoardState` already computes a per-board drift signal that costs no tokens.
- **Mechanism** — Every finding above treats the phone as a degraded desktop. The productive framing is that it is a different half of one workflow: on set you **capture** (photograph the whiteboard, dictate a note, share a location link, tick a task); at the desk you **arrange**. The product has the storage layer for this — Unsorted is literally a per-board capture inbox — and no product surface acknowledging it. Three composable pieces over existing machinery. (1) Mobile default destination is Unsorted, **always** — a capture from a phone never asks where it goes, because choosing a location by finger is the tax this whole corner is about. (2) A resume prompt: on opening a board on a large screen with N items captured since the last desktop session, the free drift signal offers one agent run — "12 things arrived from your phone; file them?" — which is `scope:'unsorted'` and the existing organise workload verbatim, under preview autonomy. (3) Capture provenance: mark elements created from a mobile session so the resume prompt and the digest can say "these came from the field", which is genuine context for the model (a whiteboard photo and a typed note are different raw material). Provenance is one field on the create op; the rest is composition.

---

# Corner SC — Scale, cost, and the physics of a big board

*Method: product rendering, realtime, Mongo and the write path, plus agent digest
cost, cache economics, walk cost, run latency and concurrency. Four measurement
probes were written as Go test files against `internal/repository/memory`, run,
and deleted; the tree is unchanged. Headline numbers, all measured: a 400-item
digest is 50,484 bytes ≈ **12,621 tokens**, re-billed at full price on every one
of up to 24 turns = **$1.51** against a **$0.35** cap. The cached prefix is
4,887 (system) + 5,887 (tool catalogue) ≈ 10.8k tokens. Scope-compile round
trips: 38 / 110 / 232 for workspaces of 397 / 2,029 / 9,331 elements.
Addressable coverage over that series: 54% / 15% / **2%**.*

*One finding from this territory — the 5-minute reconciler killing live runs — is
filed as FR1, because its consequence is a failure-UX consequence.*

## T1 — parity and live wrongs

#### SC1 · The digest is never prompt-cached, and the intent sits in front of it — the stability work in the scope compiler cannot pay off 🔴
- **Evidence** — `cognition/anthropic.go:77-88`: the **only** `CacheControl` in the codebase sits on the System block; `anthropicMessages` (`:133-173`) never sets cache control on any message block. `planner.go:493` passes `System: systemPrompt` (a const) and `Messages: messages`, where `messages[0]` is `openingMessage(scope, task)` — and `planner.go:650` writes `REQUEST ⟨user⟩: %s\n\n` **before** `scope.Render("")`. So the per-run-varying intent is the first bytes of the message.
- **Failure** — `digest.go:442-444` states the intent explicitly: "Stable ordering makes the compiled context byte-identical for an unchanged board, which is what lets the prompt cache hit at all." It cannot. The digest is in `Messages`, nothing in `Messages` carries a breakpoint, so the sort is doing work no cache reads. Even with a breakpoint added there, a cache prefix must match from byte 0 — the intent ahead of the digest guarantees divergence at the first character every run. Measured: re-sending a 12,621-token digest over 24 steps = 302,904 input tokens = **$1.51** at the registered rate, against `MaxCostUSD` of **$0.35**. So the cost cap binds at turn ~5 of 24 on a board at the scope ceiling: the largest thing in the prompt is the one thing not cached, and it silently converts `MaxSteps=24` into `MaxSteps=5` as the workspace grows.
- **Mechanism** — (a) Restructure `openingMessage` so the stable digest is its own leading content block and the varying intent and nudge follow it — the ordering discipline `digest.go` already applies internally, extended one level out. (b) Give `cognition.Message` an opt-in `CacheBreakpoint` and set it on the digest block and on the last content block of the second-to-last user turn (incremental conversation caching), so each turn reads the prior prefix instead of re-billing it. Anthropic allows four breakpoints; one is used.
- **Hard clause** — **probe.** Assert non-zero `CacheReadInputTokens` on turn 1 of a second run over an unchanged board — otherwise this regresses invisibly, exactly as it did the first time.

#### SC2 · `cache_creation_input_tokens` is never read, so the spend meter under-reports every run by the cost of its own cache write 🔴
- **Evidence** — `cognition/anthropic.go:98-105` builds `Usage{InputTokens, OutputTokens, CachedTokens: CacheReadInputTokens}`. `grep -rn "CacheCreation|cache_creation" backend/` → nothing. `provider.go:243-252 CostUSD` sums three terms — input, output, cached-read — and `provider.go:190-195 Price` carries no cache-write rate.
- **Failure** — The API reports `input_tokens` exclusive of both cache reads and cache writes, and cache writes bill **above** the base input rate. The first turn of every run writes the ~10.8k-token prefix and that write is recorded as $0. `run.Usage.CostUSD` is what `MaxCostUSD` is checked against, what `spentToday` sums for the daily cap, what the Audit view shows the user, and what PL6's whole redesign builds on. Every one is systematically low by the cache-write component — and the error grows precisely when the cache is doing the most work, the opposite of the direction an operator would guess. It also makes SC1 unmeasurable: adding breakpoints raises real first-turn spend while the meter shows it falling.
- **Mechanism** — Add `Usage.CacheWriteTokens` and `Price.CacheWritePer1M`, populate from `CacheCreationInputTokens`, include the term in `CostUSD`. The existing `price_test.go` fixture table is the right place for the assertion. **Ship this before SC1**, so the caching change is evaluated against a meter that can see it.
- **Hard clause** — **compiler.** The price table and the usage struct must change together or the ledger silently keeps a fourth term at zero.

#### SC3 · On a real workspace the scope shows container names and zero contents, and the header reports the scope's size as the board's size 🔴 *(corrects CG12's premise)*
- **Evidence** — Measured with a throwaway probe over `repository/memory` (30 sub-boards × 10 columns × 30 cards = 9,331 elements). Verbatim first lines: `BOARD …001 "Film" — 230 items to organize` / `BOARD …002 "Board 0" — 10 columns @A1` / `    …003 · COLUMN · ⟨user⟩ · Col 0` / `        … and 30 more inside (read_board …003 for the rest)`. **All 300 columns rendered a title and then that elision line — not one card.** Header string is `digest.go:719` `"BOARD %s %q — %d items to organize"` with `len(s.Items)`. Series: 397 elements → 199 shown / 213 addressable (54%); 2,029 → 128 / 296 (15%); 9,331 → 230 / 230 (**2%**). Digest tokens across that series: 5,636 → 4,242 → 7,906.
- **Failure** — CG12 frames the defect as "the 400 elements the model sees are the ones the tree happened to reach first" and prescribes relevance-ordered admission of the same budget. The measurement says something worse: at 9,331 elements the model sees 230, never 400, and what it sees is **exclusively structure** — every leaf elided, so "organise the harbour material" is answered by a model that has read no card text at all. And the header is not merely elided but wrong in kind: `len(s.Items)` is the count of what got **in**, printed in the slot a reader parses as the count of what is **there**. A run asked "how many cards do I have?" reads 230 for a 9,331-element workspace and answers with total confidence; "organise everything" plans against 2% of the board. Every honesty property the digest was built for is undermined by one `%d` in the first line.
- **Mechanism** — Three separable fixes. (a) The header states both numbers or neither — "— 230 of ~9,331 items visible here" — sourced from `CountsByParent`, which DA16 shows is already built, aggregated and exposed with no agent caller; this is the second consumer that makes DA16 pay. (b) Two-pass allowance so the budget is actually spent (SC20). (c) **Guarantee a floor: no container may render zero children while budget remains** — showing 3 of 30 cards is categorically different from showing none, because it is the difference between "a column about casting" and "a column".
- **Hard clause** — **digest.** Lands with CG12 and IN17, which rewrite the same admission policy on the relevance and attention axes.

#### SC4 · Scope compilation is one query per container — 232 serial round trips before a token is spent 🔴
- **Evidence** — `digest.go:465-467 admitChildren` opens with `elements.Children(ctx, ElementFilter{ParentID: c.ID})`, and `:423-430` calls it once per container in the frontier per level, then again for the leftover frontier at `:437-441`. Measured with a counting repository: 397 elements → **38** round trips; 2,029 → **110**; 9,331 → **232**. Meanwhile `repository/mongo/elements.go:63-84 Descendants` already batches an entire level into ONE `$in` query, and `:409-420 loadWithAncestors` does the same upward.
- **Failure** — This is the read half of DA23's argument and DA23 does not cover it — DA23 concerns `verifyOpScope`'s ancestor walk at **commit** time. This is scope **compile** time, on every run including the ones that change nothing, and it is strictly worse than a method fifty lines away in the same file. The round trips are serial awaits inside `execute` before `StateRunning` is reached: at 2ms in-VPC RTT ~0.5s; against Atlas across a region at 20ms, **4.6 seconds of dead time on every run**, charged to the deadline (which FR1 shows is really ~2.5 minutes) and invisible in the journal, because the "read board — N items in scope" event fires once, after all of it. It scales with workspace **width**, which is exactly where a film production board goes.
- **Mechanism** — `admitChildren` becomes `admitLevel(frontier, share)`: one `ChildrenOf(ctx, parentIDs []string)` over the existing `{location.parentId, deletedAt}` index — the same `$in` shape `Descendants` uses — then partition by parent in Go and apply the per-container allowance exactly as today. O(containers) becomes O(depth), and depth is capped at 4.
- **Hard clause** — **probe.** A counting-repository ceiling: compiling a 9,331-element workspace must issue ≤ 6 reads. DA23 notes the suite "does not currently know how to assert" a query count; the wrapper is fifteen lines and this is the cheapest place to teach it.

#### SC5 · Eleven hot components subscribe to the whole store, so a collaborator's cursor re-renders every card on the board — `memo()` is defeated 🔴
- **Evidence** — zustand ^5.0.3; a hook called with no selector re-renders on every `set`. Eleven no-selector subscriptions on the hot path: `BoardCanvas.tsx:27` and `:28`, `ElementShell.tsx:44`, `LineLayer.tsx:43`, `ColumnView.tsx:16`, `TaskListView.tsx:16` and `:220`, `cards.tsx:46` and `:401`, `UnsortedTray.tsx:12`, `AgentAsk.tsx:45`. **`ElementShell` is wrapped in `memo` at `:42` and then subscribes at `:44`**, which makes the memo unreachable for any store change. Producer: `socket.ts:97-102` → `boardStore.ts:298` `set(s => ({ presence: {...s.presence, [id]: u} }))` — a new root state object — at `sendCursor`'s 20 Hz per peer.
- **Failure** — This is the mechanism behind "no windowing, only `memo()`": the memo is not merely insufficient, it is **inoperative**. On a 1,000-element board with four collaborators, pointer movement alone produces ~80,000 component re-renders per second with nothing on the board changing. `setRemoteEditing` does the same. Separately `useView(s => s.drag)` at `ElementShell.tsx:45` gets a fresh object on every `setDrag`, so dragging **one** card re-renders all 1,000 shells at pointer rate; and `BoardCanvas.tsx:144` writes `lastPointer` into the store on every pointermove while `:28` subscribes wholesale, so moving the mouse over empty canvas reconciles 1,000 memo'd children at 120 Hz. For a filmmaker with a wide board and an editor on the same canvas, this is the entire felt performance of the product.
- **Mechanism** — Selectors, then a size cap. (a) Replace all eleven with field selectors, and where a shell needs only its own membership select the derived boolean (`useBoard(s => s.selection.has(id))`) so the subscription is false→false and does not fire. (b) Move `presence`, `remoteEditing` and `lastPointer` out of the stores every card reads — `lastPointer` should be a `useRef`, since nothing renders from it. (c) `setDrag` publishes `{ids,dx,dy}` through a ref + rAF with only the dragged shells subscribing.
- **Hard clause** — **probe.** A render-count assertion in the existing `render.test.tsx` harness: dispatching a `presence.cursor` message must re-render **zero** `ElementShell`s.

#### SC6 · `GET /boards/:id/children` is a per-container `Descendants` N+1, unpaginated and unprojected — and it fires on every reconnect 🔴
- **Evidence** — `board_service.go:78` reads direct children, `:83-89` collects container ids, then `:90-96` loops `s.elements.Descendants(ctx, cid, false)` — one multi-level BFS per container. `Descendants` takes no limit; `find` (`elements.go:495-513`) never calls `SetProjection`; the route has no page parameter. Callers: `boardStore.ts:125-129 openBoard` and `:146-150 refreshBoard`, and `refreshBoard` is invoked from `socket.ts:57` on **every reconnect** and `boardStore.ts:240` on every server-rejected transaction.
- **Failure** — A board with 30 columns issues 31+ round trips for one page load, and every column holding a TASK_LIST adds a level — while `Descendants` already batches a level into one `$in`, so the batching primitive is inside the function being called in a loop. The payload half is worse: every element's full content ships, including uncapped `content.doc` and `content.strokes` (`BoardCanvas.tsx:172-178` stores every raw pointer sample with no simplification), so a board of 600 rich notes returns every document body in one unpaginated response — on load, on reconnect, and after any rejected write. The reconnect case is precisely when the network is bad.
- **Mechanism** — (a) One call: `Descendants(ctx, containerIDs []string)` — the loop already seeds from a frontier slice, so a multi-root seed changes nothing about its shape. (b) A `fields=summary` projection for the canvas payload omitting `content.doc` and `content.strokes`, with the body fetched lazily on edit or on entering the viewport — the same read/write asymmetry the digest already draws, composing with SC11. (c) A hard `Descendants` limit with a truncation flag, which DA23 also wants on the delete/export side.
- **Hard clause** — **handler.**

#### SC7 · `MergePatch` is read-modify-`ReplaceOne`, so nudging a card rewrites its whole document — and concurrent patches lose each other wholesale 🔴 *(contradicts `hub.go`'s stated concurrency model)*
- **Evidence** — `repository/mongo/elements.go:90-117`: `FindOne` decodes the entire document into `bson.M`, `mergeValue` applies the patch in Go, then `ReplaceOne(ctx, {_id: id}, raw)` — no `$set`, no version predicate, no `FindOneAndUpdate`. This is the only element write path.
- **Failure** — Two costs. (1) Write amplification: a moveOp carrying two floats reads and rewrites the full document including an arbitrarily large `content.doc` and `content.strokes`; the oplog carries the full replacement too, so replication traffic scales with document size rather than change size. (2) Correctness: `realtime/hub.go:1-5` states the design as "concurrency is element-granular … two users on different cards merge trivially; the same card resolves server-authoritatively (last writer wins)." **`ReplaceOne` with no predicate makes it document-granular.** If A moves a card while B patches its labels, whichever write lands second was built from a snapshot taken before the other read and silently discards the other change entirely. The stated merge semantics hold inside the merge-patch function and are thrown away by the write that follows it. On a shared production board this reads as "my label came off by itself".
- **Mechanism** — Translate the merge-patch into `$set`/`$unset` — flatten the patch to dotted paths, nulls becoming `$unset` — and issue one `FindOneAndUpdate` with `ReturnDocument: After`. One round trip instead of two, writes only changed leaves, and makes the concurrent case genuinely field-granular, which is what the hub's header claims. The `mergeValue` recursion already walks the patch tree; it becomes a path accumulator. Keep the `patchableRoots` whitelist.
- **Hard clause** — **probe.** A repository contract test where two interleaved patches to disjoint fields must both survive.

#### SC8 · The run journal counts its own collection before every insert — O(n²) per run, and a lost race silently punches the gap the index exists to forbid 🔴
- **Evidence** — `repository/mongo/agent.go:132-148 Append`: a 5-attempt loop that each time does `CountDocuments({runId})`, sets `Sequence = n+1`, `InsertOne`, retries on duplicate key, and after 5 failures returns `ErrConflict`. `indexes.go:49-53` declares the unique `{runId, sequence}` index with the comment "The journal is ordered and gap-free: this uniqueness is what makes a client's `events since N` cursor meaningful." `emit` swallows the failure with a `Warn`.
- **Failure** — Two round trips per event and a count scanning a growing range: a run emitting E events does E counts averaging E/2 entries each, so journal cost is quadratic in run length. That is the small half. The large half is that count-then-insert is check-then-act and the code knows it (hence the retry). Concurrent emitters exist — the run goroutine emits per step and per tool outcome while the steer path and the state machine emit on the same run. When five attempts lose, **the event is dropped and only a warning is logged** — so the sequence is gap-free by index and the journal is missing a row, and the frontend's resumable cursor (`sequence > since`) has no way to notice. The live run view stops updating and the user sees a run that appears to have stalled. It appears only under load, which is when someone is most likely to be watching. **This must be fixed before a single new event type is added**, and every wave in both documents adds one.
- **Mechanism** — Sequence must not be derived from a count. Either `$inc` an `eventSeq` counter on the run document inside the CAS the state machine already performs — atomic, one round trip, no retry loop, no scan — or drop the dense sequence and cursor on `(at, _id)`. The first preserves the client contract exactly. Either way a failed append must **escalate rather than warn**: an event the journal could not record is a hole in the only audit surface the agent has.
- **Hard clause** — **handler.**

#### SC9 · A slow consumer on a busy board can panic the process — `Send` can write to a channel a concurrent broadcast just closed 🔴
- **Evidence** — `realtime/client.go:93-99` `func (c *Client) Send(msg []byte) { select { case c.send <- msg: default: c.close() } }`; `:101-109 close` is `once.Do` ending in `close(c.send)`. `hub.go:138-155 broadcast` snapshots targets under `RLock`, **releases the lock at `:151`**, then calls `c.Send(env)` outside it; `BroadcastTransaction` (`:117-135`) does the same. Buffer is 64 (`client.go:52`). `readPump` (`:111-132`) has `defer c.close()` and **no recover**.
- **Failure** — `sync.Once` prevents a double close; it does not prevent a send on an already-closed channel, and a send case in a `select` on a closed channel **panics** rather than falling to `default`. The window is opened by exactly the load this corner is about: two goroutines broadcast concurrently (a committed transaction from the HTTP handler and a cursor tick from another client's readPump), both snapshot the same target list before either sends, the first fills client C's 64-slot buffer and closes it, the second sends into the closed channel. Cursor traffic is 20 Hz per peer and a 240-op agent transaction is a single large frame, so a room with several collaborators and one client on a bad link is the natural setting. When the losing goroutine is a `readPump` — no recover anywhere on that stack — **the panic takes down the whole server process**, dropping every board room. Independently the drop policy is a thrash loop: a disconnected client reconnects and triggers `refreshBoard()`, which is SC6's unpaginated three-call fetch — so the response to a congested client is the largest payload in the API.
- **Mechanism** — Make the send path unable to touch a closed channel: guard send/close with a mutex and a `closed` bool, or (cleaner) never close `c.send` at all — signal shutdown with a separate `done chan struct{}` that `writePump` selects on. Then soften the drop policy: on overflow discard only coalescable frames (a peer's `presence.cursor`, where the newest supersedes every queued one) and disconnect only when a durable frame would be lost. With SC16's server-side coalescing the common cause of the drop disappears entirely.
- **Hard clause** — **handler.**

#### SC10 · Three shipped queries have no usable index, and the board's core read sorts in memory 🔴
- **Evidence** — Query shapes in `elements.go` against `indexes.go`. (a) `Children` (`:47-59`) matches `{location.parentId, deletedAt[, section][, type]}` and sorts `{location.index: 1}`; the index is `{location.parentId, deletedAt}` — **the sort key is absent**, so every board read does a blocking in-memory SORT. (b) `Trashed` (`:194-203`) matches `{deletedAt != null, $or:[{deletedBy: sub},{createdBy: sub}]}`; the only trash index is `{deletedBy, deletedAt}` and **there is no index on `createdBy` anywhere in the file**, so that `$or` branch forces a full collection scan on every Trash open. (c) `PurgeExpired` (`:336-340`) matches `{deletedAt: {$ne:null, $lt:t}}`; `deletedAt` is never an index prefix, so the maintenance sweep is a full collection scan on its ticker. (d) `BoardsOwnedBy` `$or`s an unindexed `acl.editors` and sorts an unindexed `updatedAt`.
- **Failure** — None of these is covered by DA8, which is about Search's `$regex` and its ownership predicate — and DA8 does not observe that `createdBy`, the field its own fix leans on, has no index at all. (a) bites first and hardest: `Children` is the hottest query in the system, called by `BoardService.Children`, by `admitChildren` on every scope compile, and by `rehomeConnectors`. **An unindexed sort is served from memory and Mongo aborts a sort exceeding its memory limit**, so on a board whose documents carry large `content.doc`/`content.strokes` this does not degrade gracefully — it errors, and the error surfaces as a board that will not open. (c) means the purge's cost grows with total database size forever rather than with the trash.
- **Mechanism** — Four additive indexes: extend `{location.parentId, deletedAt}` to include `location.index` so `Children` is covered for match+sort; add `{createdBy, deletedAt:-1}`; add sparse `{deletedAt}`; add `{acl.editors, type}` and `{acl.ownerId, updatedAt:-1}`.
- **Hard clause** — **probe.** An `explain()` assertion per query shape asserting IXSCAN and **no SORT stage** — `repository/repotest` already provides the Mongo-backed harness.

#### SC11 · Every card mounts a full ProseMirror editor whether or not it is being edited, and no virtualization primitive exists anywhere
- **Evidence** — `NoteCard.tsx:57-72` calls `useEditor` **unconditionally** at the top of the component with seven extensions and `editable:false`; `ElementView.tsx:26-27` renders NoteCard for every CARD and `:32-36` a second one for every CLONE. `BoardCanvas.tsx:304-312` maps every element in `canvasElements` — no viewport filter, no windowing. `grep -rn "react-window|react-virtual|virtuoso|IntersectionObserver|content-visibility" frontend/src` → **nothing at all**.
- **Failure** — This is the concrete content of "no windowing, only `memo()`", and it explains why memo could never have sufficed: the cost is not re-render cost, it is **mount** cost that exists whether or not anything re-renders. A production board with 600 notes instantiates 600 EditorViews, 600 parsed documents and 600 plugin stacks at open, all live and all subscribed, and `NoteCard.tsx:85-91` then runs a `JSON.stringify` comparison per editor on every content change. The grep result is the actionable part: `content-visibility: auto` with `contain-intrinsic-size` is a two-line CSS change that lets the browser skip layout and paint for offscreen cards with no JS at all, and it is not being used.
- **Mechanism** — Three tiers, cheapest first. (a) `content-visibility: auto` + `contain-intrinsic-size` on the shell — immediate paint relief, zero risk. (b) Lazy editor: render `content.textPreview` as static styled markup and instantiate `useEditor` only when `editingId === shellId` — the store already tracks exactly that and the read path already has the preview string. That removes 600 editors and leaves 600 divs. (c) Only then true windowing, filtered by the viewport rect the view store already maintains. Do (a) and (b) first — they are most of the win, and (c) interacts with marquee hit-testing, `fitAll` and `LineLayer`'s sizes map, all of which currently assume every element is mounted.
- **Hard clause** — **handler** (client-side); (b) must not lose an in-flight edit on unmount.

## T2 — collaborator

#### SC12 · A transaction applies its ops one at a time with two round trips each and no Mongo session
- **Evidence** — `transaction_service.go:126-134` loops `applyOp` and returns on the first error, with no rollback and no `mongo.Session`/`WithTransaction` anywhere in the file. Each update/move goes through `MergePatch` = FindOne + ReplaceOne (SC7), so an N-op transaction is 2N round trips. The agent's ceiling is `MaxOps = MaxActions * 4 = 240`; the human path has no op cap at all (DA23).
- **Failure** — The pre-validation loop genuinely prevents the **authorization** failure its comment is about; it cannot prevent a write failure at op 150 of 240 (see FR5 for the recovery consequence). Combined with SC7's two-round-trip patch, a 240-op agent commit is ~480 serial Mongo round trips plus DA23's containment walks plus SC13's move captures, inside one HTTP request.
- **Mechanism** — Wrap the apply loop in `mongo.Session.WithTransaction` (which makes the deployment's replica-set posture a decision rather than an accident) and combine with DA21's ordering fix — journal row first in a `created` state, then apply, then mark committed — since both are one edit to the same method. Batch with `BulkWrite` once SC7 turns each op into a single `$set`.
- **Hard clause** — **handler**, shared with FR5 and DA21.

#### SC13 · `captureMoves` issues an un-memoized `Get` plus an ancestor walk per move op, right beside a cache built for exactly that
- **Evidence** — `transaction_service.go:830-856`: for every move-or-update op carrying a `location.parentId`, `s.elements.Get(ctx, op.ElementID)` then `s.drawnOn(ctx, el)` — a per-element walk. Ten lines earlier, `:101` builds `boardCache := map[string]bool{}` memoizing `"elementID|boardID"` for the validation pass, and `captureMoves` uses neither it nor anything like it.
- **Failure** — DA23 counts the containment walk in `verifyOpScope`/`verifyDelegation`/`assertNoCycle` and prescribes one batched `ancestryOf(ids)` per transaction. `captureMoves` is a **fourth** independent walk over the same elements in the same request and DA23 does not name it — so a fix following DA23's mechanism literally leaves a quarter of the cost in place. On the canonical agent plan (a filing run where every action is a move) this is the dominant term, and a human's multi-select drag of forty cards is forty Gets plus forty walks for information the validation pass already loaded.
- **Mechanism** — Fold it into DA23's batch: one `ancestryOf(allOpElementIDs)` at the top of `ApplyWithMeta` populates a single map that `verifyOpScope`, `verifyDelegation`, `assertNoCycle` and `captureMoves` all read. `rehomeConnectors` is the model to copy — its comment already articulates the principle ("One read per CANVAS, not per moved element") and it is the only one of the five that follows it.
- **Hard clause** — **handler**, shared with DA23.

#### SC14 · The 16 MB BSON ceiling sits between a 64 MB body limit and an unbounded op count, and the journal is written after the ops land
- **Evidence** — `transaction_service.go:126-134` applies every op, `:159` then inserts a `Transaction` carrying every op's `Changes` **and** `UndoChanges`. `boardStore.ts:353-360` builds `undoChanges` via `snapshotForUndo` (`:65-82`), which for a content patch copies the prior value of every touched key — so a note save carries the new doc and the old doc, two full document bodies, in one op. DA23 establishes the only human-path bound is `BodyLimit("64M")`.
- **Failure** — Mongo's hard per-document limit is 16 MB. A transaction the router accepts at up to 64 MB can therefore be applied in full and then fail to journal — landing in exactly FR5's partial-commit hole, but **deterministically** rather than by luck, and reachable without any adversary: a few large sketches or documents in one multi-select drag will do it. Because the insert precedes the broadcast, collaborators never receive the change and their boards silently diverge until someone reloads. DA21 and DA23 each touch one edge and neither names the ceiling or the 2× amplification that reaches it twice as fast — which is also the steady-state driver behind DA15: the journal grows at roughly twice the rate of the content it describes, forever.
- **Mechanism** — Bound where the bound is knowable: reject any transaction whose serialized ops exceed a safe fraction of 16 MB (8 MB) at the handler, naming the size — a refusal before any write is categorically better than a partial commit. For the amplification, store `UndoChanges` for large content **by reference**: the journal already holds the transaction id, so a prior-value blob can live once in the blob store rather than inline in every op. And align the body limit with the real ceiling rather than leaving a 4× gap where the router says yes and the database says no.
- **Hard clause** — **handler.**

#### SC15 · Runs spawn as unbounded goroutines and the spend cap is checked once, against a sum that cannot see in-flight runs *(deepens PL6)*
- **Evidence** — `service.go:237` `go s.execute(context.Background(), …)` — no semaphore, no queue, no worker pool anywhere in the package. The only admission gate is one-run-per-**board** (and MP5 shows it is keyed on the root). `checkDailyCap` is called once at `:170` and sums `r.Usage.CostUSD` over `ListByBoard(tenant, "", 500)` — but `run.Usage.Add(usage)` at `:354` is in-memory and only reaches the database when `transition` writes the run at a terminal state.
- **Failure** — PL6 identifies that the cap is admission-time-only and that one run can overshoot. The concurrency mechanism it does not name is what makes that unbounded: because in-flight spend is invisible to `spentToday`, K runs started within the same window all read the same stale total and all pass. A user with fifteen boards can hold fifteen concurrent runs, each with an 8-minute budget and a $0.35 ceiling, all admitted against a sum of $0.00 — **the daily cap bypassed by a factor of K** with nothing unusual happening. Every one of those goroutines holds a full conversation in memory (SC1 measured 302k cumulative input tokens over 24 steps, plus decoded attachment bytes), and nothing bounds the process. PL14's rate limiter is per-IP and in-memory, so it does not bind here either.
- **Mechanism** — A counting semaphore around `execute` sized from config, with runs beyond it **queued** rather than rejected — that is S2's queue, and MP5's key fix is a prerequisite for the lock but not for the semaphore, which can ship first and alone. Then make in-flight spend visible: persist `run.Usage` on the lease renewal FR1 introduces (a write already going to the database), so `spentToday` sums live runs too, and move the aggregation to a `$group` as PL6 prescribes.
- **Hard clause** — **handler.**

#### SC16 · Cursor fan-out is O(N²) with no coalescing, and the response to congestion is the largest payload in the API
- **Evidence** — `realtime/client.go:136-150`: each `presence.cursor` message calls `hub.broadcast`, and `hub.go:138-155` takes an RWMutex read lock, allocates a target slice, and marshals a fresh envelope via two `json.Marshal` calls **per event**. The client throttles to 20 Hz per peer. Buffer 64; overflow disconnects; reconnect triggers SC6's three unpaginated calls.
- **Failure** — N clients × 20 Hz × (N−1) recipients: 6 collaborators produce 600 messages/second, 10 produce 1,800, each with a lock acquisition, a slice allocation and two marshals on the server, and each landing on a client where SC5 turns it into a full board re-render. Nothing coalesces: if a client's queue already holds three stale positions for peer X, all four are delivered. For the actual use case — a director and an editor on one production board — this is the difference between the canvas feeling live and feeling like it is stuttering.
- **Mechanism** — Coalesce at the source: hold the latest cursor per client and flush the room's aggregate on a server-side ~10 Hz ticker as ONE `presence.cursors` frame containing every peer's position — O(N²) messages become O(N) frames and one marshal per tick. That single change also removes the dominant cause of buffer overflow, so SC9's drop policy stops firing in normal operation. On the client, apply cursor frames to a store nothing on the canvas subscribes to (SC5), so the rate stops mattering.
- **Hard clause** — none; composition over existing frames.

#### SC17 · The line layer mounts two 200,000 × 200,000 pixel SVG surfaces and recomputes every line's geometry on every drag frame
- **Evidence** — `LineLayer.tsx:15` `const EXTENT = 100_000;` and `:314-319` renders `<svg width={EXTENT*2} height={EXTENT*2}>` — 200,000px square — with a **second identical surface** at `:339-344` whenever any line is selected. `:43` subscribes to `useBoard()` with no selector; `:44-46` to sizes and drag. `:229-310` maps **every** line and recomputes `resolveEnd`/trim/bezier control points for all of them on each render, regardless of whether the dragged element is an endpoint of any.
- **Failure** — Two independent costs. The surface: a 200,000² SVG with `overflow: visible` gives the compositor a layer whose declared bounds are four orders of magnitude past the viewport, doubled on selection. The recompute: `drag` changes identity on every pointermove, so a 200-line board recomputes 200 beziers plus 400 trims (each with `Math.hypot` and divisions) at pointer rate while dragging one card that may touch none of them. Both are invisible on a demo board with four lines and both are on the critical path for a workflow diagram — which is exactly what the newest capability wave produces.
- **Mechanism** — (a) Size the SVG to the union of line bounding boxes plus a margin, recomputed only when lines change — the geometry is already computed in the same pass. (b) Precompute a `Map<elementId, lineId[]>` adjacency once per line-set change and, during a drag, recompute only lines incident to `drag.ids`, memoizing the rest by `(line, sizes[from], sizes[to])`. (c) Selection handles render into the same SVG under a higher-order `<g>`, or into a small overlay sized to the handles.
- **Hard clause** — none.

#### SC18 · Opening a board performs O(n²) object copies through `reportSize`, each one re-rendering the line layer
- **Evidence** — `viewStore.ts:67-72 reportSize` does `set(s => ({ sizes: {...s.sizes, [id]: {w,h}} }))` — a full spread per call. `ElementShell.tsx:56-64`: every shell calls it once on mount and then on every ResizeObserver callback. No batching, no rAF coalescing. `LineLayer.tsx:44` subscribes to `sizes`.
- **Failure** — Mounting a 1,000-element board issues 1,000 sequential `set` calls whose spreads copy 0, 1, 2, … 999 keys — about **500,000 key copies** and 1,000 discarded intermediate objects, at exactly the moment the user is waiting for the board to paint. Each also produces a new `sizes` identity, so `LineLayer` re-renders 1,000 times during board open, recomputing every line's geometry each time (SC17). GSAP's per-shell mount animation runs concurrently. This is the largest avoidable cost in time-to-interactive on a large board and it is entirely a batching problem.
- **Mechanism** — Buffer size reports in a module-level `Map` and flush once per animation frame with a single `set` — one object construction and one subscriber notification per frame instead of per element. `reportSize` already has the right sub-pixel early-out; it just needs the writes coalesced.
- **Hard clause** — **probe.** A store-write counter in the render harness: mounting N shells must produce O(frames), not O(N), writes to `sizes`.

#### SC19 · `NotifyUser` scans every room and every client in the process to find one person's connections
- **Evidence** — `realtime/hub.go:66-81` holds `h.mu.RLock()` and iterates `for _, r := range h.rooms { for c := range r.clients { if c.Principal.Sub == sub …` — there is no index from subject to clients.
- **Failure** — O(total connections in the process) per notification, **under a lock every broadcast and every registration also needs** — so notification volume couples directly to cursor-broadcast latency across unrelated boards. Notifications are produced per assignment and per comment, and an agent plan that assigns twenty tasks fires twenty of these in a burst at commit time, each sweeping the whole process's client set while the same commit's `BroadcastTransaction` contends for the same lock. It is small today, and it is the kind of small that only reveals itself at the load where everything else is also failing.
- **Mechanism** — Maintain `bySub map[string]map[*Client]struct{}` alongside `rooms`, updated in `Register`/`Unregister` — the two places that already hold the write lock. `NotifyUser` becomes a map lookup. Ten lines, and it removes the only whole-process scan on the realtime hot path.
- **Hard clause** — none.

#### SC20 · The scope's fair-share divisor permanently strands budget — a wider workspace makes the agent see LESS, not differently
- **Evidence** — `digest.go:417-422`: `share := maxPerContainer; if fair := budget / len(frontier); fair < share { share = fair }; if share < 1 { share = 1 }` — computed once per depth level, applied uniformly, **with no redistribution pass** over containers that came in under their allowance. Measured: at 2,029 elements `CompileScope` admitted **296 addressable against a 400 budget — 104 slots never spent** — and the rendered digest was 4,242 tokens, *smaller* than the 5,636 produced by a 397-element workspace.
- **Failure** — The mechanism under SC3, worth naming separately because it is a self-contained arithmetic bug with a self-contained fix. The divisor is the frontier's width, so one wide level permanently shrinks every container's allowance even when the global budget has room; a container holding two cards spends 2 of its share of 13 and the other 11 evaporate. It also **inverts** the intended relationship between workspace size and context: breadth-first keeps the shallow structure complete, and the leaves that survive get fewer as the board grows, so the agent's picture of a large production workspace is strictly poorer than its picture of a small one, in tokens as well as in items. CG12 assumes the 400 budget is spent and proposes aiming it; there is nothing to aim.
- **Mechanism** — Two passes per level. Pass one spends `share` and records `unspent = share - taken` per container plus the set that hit its cap; pass two redistributes `sum(unspent)` over the capped set, repeating until the level's budget is exhausted or no container wants more. Pure arithmetic, no new reads, and it composes with CG12 (who is served first) and IN17 (at what fidelity) without conflicting with either.
- **Hard clause** — **probe.** `len(scope.Elements)` must equal `min(maxScopeElements, workspace size)` for any workspace shape.

#### SC21 · The tool catalogue forks the cache prefix by autonomy mode and by label availability, splitting the one working cache three ways
- **Evidence** — `planner.go:400` `tools := ToolCatalogue(task.Autonomy == AutonomyPreview, pl.labels != nil)`. Tools serialize **before** the System block, and the sole cache breakpoint sits on System, so the cached prefix is tools ++ system. Measured: 49 tools / 23,548 bytes (preview), 45 / 21,612 (auto), 47 / 22,702 (no labels) — three distinct prefixes of roughly 5.4k–5.9k tokens each. `anthropic.go:71-73` sets `Strict` on a forced tool, forking it a fourth way.
- **Failure** — Ephemeral cache entries have a 5-minute TTL, so each variant needs its own traffic to stay warm, and a user who alternates Preview and Do-it — which the composer actively invites — misses the cache on every switch, paying full input price for ~5.7k tokens plus the cache-write premium SC2 shows is not even recorded. For a single-user deployment, which this is, the variant-specific traffic is thin enough that the cache may rarely be warm at all.
- **Mechanism** — Serve **one** catalogue and express the variation in the system text, which is already cached in the same block: always emit all 49 schemas and state the withholding as a rule ("deletion is not available on this run") — the handler layer already enforces it independently, so schema-level withholding is belt-and-braces rather than the security boundary. If a capability must be genuinely absent from the schema, place the varying tools **after** a cache breakpoint so the shared 45-tool core stays cached. The invariant SC1 needs: **the cacheable prefix must not vary with anything that varies per run.**
- **Hard clause** — **schema.**

## T3 — frontier

#### SC22 · There is not one benchmark in the codebase and the largest eval fixture is twelve elements — nothing in the corpus can observe any finding in this corner
- **Evidence** — `grep -rn "func Benchmark" backend/` → **zero results across the whole repository** (re-verified). The corpus's largest fixtures are loops of 10 and 12 elements; one assertion reads "2 columns + 6 cards, all addressable". Against that: `maxScopeElements` 400, `Budget.MaxActions` 60, `Delegation.MaxOps` 240 — and no test exercises a workspace within an order of magnitude of any of them.
- **Mechanism** — M5 argues the corpus never leaves flatland; MP11 that it never leaves single-player; CG4 that it never runs a probe twice. **This is the fourth axis and the one this corner lives on: it never leaves the small.** Every finding here — the scope collapsing to container names at 2% coverage, the header misreporting 9,331 as 230, 232 round trips per compile, 104 stranded budget slots, a digest costing more than the run's own cost cap — is invisible to a 12-element fixture, and each would have been caught by a single fixture at the ceiling. So: a parametric `buildWorkspace(boards, cols, cards)` (about twenty lines against `repository/memory`) at ≈400, ≈2,000 and ≈9,000, asserting what must be true of a big board — addressable count equals the budget, the header's total matches reality, no container renders zero children while budget remains, `CompileScope` issues ≤ 6 reads, digest tokens stay under a stated ceiling. Then real `func Benchmark`s for `CompileScope`, `ApplyWithMeta` at 240 ops and `BoardService.Children` at 1,000 elements, **gated in CI on query count rather than wall time** so they are stable. DA23's own note that "the probe is a query-count benchmark, which the suite does not currently know how to assert" is not a gap in one test but a missing category, and the tooling is a fifteen-line counting wrapper. Ship first: none of the other fixes in this corner can be shown to work without it.

#### SC23 · Unbounded growth on both sides of the element document — sketch strokes stored raw, `Descendants` uncapped, the read path unprojected
- **Evidence** — Write side: `BoardCanvas.tsx:146-149` `setDrawStroke([...drawStroke, [pt.x, pt.y]])` on every pointermove — an O(n²) array copy **and** a raw sample per event — and `:172-178` persists `content.strokes` with no simplification, decimation or point cap. `textPreview` is capped at 500 chars; `content.doc` has no cap anywhere. Read side: `Descendants` takes no limit and `find` never projects.
- **Mechanism** — A 30-second freehand scribble stores several thousand raw coordinate pairs in one element, and that element then rides in full through `BoardService.Children` on every board open (SC6), through `Descendants` in Duplicate/Export/DeleteAccount, through `MergePatch`'s read-modify-replace on every position nudge (SC7), and through `Transaction.Ops` twice per edit (SC14).
