One element a user creates in half a minute becomes a permanent multiplier on five hot paths. So: at capture, run Ramer–Douglas–Peucker (or distance-thresholded decimation) before persisting and cap total points per SKETCH — visually identical, an order of magnitude smaller, and the standard treatment; accumulate the in-progress stroke in a ref mirrored to state at rAF rate, removing the O(n²) copy. At retrieval, SC6's summary projection plus DA23's limit-and-truncation-flag. Three independent changes, each paying on its own.

#### SC24 · Undo payload doubling: every edit journals and broadcasts the prior value alongside the new one, forever
- **Evidence** — `boardStore.ts:65-82 snapshotForUndo` copies the current value of every key a patch touches; `:353-360` attaches it to every op. `plan.go:516-525` does the same server-side for label sets, deliberately and correctly. `transaction_service.go:149-158` stores both `Changes` and `UndoChanges` in `Transaction.Ops`, `:159` journals it, `:162` **broadcasts the entire transaction object to every client in the room**, and DA15 establishes the journal is never deleted.
- **Mechanism** — For a note save, `undoChanges.content.doc` is a complete copy of the previous body: the transaction carries the document twice, the journal keeps both copies forever, and every collaborator receives both over the socket. The undo semantics require the prior value — that is not the defect. The defect is that the **transport, the broadcast and the retention all inherit a payload sized for local undo.** It is the steady-state driver behind DA15's retention problem, a direct contributor to SC9's buffer overflow (the largest frames on the wire are the ones carrying two document bodies), and the reason SC14's 16 MB ceiling arrives at half the op count one would expect. So: split the wire format from the storage format. The broadcast envelope carries `Changes` only — a peer applying a remote transaction never reads `UndoChanges` (`boardStore.ts:180-226 applyOps` touches only `op.changes`), so it is pure waste on every frame today. For storage, keep `UndoChanges` inline for small values and spill large bodies to the blob store by reference, which also gives DA15's retention sweep something cheap to delete. The client's local undo stack is unaffected — it already holds the ops it built in memory.

---

# Corner IL — Instrumentation and the learning loop

*Method: all three prior documents read in full, then the run and event schema,
the state machine, the eval harness and corpus, the frontend agent store, and
the repository indexes. Confirmed baseline: zero analytics or telemetry
libraries in either tier — no prometheus, otel, expvar or statsd in
`backend/go.mod`; `posthog|amplitude|mixpanel|segment|sentry|gtag|plausible`
across `frontend/src` returns nothing but a false positive in
`lib/iconCatalog.ts`.*

*The organising read, and it corrects the premise this territory was commissioned
under: **"nobody collects the data" is half wrong.** The product writes a rich,
ordered, timestamped, durable event stream and a run document that between them
already contain most of what a learning loop needs — and gave them exactly one
reader (a browser tab) and one query shape (by run id). Three things are
genuinely absent and cheap. Three more are being **actively destroyed**, and are
getting worse every hour the product is used.*

## T1 — parity and live wrongs

#### IL1 · The proposed plan is overwritten by the applied plan at commit — the correction diff is destroyed, not merely unread 🔴 *(corrects LP1)*
- **Evidence** — `service.go:574` `run.Plan = effective` inside `commit()`. `contracts.go:276-281` — the `Adjustment` struct carries **only** `json` tags, no `bson`, so it has no persistent home anywhere in the schema. `agentStore.ts:215` holds adjustments in memory and `:226` clears them on a successful apply.
- **Failure** — LP1 and LP4 both propose diffing applied-vs-proposed at commit. That works forward and **cannot be backfilled**: every run already in the database that was applied with adjustments has lost the proposal it was adjusted from. The evidence of every correction this user has ever made is gone, and each further day of use permanently destroys the only supervision signal the product can legitimately learn from. There is a second, live consequence: `Run.RevertedElementIDs` and the outcome card's per-row Undo index into a plan that is no longer the plan the person reviewed — the row they dropped is simply absent from their own audit trail.
- **Mechanism** — Split the field rather than add a pipeline. `Run.ProposedPlan` written once at the PROPOSED transition (immutable) beside the existing `Run.Plan` (effective); both are already bson-marshalled subdocuments, so this is one field and no new collection. Inside `commit()`, where both halves are in hand, write `Run.Corrections []Correction{kind, seq, actionKind, cascade bool, at}` — the only place in the system where the diff is computable without a join. Retention rides `PurgeTenant` unchanged.
- **Hard clause** — **schema.** Every hour this is not shipped is an hour of unrecoverable labels; it outranks LP1 itself.

#### IL2 · Cascade-dropped actions are indistinguishable from human-dropped ones, so the first correction miner learns a 10× lie 🔴 *(corrects LP1)*
- **Evidence** — `verify.go:376-377` `case AdjustDrop: dropped[adj.Seq] = true`; `:404-417` the parent-death cascade writes into the **same** `dropped` map; the effective plan simply omits both. The client mirror does the same (`agentStore.ts:533-548`).
- **Failure** — LP1's Detect stage is "each Adjustment is a correction record". One click dropping a column that holds nine cards produces **ten** missing actions. A naive diff writes ten correction records, nine of which the human never made, and LP1's Generalize stage ("≥2 same-shaped corrections become a candidate rule") then infers a durable rule from a single click. The most common correction shape in the product carries the largest amplification error, and the bug is invisible because the two kinds of drop are the same boolean.
- **Mechanism** — `ApplyAdjustments` returns drop **provenance** alongside the plan: `explicit` vs `cascade(parentSeq)`. Only explicit drops become correction records; a cascade becomes one record with a child count. The same change makes the review list honest — today dropping a container silently removes its nine children with no row saying so, which is also why a reviewer's drop is a bigger act than they know (LP3).
- **Hard clause** — **compiler.**

#### IL4 · Refine silently deletes the person's pending row edits and never tells the model about them 🔴
- **Evidence** — `agentStore.ts:259` sets `adjustments: []` on a successful refine; `api/client.ts:159 agentRefine(id, note)` sends only the note; `service.go:628-675` appends to `Task.Refinements` and re-plans from prose.
- **Failure** — Two failures on one line. **Product:** drop three rows, then type "also make it four columns", and the three drops vanish — the second plan re-proposes exactly what you just removed, which reads as the agent ignoring you and is the most annoying possible outcome of the refine feature. **Learning:** refinement is the only correction channel that reaches the model, and it is fed free prose while the typed signal sitting beside it in the same store is deleted. CG13 diagnoses refinement re-authoring from a lossy prose summary; this is the same disease one layer up, where the non-lossy data is already in hand.
- **Mechanism** — Send the pending adjustments with the refine note. Render them into the replay as **facts the model must obey** — "they removed: create_column 'Ideas'; do not propose it again" — rather than as prose to be re-derived, and record them as corrections with `outcome: refined`. CG13's seq re-keying problem does not arise, because the drops are consumed as instructions rather than carried forward as state.
- **Hard clause** — **handler.**

#### IL5 · Every terminal state writes the same `CompletedAt`, so "regret within N minutes" — LP4's headline metric — is not computable *(corrects LP4)*
- **Evidence** — `contracts.go:392-394`: the Run carries only CreatedAt/UpdatedAt/CompletedAt. `service.go:896-925 transition` sets `CompletedAt = UpdatedAt` for **every** terminal state, and `contracts.go:91-92` makes COMPLETED→REVERTED legal — so a reverted run's `CompletedAt` is the revert time and the apply time is gone. Partial revert is worse: `revert.go:105-113` appends to `RevertedElementIDs` and stamps only `UpdatedAt`, so N per-action reverts spread over an hour collapse to one timestamp.
- **Failure** — LP4 lists `revertedWithin` among four questions nothing can currently answer, and it will **still** be unanswerable after LP4's RunOutcome record lands, because both ends of the interval are stored in the same field and the second write erases the first. The same absence kills three more: time-to-decide (PROPOSED→APPLYING), model latency (CREATED→PROPOSED), and review duration — all of which LP3's approval-fatigue argument depends on being measurable.
- **Mechanism** — Per-state stamps written in the one place state is written: `Run.StateAt map[RunState]time.Time` set in `transition`, plus a `RevertedAt` on each entry appended to `RevertedElementIDs`. Everything downstream becomes arithmetic over fields, with no new collection and no new event.
- **Hard clause** — **schema.**

#### IL6 · The journal already holds every timing the loop needs and is indexed so that nothing can ask for it
- **Evidence** — `service.go:962-990 emit` stamps `At` on every event; `:925` emits `EvRunState {from,to,reason}` for every transition; `:389` `EvPlanReady` carries the proposed action count and `:576` `EvOpsCommitted` the applied count. `repository/mongo/indexes.go:49-53` — the `agent_events` collection has **exactly one** index, unique `(runId, sequence)`. `store.go:31-38 EventStore` exposes Append, `List(runID, since, limit)` and DeleteByTenant; there is no query by time, tenant, board or type, **and no method signature that could carry one**.
- **Failure** — This is the finding that reframes the territory. The timing data is not missing; it is unreadable. Every aggregate over the journal is a full collection scan, and the port the agent package depends on cannot express one — so PL15's analytics endpoint would be written against a repository interface with no aggregate method. The cheapest instrumentation in the whole product is two compound indexes and one port method, not a new event pipeline.
- **Mechanism** — Denormalise `tenantSub` and `rootBoardId` onto the Event (both are in hand at every emit call site), add `(at, type)` and `(runId, type)` indexes, and add `EventStore.Aggregate(ctx, tenant, since, types)` returning grouped counts via a `$group`. LP4's four questions then become queries against data that already exists, and PL15's endpoint is a projection rather than a data-collection project.
- **Hard clause** — **schema.** Highest ratio of answered questions to lines of code in this corner.

#### IL7 · Every journal append runs a `CountDocuments` over the run's own events — the instrumentation carrier is O(n²) before anyone adds an event to it 🔴
- **Evidence** — See SC8 for the full mechanism; this corner records the consequence for the loop: `repository/mongo/agent.go:132-146` derives the next sequence by counting, with a 5-attempt retry, and `emit` swallows the terminal failure with a `Warn`.
- **Failure** — Every proposal across all four documents pushes in exactly one direction: more events. Streaming ghosts (IN12), presence (MP16), per-phase cost (CG8), correction records (IL1), acceptance counters (LP11), refusal counters (IL26), security counters (IL24). **This must be fixed before a single new event type is added**, or instrumentation makes the runtime worse in the exact proportion that it makes the agent better — and a lost race drops the event silently, so the audit surface acquires holes precisely under the load that produces the most interesting behaviour.
- **Mechanism** — SC8's atomic `$inc` allocation, plus one clause this corner insists on: **a failed append must escalate, not warn.** An event the journal could not record is a hole in the only record the loop has.
- **Hard clause** — **handler**, shared with SC8.

#### IL8 · `EvRunCreated` means three different things, so the first histogram anyone builds over the journal is wrong 🔴 *(corrects PL15)*
- **Evidence** — `service.go:230` emits `EvRunCreated` "run admitted"; `:659` emits `EvRunCreated` "revision requested" from `Refine`; `:697` emits `EvRunCreated` "steered mid-run" from `Steer`. The only discriminator is an untranslated English `Message`, and the latter two carry `{"note": …}` — raw user text — under a type name that says a run began.
- **Failure** — PL15 proposes a "refusal histogram from event types" and LP4 deterministic aggregates over terminal transitions. `count(type = run.created)` counts runs **plus** refinements **plus** steers, so run count, refine rate and steer rate are all wrong from the first query. Event vocabulary is the one thing that becomes expensive to change once consumers exist, and three consumers are already proposed (PL7 webhooks, PL17 MCP audit, PL15 analytics).
- **Mechanism** — `EvRefined` and `EvSteered` as their own EventTypes; `EvRunCreated` reserved for admission. Two constants, three call sites, **and it must land before any consumer is built.**
- **Hard clause** — **schema.**

#### IL9 · The provider records which model answered — in a field whose own comment says it exists to explain regressions, that nothing reads, and that races 🔴 *(absorbs the ledger half: no model, no price, on any run)*
- **Evidence** — `cognition/resilient.go:27-31`, verbatim: "lastUsed records which provider answered, so a quality regression can be explained later. Silent substitution is the thing to avoid: a run that quietly ran on a weaker model and produced a worse plan is impossible to diagnose after the fact." It is written at `:83` `f.lastUsed = p` **with no mutex**, and read only by `Model()`, whose only callers are CLI printing and price registration. `grep -rn "Model()" backend/internal/agent/*.go` returns nothing — **no Run records a model.** `cli/serve.go:137` builds ONE `NewFallback` for the whole process and every run executes in its own goroutine. CI runs `go test ./...` with **no `-race`**. Separately `provider.go:243-252 CostUSD` computes from the in-process price table at call time, and `RegisterPrice` can overwrite a rate at boot.
- **Failure** — The exact failure the comment forbids is the shipping behaviour. Not one run in the database can be attributed to a model, so no prompt change, model swap or fallback event can ever be evaluated against production outcomes — CG8's per-phase routing is unmeasurable in production by construction, and S1's provider fallback would silently degrade quality with no trace. The field is also a process-global mutable written concurrently: it races, and even if read it would answer for whichever run last finished. And the ledger half compounds it: a deployment that corrects a wrong rate or switches tier produces a run history whose dollar figures were computed against different, unrecorded rules — so `AgentDone.tsx:63` shows the user a price and never tells them what they bought.
- **Mechanism** — `cognition.Response` carries `Provider` and `Model` (the provider knows both at return); the planner folds them into Usage; `Run.Usage.Models []string` — an **ordered set**, because a fallback mid-run is precisely the case worth seeing — plus `Run.PricedAt Price`, the actual rates **copied** rather than referenced so the ledger is self-describing. Delete `lastUsed`. Add `-race` to the CI test step.
- **Hard clause** — **schema.**

#### IL10 · No run records the code that produced it, so no metric survives a deploy
- **Evidence** — `contracts.go:327-395`: the Run carries Task, State, Plan, Verdict, TransactionIDs, Steers, Usage and timestamps, and **nothing identifying the system prompt, the tool catalogue, the digest budgets or the build**. The prompt is Go string literals in `planner.go`; scope budgets are package constants; the git SHA is stamped nowhere in the tree.
- **Failure** — This is the difference between telemetry and a learning loop, and no prior document names it. Every proposal in all four documents changes a prompt, a budget, a tool set or a model. With no experiment identity on the run, an apply-rate that moves after a deploy cannot be attributed, a regression cannot be bisected, and the honest answer to "did that wave help?" is permanently "we cannot tell" — which is exactly how G2's rate came to be established by hand and recorded in prose. **Every other finding in this corner compounds only if this one exists.**
- **Mechanism** — One stamp, four hashes, all computable at boot: `Run.Build{gitSHA, promptHash, catalogueHash, budgetsHash}` — hash the assembled system prompt and the marshalled `ToolCatalogue` once at startup, hash the budget constants, take the SHA from a linker flag. No schema per experiment, no feature-flag system. Every aggregate then becomes groupable by build, and "the last 200 runs on build A vs build B" is one `$group`.
- **Hard clause** — **schema.**

#### IL11 · Retry — the purest "you got it wrong, do it again" — creates an unlinked run and mutates the intent so even matching cannot recover it 🔴
- **Evidence** — `service.go:736-762 Retry` composes `intent = prior.Task.Intent` plus `"\nthen: " + note` per refinement and calls `Create` with **no** `ContinuesRunID`, while `Continue` (`:776-800`) sets it. Retry also silently drops `Viewport`, which Continue carries — evidence the two paths were copied and drifted.
- **Failure** — The product knows, at that exact moment, that a person just re-asked because the last answer failed; it has the prior run in hand; and it writes a row that looks like a first attempt with a different intent string. So **the retry rate — the cleanest per-capability failure rate obtainable, and the only one needing no interpretation — reads as zero.** Worse, the concatenation defeats the fallback of intent-similarity clustering, because the retry's intent is not the original intent. No prior document names retry as a signal; LP4 enumerates discard, revert and refine and misses the one that is a struct field away.
- **Mechanism** — `Run.RetryOfRunID` beside the existing `ContinuesRunID` (identical field shape, same sparse index pattern), and keep the composed refinements in `Task.Refinements` rather than concatenating them into `Intent`, so the original request text stays comparable across attempts. Carry `Viewport` (FR15).
- **Hard clause** — **schema.**

#### IL12 · Nothing distinguishes an intent the person typed from one the product wrote for them
- **Evidence** — `contracts.go:138-170 TaskSpec` has no source field. Four producers converge on `Create` indistinguishably: the composer's typed text; `Continue`'s server-composed `continuationIntent(prior)`; `Retry`'s concatenation; and `Drift.Intent`, a canned server-authored request the ambient pill sends verbatim.
- **Failure** — Every rate in every proposed dashboard is meaningless while the denominator mixes populations. A hint accepted with one click and a paragraph somebody composed at 2 a.m. have different priors on apply rate, adjustment rate and cost tolerance. LP11 asks for shown/accepted counters on the hint — but the run a hint produces cannot be told apart from any other run, so LP11's own metric stops at the click and never reaches the outcome it exists to justify. PL16's proposed `origin` enum is about **transport** (web/api/mcp/email), not authorship, so it does not close this.
- **Mechanism** — `TaskSpec.Source` — `typed | hint:<kind> | continue | retry | schedule | rule` — set at the four call sites that already exist. One string: the segmentation key every rate needs and the join key LP11's counters need to reach an outcome.
- **Hard clause** — **schema.**

#### IL17 · The eval harness cannot exercise the loop it is being asked to learn from — and it commits with no delegation at all 🔴 *(corrects LP4's hard clause; two territories)*
- **Evidence** — `cli/agenteval.go:174-230 runProbe` calls `agent.NewPlanner(provider, elements, nil, nil, nil, nil).Run(...)` **directly** (`:211-212`): `agent.Service`, the RunStore, the state machine, Apply, adjustments, Discard, Revert and the journal are never instantiated. The emit callback discards the payload entirely (`:203`). Every probe reuses the hardcoded run id `"5eed000000000000000000ru"`. And `applyPlan` (`:243-252`) commits with `&domain.Principal{Sub: "eval"}` carrying **no `Delegation` field** — while `transaction_service.go:82` gates the **entire** delegation block on `if d := p.Delegation; d != nil`, so a nil delegation skips expiry (`:83`), root/on-behalf-of (`:86`), MaxOps (`:89`), `verifyDelegation` per op (`:110`) and DA11's content denylist (`:357-365`). The comment at `:241-242` states the choice deliberately. Probe fixtures are Go closures; `find backend -name testdata` returns nothing.
- **Failure** — Two axes at once. **(a)** LP4's hard clause reads "the promoted probe must run in the existing harness unmodified"; it cannot, because every artefact of the learning loop — an adjustment, a discard, a revert, a correction record, a state timestamp, a journal row — belongs to the Service layer the harness does not construct. A promoted real-world failure whose defining property is "the human dropped these four rows and then fixed two by hand" is not expressible. **(b)** The one write path in the corpus bypasses the exact layer where the product's most consequential failures live: FR12 (a reviewed plan destroyed by an expired grant) is invisible because expiry is inside the skipped block, and DA2 — cross-board filing 403ing on every case it exists for — is invisible for the same reason, which is precisely why Part One's researchers had to write disposable Go probes to prove it. The stated rationale is reasonable in isolation and wrong in composition: the envelope's unit tests exercise it with hand-built ops, and the corpus exercises **model-built** ops with no envelope, so nothing anywhere tests the pair. **The corpus never leaves the un-delegated world**, and that is the axis the agent's entire safety architecture lives on.
- **Mechanism** — Probe-layer only, no product code. (1) A Service-backed harness tier wired to `backend/internal/agent/agentmem`, which already implements RunStore and EventStore for exactly this purpose, so a probe can express "apply with adjustments {drop 3}" and assert on the resulting correction records, state timestamps and journal. (2) `applyPlan` mints the same delegation `agent.Service.Create` does — same capabilities, same MaxOps, same root, same expiry. Three probes fall out immediately and are worth more than the change costs: an expired grant must fail **recoverably** (FR12's acceptance line); a plan filing into a destination board must commit (DA2's, turning a throwaway script into a standing regression test); and a plan whose ops carry a key outside its ActionKind's declared set must be refused (DA11, table-driven). (3) Give each probe a distinct run id, and keep the plan-only path for the probes that do not need the loop.
- **Hard clause** — **probe.** Belongs in WAVE 0 beside CG4, MP11, SC22 and AX33.

#### IL18 · The eval sweep produces no artefact, so the product's only ground truth has no memory
- **Evidence** — `cli/agenteval.go:103` — the command's sole flag is `--verbose`. Results go to `fmt.Printf` and `os.Exit(1)`; nothing is written to disk; CI does not run it (correctly — it costs money). PERFECTION's "33 probes, 31–33 passing per run, ~$0.68/sweep" is a sentence a person typed into a markdown file.
- **Failure** — CG4 proposes `--repeat N`, `--compare` and a per-probe `Floor` — all correct, and all still ephemeral. Without a written record keyed to a build identity (IL10), a rate measured today cannot be compared with a rate measured after the next wave except by a human remembering a number, which is exactly how the two known-flaky probes came to be characterised in prose. The corpus is the only instrument the agent has ever been measured with and it forgets everything the moment the terminal scrolls.
- **Mechanism** — `--json out.jsonl` emitting one row per probe `{id, pass, verdict, cost, elapsed, actionKindCounts, model, promptHash, gitSHA, at}`, appended to a checked-in `evals/history.jsonl`; a `--baseline` mode that fails the sweep on a per-probe **rate drop** rather than on any single miss. Offline, cheap, and it turns CG4's floors from prose into data.
- **Hard clause** — **probe.**

#### IL22 · Every metric proposed so far is a read of raw user content — and the metrics that matter need no content at all
- **Evidence** — Content lives throughout the measurable surface: the journal stores the raw intent (`EvRunCreated` data `{"intent": …}`), refinement notes and steer text; `Run.Task.Intent` and the whole `Plan` — titles, card bodies, comment bodies — live on the run document. `PurgeTenant` correctly deletes both on account deletion, which is the system's own classification of this data as personal.
- **Failure** — This constraint determines the shape of the entire learning loop and no prior document states it. Any aggregate computed by reading runs is derived from content the user may delete at any moment, so a dashboard that caches outside that deletion path silently breaks the promise `PurgeTenant` makes. Meanwhile every metric anyone actually wants — apply rate, adjustment rate by action kind, time-to-decide, actions per turn, cost per accepted change, retry rate, security-event rate — is counts, enumerations, durations and hashes.
- **Mechanism** — A second, **content-free** `agent_metrics` collection written at terminal transitions, containing only enumerations and numbers — `{build (IL10), model (IL9), source (IL12), state, actionCountsByKind, correctionsByKind (IL1/IL2), durations (IL5), cost, calls, actionsPerTurn}` — plus opaque hashes (IL16's `IntentKey`). Never a title, a body, an element id, a board name or an intent string. That store is legitimately durable across account deletion because it holds nothing about a person; the content-bearing journal and run documents keep their current tenant scoping and purge behaviour. **Invariant: nothing that could be rendered to a model as text ever enters the metrics store.** This is what makes "we do not learn from your content" true by construction rather than by policy, and it is what lets IL23's dashboard exist at all.
- **Hard clause** — **schema.** Must precede IL23, not follow it.

#### IL23 · There is no operator, so the operator dashboard has no principal — and no log line to fall back on
- **Evidence** — Every agent read is tenant-scoped by construction; no tenant-wide list exists (PL9). `grep -rn "admin" backend/internal/transport/http backend/internal/domain` returns only Keycloak admin-client references — **there is no role, no claim check, no admin group in the auth middleware.** The agent service logs at Info exactly twice, both about expiry and crash reconciliation; a completed run, its cost, its verdict and its state are logged at **no** level. No metrics library exists in `go.mod`.
- **Failure** — PL15 proposes `GET /agent/analytics` and correctly constrains it "tenant-scoped, never cross-tenant" — which means the resulting dashboard cannot answer a single operator question. "Is the agent working for anyone?", "did last night's deploy raise the discard rate?", "which capability gets dropped most across all users?" are cross-tenant by definition. Today the honest answer for the person running this product is: open a Mongo shell. And there is no degraded fallback — with no Info line per run outcome, even log-scraping has nothing to scrape.
- **Mechanism** — Split the surface, which is the whole point. (1) A **user** surface: PL15's tenant-scoped "what has Qomra done for me and what did it cost", over PL9's port. (2) An **operator** surface reading ONLY the content-free store (IL22), which needs no per-tenant authority because it holds nothing per-tenant — apply/discard/revert/retry rates by build and by source, adjustment rate by action kind (which capability is worst), actions per turn, p50/p95 time-to-decide, cost per accepted change, provider error rate, security-event rate, all sliced by build so a deploy is a visible step change. Gate it on a Keycloak realm role checked once in the auth middleware — one claim check, the only new authority required. (3) Ship one `log.Info` per terminal transition with `{runId, state, actions, cost, calls, build}` **on day one**, so the fallback exists before the dashboard does.
- **Hard clause** — **handler.**

#### IL24 · The injection detectors have a numerator and no denominator, and nothing counts either 🔴
- **Evidence** — `contracts.go:415-419` — `EvSecIDOutOfScope` ("the primary detector for a successful prompt injection") and `EvSecSanitized` are journaled with a deliberate `security.` type prefix "so alerting can key on the prefix". Nothing in the tree alerts or counts: the only consumer anywhere is the eval harness's throwaway closure. `agent_events` has no index by type (IL6). `StateQuarantine` is a terminal state with no counter — and, per FR9, no producer either.
- **Failure** — Three documents add read surfaces on the argument that the injection posture holds — I4 (url reads), PL3 (text attachments), DA6 (comments), LP5 (peer-authored content), PL20 (email), PL17 (external MCP agents). The posture is a detector with **no measured base rate**: nobody knows how often it fires today, so nobody will be able to tell when a new read surface makes it fire more, and the quarantine rate — the one number that says whether the agent is under attack — is unobservable. This is the one place in this corner where the missing measurement is a safety property rather than a product one, and the code already named the alerting hook it never got.
- **Mechanism** — Count security events and quarantined terminal states in the content-free store (kind + build + source only, **never** the offending text), with a rate alert on the operator surface, and a probe asserting the counter increments when the corpus's injection probe fires. Rides IL6's index and IL22's store; costs nothing new.
- **Hard clause** — **handler.**

#### IL25 · Actions-per-turn is already emitted on every step *(corrects LP4)*
- **Evidence** — `planner.go:506-509` `emit(EvStepFinished, stepSummary(step, resp), map[string]any{"step": step, "calls": len(resp.Calls)})` fires on every model turn of every run, with a timestamp. LP4 lists "actions-per-turn — W4's starvation metric, measured in production rather than asserted by a prompt-conformance eval" among the four things its new RunOutcome record would make possible.
- **Failure** — It is not missing; it is unreadable, and that distinction changes the fix from a schema project to an index. The same journal already carries the proposed count (`EvPlanReady`) and the applied count (`EvOpsCommitted`), so proposed-minus-applied — the aggregate drop rate — is derivable today with no new writes at all.
- **Mechanism** — No new events. IL6's `(at, type)` index plus the denormalised tenant field makes `avg(calls) by build` a `$group` over `type = step.finished`. **LP4's RunOutcome should be scoped down accordingly:** it needs to add only what the journal genuinely cannot express — corrections (IL1/IL2), source (IL12) and build (IL10).
- **Hard clause** — **digest** — in the sense the law means it: the read that makes the rest possible.

## T2 — collaborator

#### IL3 · A discard carrying pending adjustments — the loudest rejection in the product — is deleted client-side before the request is sent 🔴
- **Evidence** — `agentStore.ts:266-273 discard()` calls `api.agentDiscard(run.id)` and clears `adjustments` in the same `set`; `api/client.ts:160` posts an empty body; `service.go:605-627 Discard` accepts no payload and records only `state: DISCARDED` with an empty reason.
- **Failure** — "I dropped four rows, reparented two, then gave up" and "I looked at it and said no" are categorically different events: the first names exactly which capabilities failed, with typed targets. Both land in the database as the same row. LP4 proposes one-tap reason chips on Discard — but the person has **already told you**, in a validated typed vocabulary, and the client throws it away before a chip would render. The chip is a worse signal than the one being discarded.
- **Mechanism** — `POST /agent/runs/:id/discard {adjustments, reasonChip?}` — the adjustment vocabulary is already server-validated on the apply path, so accepting it here costs one field and no new type. Store as corrections with `outcome: abandoned`, the highest-weight label in the set.
- **Hard clause** — **schema.**

#### IL13 · The review surface computes an attention record and spends it on hover styling
- **Evidence** — `agentStore.ts:33-46` — `hoverSeq` (which plan row the pointer is on) and `tileFocus {id,label,seqs,pinned}` (which destination board the reviewer drilled into, and whether they pinned the list to it) are live client state. Both are cleared on every terminal transition and **neither is ever transmitted**. Events carry per-event `at`, so the plan's arrival time is known client-side.
- **Failure** — The entire safety argument is "a human reviews the plan", and LP3 argues from human-factors literature that the guarantee decays under volume while proposing no way to observe the decay. It is observable now, from state the client already computes: whether the reviewer hovered any row, whether they opened a destination, how long between the plan rendering and Apply. Today "approved after 900ms without touching a row" and "approved after two minutes of drilling into four boards" are byte-identical database records. LP3's risk banding and collapsing cannot be validated without this, and shipping collapsing blind is exactly the failure LP3 warns about.
- **Mechanism** — One review-summary object posted with Apply or Discard — `{msToDecision, rowsHovered, tilesOpened, scrolledToEnd, adjustmentsMade, adjustmentsRetracted}` — derived entirely from existing state. No new UI, no new events, one field on the existing request. **Counts and durations only: no per-row timing, no content, nothing identifying which card was looked at** (IL22). Note the dependency inversion this creates with AX9: once focus produces `hoverSeq`, the metric measures keyboard reviewers honestly too.
- **Hard clause** — **schema.**

#### IL15 · The strongest post-apply signal — the human quietly fixing the agent's work by hand — is fully recorded, fully indexed, and never joined
- **Evidence** — `domain/transaction.go:29-51` carries BoardID, UserID, `Ops[].ElementID`, CreatedAt, `Origin` (human|agent) and `AgentRunID`; the agent stamps `Origin: OriginAgent, AgentRunID: run.ID` at commit. `repository/mongo/indexes.go:31-36` indexes `(boardId, createdAt desc)` and `agentRunId` sparse — **with the comment "What did the AI change on this board?"**. Nothing in the tree joins the two sides.
- **Failure** — Revert is rare and loud; hand-repair is common and silent. A person who applies a 40-action plan and then, over the next four minutes, renames two columns and drags six cards out of one has told you exactly which two actions were wrong **and what the right answer was**. LP4 counts `revertedWithin` as regret and structurally misses the larger population who never press revert because fixing is faster than explaining. It is also the only correction channel carrying a corrected **value** — the Adjustment vocabulary expresses "wrong" before apply and there is no post-apply vocabulary at all, so the transaction journal is already it.
- **Mechanism** — A `repairs` aggregation over data that needs no new writes: `$lookup` agent transactions by `agentRunId` to get the run's element ids, then a windowed scan on the `(boardId, createdAt)` index for human-origin transactions within N minutes whose op element ids intersect that set, emitting per-action `repairedWithin` plus the op kind. **Privacy clause:** store the op kind and the element id only — never the new content — unless the tenant explicitly opts in, because the corrected value IS user content (IL22).
- **Hard clause** — **handler.** One of the two highest-value items in this corner and it needs no new writes at all.

#### IL16 · Re-asking is detectable today with indexes that already exist, and nothing looks
- **Evidence** — `contracts.go:139-141` stores `Task.Intent` raw; `indexes.go:38-39` indexes `agent_runs` on `(tenantSub, createdAt desc)` and `(task.rootBoardId, createdAt desc)`.
- **Failure** — "Where do people re-ask the same thing" sounds like a new pipeline and is a two-index query: successive runs by one tenant on one board within a window whose intents are near-duplicates. Three consecutive near-identical intents is the operational definition of a capability that does not work. It is also the missing trigger for IN14 ("suggest the schedule or rule after the third identical run"), which is specified with no mechanism for detecting the third — because none exists.
- **Mechanism** — Store a normalized `Task.IntentKey` at Create — lowercased, punctuation-stripped, stopworded, hashed. Re-ask clusters become a `$group`, and IN14 gets its trigger. Because the key is a hash and not the text, the aggregate is content-free by construction, which is what makes it legal to retain in IL22's store.
- **Hard clause** — **schema.**

#### IL19 · The one aggregate read that exists deliberately excludes every run worth learning from *(corrects PL15's siting)*
- **Evidence** — `service.go:852-886 Audit` filters to COMPLETED/REVERTED/PARTIAL with `len(Plan.Actions) > 0`, with the comment "a discarded preview changed nothing, and listing it as a change is exactly the noise that makes an audit log stop being read". It is also tenant-filtered. PL15 proposes `GET /agent/analytics` "rendered beside the audit view".
- **Failure** — The filter is right for an audit and fatal for a learning surface: discarded, failed, quarantined, question-only and zero-action runs are **one hundred percent of the negative label set**. Building the analytics next to the audit invites reusing its query shape, and the first version will confidently report an apply rate of 1.0 — a metric wrong in the direction that flatters the product, which is the worst kind.
- **Mechanism** — The analytics read gets its own port (PL9's `ListByTenant` with an explicit state filter) and **its first probe assertion is that DISCARDED runs appear in the denominator.** The audit view stays exactly as it is — it is correct for what it is. (FR21 makes the separate, opposite argument for the audit itself: it must include runs that wrote and then failed.)
- **Hard clause** — **probe.**

#### IL20 · The suggestion surface with the clearest yes/no in the product records neither branch
- **Evidence** — `AgentDone.tsx:100-138` — the `ProposedRule` card's Save button appends the sentence to `board.content.agentInstructions` through an ordinary board transaction **with no run link and no provenance**; "Not now" sets local `dismissed` state and the card vanishes leaving nothing. The model writes `Plan.ProposedRule` and the outcome is never written back to the run.
- **Failure** — A two-button experiment the product runs on every corrected run and never scores. "Does the agent propose rules people actually want?" is the direct measure of whether the entire memory wave (CG5, CG6, LP1, IN14) is worth building, and it is unanswerable. Worse, an accepted rule enters the board's rules string indistinguishable from something the owner typed — so CG5's future memory store has no lineage to migrate into `evidenceRunIDs`, and LP6's HUMAN/DERIVED/AGENT tiering has nothing to tier existing rules by. **The moment the provenance is knowable is the moment it is discarded.**
- **Mechanism** — `POST /agent/runs/:id/rule {accepted: bool}` recorded on the run before the board write; the board write carries `{text, sourceRunId}` rather than a bare string append. Two calls, and the memory wave arrives already measurable instead of retrospectively unmeasurable.
- **Hard clause** — **schema.**

#### IL21 · The frontend is where several agent failures actually happen, and it reports them to `console.error`
- **Evidence** — `Toaster.tsx:63` `componentDidCatch(error) { console.error('render error', error); }`, and the whole-app crash screen (`:66-80`) is hardcoded English on a bilingual product. `vite.config.ts` configures no `build.sourcemap`. `api/client.ts:24-28` sends Authorization, Content-Type and the share headers only — no client build identifier. `public/sw.js:30-38` caches hashed assets cache-first, so a broken bundle persists on a client after the server is fixed.
- **Failure** — The agent's UI is the most complex surface in the product — the ghost layer, the review dock, and `computeEffective` (`agentStore.ts:504-566`), a hand-maintained mirror of the server's `ApplyAdjustments` that **must** agree with it. When those two diverge the symptom is a React crash or a silently wrong preview on a machine in Oman, and the operator learns nothing, ever. This is the one class of failure server-side instrumentation cannot see, and the class most likely to be produced by the very waves these documents propose.
- **Mechanism** — Minimal and privacy-clean: `componentDidCatch` and the api client's failure path POST `{buildSHA, route, errorName, stackTop, lang}` to one authenticated, rate-limited endpoint — no user content, no board ids, no element ids. Add `build.sourcemap: 'hidden'` so a stack resolves, and an `X-Client-Build` header so a stale service-worker client is detectable from the server side (MO9).
- **Hard clause** — **handler.**

#### IL26 · Every tool refusal the model receives, and the whole transcript containing them, is discarded when the run returns *(corrects PL15)*
- **Evidence** — `tools.go:1201-1216 Execute` returns `r.fail(...)` outcomes straight to the model **with no emit**; `EvActionStaged` fires only on a successful stage. `planner.go:433` `messages := []cognition.Message{opening}` is a local slice that dies with the function — tool results, refusals, retries and the review turn are never persisted. Harness refusals land instead as free-text prose in `Plan.Notes`, uncountable by kind.
- **Failure** — Which tools the model tries and fails to use, and how often it retries after a refusal, is the best per-capability difficulty measure available and the direct evidence for the CV/DA-corner claims that a verb is unreachable (DA4's TASK blindness, DA2's cross-board 403s, CV8's selection-scope refusals). PL15 proposes a "refusal histogram from event types" — there are no refusal event types, and the refusals themselves exist only inside a discarded slice. **The most instructive thing that happens in a run is the part nothing records.**
- **Mechanism** — An `EvToolRefused` event carrying `{tool, reasonCode}` — a small closed enum, **never** the message text or the offending id — emitted from `reply.fail`. That gives a refusal histogram by tool and by build with no content and no transcript retention, which is also the right privacy answer: the transcript should stay unpersisted; only the typed shape of what failed needs to survive.
- **Hard clause** — **schema**, and it must land with IL8 so the vocabulary changes once.

#### IL27 · The product deletes agent runs as the user's personal data and refuses to export them as the user's personal data
- **Evidence** — `service/account_service.go:175-214 DataExport` contains User, Boards, Elements and Labels only; agent runs, plans, intents, steers, refinements, cost and the entire event journal are absent. Meanwhile `DeleteAccount` calls `PurgeTenant`, which deletes both.
- **Failure** — The system's two data-subject paths classify the same records differently: deletion treats a run as the user's personal data, export treats it as not the user's data. For a filmmaker whose intents describe an unreleased production, the runs contain the most sensitive text in the account — what they asked for, in their own words, plus everything the agent wrote about it. It also matters for the loop's legitimacy: the honest position "we keep only content-free aggregates and you can see and take everything else" (IL22) is one the export currently cannot back up. (DL13 covers the same inconsistency across five more collections; DA15 covers the transaction journal, which is neither deleted nor exported — three data classes treated three different ways.)
- **Mechanism** — Add `AgentRuns` and, behind a flag, the event journal to `DataExport` — the ports already exist (`ListByBoard` with an empty board id is the tenant-wide read the quota code already uses). Then state the retention contract in the Privacy tab in **both** languages: content-bearing runs are yours, exportable and deletable; content-free metrics are the product's and are never trained on and never contain your words.
- **Hard clause** — **handler**, shared with DL13.

## T3 — frontier

#### IL14 · An adjustment made and then retracted is deleted by the affordance designed to be forgiving
- **Evidence** — `agentStore.ts:217-218 undoAdjust(seq)` filters every adjustment aimed at that row out of the array; the comment calls it "the 'put it back' affordance".
- **Mechanism** — "I dropped this, thought about it, and put it back" is a hesitation signal, and it is the cheapest available discriminator between a plan that is **wrong** and a plan that is merely **surprising** — precisely the distinction LP3's risk bands need to be calibrated against and the ground truth CG11's confidence chip would have to be graded on. It is currently deleted, and deleted by the one interaction that proves the reviewer was actually reading. So: an append-only `adjustmentLog` beside the mutable array, recording both applications and retractions with a monotonic index; ship counts with IL13's summary. Two lines, no UI change, no content.

---

# Corner DF — The domain: film production as it is actually practised, and Oman

*Method: the craft's real artefacts and their structures, sourced and cited,
reasoned against `planner.go`, `tools.go`, `digest.go`, the corpus and the
frontend. PERFECTION and HORIZON contain no domain modelling at all; Part One
touches film only as flavour (IN4's interior/exterior, I3's "shoot schedules ARE
timelines", scattered "Pre-Production" examples). No prior item names a single
film **artefact**, its structure, its vocabulary, or an Oman constraint. So this
corner is genuinely unoccupied, and the five items that CONTRADICT prior work
(DF2, DF3, DF9, DF12, DF14) are the ones to read first.*

*The central claim in one line: **`planner.go:97-108` contains twelve lines of
correct film nouns and zero film structure, and every structural rule in the same
prompt was tuned on generic knowledge-work boards where it happens to be wrong
for this trade.** The agent can say "call sheet" and cannot make one. That is
exactly the gap between a colleague and a toy, and it closes almost entirely in
prompt, data and templates — the plumbing already exists.*

## T1 — parity and live wrongs

#### DF1 · The film knowledge in the prompt is a noun list, not a domain model — twelve lines naming artefacts the agent cannot build
- **Evidence** — `planner.go:97-108`, the **only** domain content in the entire system prompt: "script lock, casting, location scouting, permits, shot list, schedule, budget lock; call sheets, principal photography, dailies, continuity log, sound report; assembly cut, picture lock, sound mix, colour grade, delivery masters." Repeated in compressed form at `:309-313`. Grep of the whole backend for `call sheet|stripboard|breakdown sheet|day out of days|deliverable` outside those two blocks returns nothing.
- **Failure** — Every one of those nouns is a real artefact with a structure a practitioner recognises instantly and rejects if malformed. A call sheet has a header block, a scene table, a cast table with per-person makeup and set times, department notes, walkie channels, weather, sunrise/sunset, nearest hospital and an advance schedule. The agent knows the **word** and produces a sticky note saying "Call sheets". That is the precise moment a filmmaker decides the tool is a toy: it used the right word and then proved it did not know what the word meant. Everything else in this corner is a consequence of this one absence.
- **Mechanism** — A domain pack, not a bigger prompt. A `domain/film.go` table of **artefact specs** — per artefact: canonical name (en + ar), the board shape it takes (document / table with named columns / column of cards / to-do list / connected graph), required fields, and one worked micro-example. The digest gains a DOMAIN block **only** when the board's own vocabulary triggers it (a cheap keyword match over element titles: scene/shot/call/cast/crew/location/grade/mix/مشهد/لقطة/تصوير), so the pack costs zero tokens on a non-film board. The AUTHORING register consults it before staging. Same shape as LP15's playbooks and `cardSwatches`' name→hex resolution: the model names the artefact, the server supplies the structure.
- **Hard clause** — **digest.** Conditional inclusion is what keeps the pack free for everyone else.

#### DF2 · The naming rule forbids the trade's own identifier — and uses a film example to do it 🔴
- **Evidence** — `tools.go:178-181`, `create_column`'s schema, literally: `"Name the category, not the item: \"Data Chip\", not \"Scene 3: The Data Chip\"."` Restated at `planner.go:127-128`. Against: scene numbers are **locked** at the shooting script and never renumbered (inserted scenes get letters — A-scenes), precisely because breakdown, stripboard, DOOD, call sheet, lined script, camera and sound reports and editorial all key off them.
- **Failure** — A live wrong dressed as a style rule, aimed at exactly this user. Strip "Scene 3" from a card and you have severed the only join key the production has. A 1st AD who asks the agent to organise a breakdown gets back cards named "Harbour Office" and "Chase" — unschedulable, unmatchable to the script, unusable on set. And the example chosen to teach the rule is a **film** example, which means the rule was written by reasoning about film without knowing film. No prior document questioned it; CV18 and CV13 both accept the aesthetic premise.
- **Mechanism** — Make the rule conditional rather than universal: titles are labels **except** where the domain has a canonical identifier — a scene number, a shot number, a budget account code, an invoice number — in which case the identifier leads and is never rewritten. Add a rename guard refusing (with a routing message, as P1's lock refusal is specified) any rename that would drop a leading `^\d+[A-Z]?[\s.:—-]` token or a `^\d+\.\d+` account code from an existing title.
- **Hard clause** — **handler.** The guard, not the prompt line — a prompt sentence loses to a strong aesthetic instruction three paragraphs earlier.

#### DF3 · The 20-character column title cannot hold a slugline, and a slugline is the unit film schedules are built from 🔴
- **Evidence** — `tools.go:180` "Column name, 20 characters or fewer — headers render uppercase and clip beyond that." Board titles cap at 24 (`:169`). A scene heading is `3 INT. HARBOUR OFFICE – NIGHT` (29 chars) or `12 EXT. WADI SHAB – DAY` (23). A stripboard strip **is** scene number + INT/EXT + set + D/N + cast IDs + page count.
- **Failure** — The product's structural primitive (the column) is physically too narrow for the domain's structural primitive (the scene). So the agent must either truncate — destroying INT/EXT and D/N, the two facts that decide lighting, permits and schedule order — or abandon columns for this material entirely. It currently does neither knowingly: it invents a short paraphrase and loses the data silently. A genuine product constraint, not an agent bug, which is why it belongs here: the fix is knowing **which shape to reach for**, not widening the header.
- **Mechanism** — Teach the mapping, do not widen the column. A scene set is a TABLE (`#`, `I/E`, `Set`, `D/N`, `Pages`, `Cast`, `Notes`) or a card per scene with the slugline as its first line and the number in the title; never a column per scene. The prompt gains a hard clause: **"A container title is a bucket name. A scene, a shot, a budget line and a deliverable are ROWS, not buckets."** Pairs with DF14 — the table has to be readable back before this is honest advice.
- **Hard clause** — **digest**, via DF14.

#### DF4 · The call sheet — the trade's densest artefact, and the agent has every part except the recipe
- **Evidence** — Structure: production title + day X of Y + date; general crew call; set address **and separate** parking/basecamp address; nearest hospital address and route; weather with sunrise/sunset; a scene table (scene #, I/E, description, D/N, pages, cast IDs, location); a cast table (cast ID, character, actor, pickup / makeup-hair / set times, status); a crew grid by department; department notes; walkie channels; advance schedule for tomorrow. Product side: `create_table` (`tools.go:338-352`), `write_document` (`:465`), `create_column`, `create_heading` — all present; **no composite that assembles them.**
- **Failure** — The artefact a filmmaker sends out every shooting day, and the one that most obviously separates a colleague from a toy. It is board-shaped in a way that flatters QomraNote specifically: a BOARD per shooting day, headings for regions, two tables, a crew column, a document for notes — exactly what a nested board is for. Asked "make tomorrow's call sheet", the agent today produces one note. And the information to fill it is mostly already on the user's other boards, which makes this the flagship demo for cross-board reads (I5/DA2).
- **Mechanism** — A `film.callSheet` artefact spec plus a composite recipe in the PLAYBOOK block (where every other composite already lives): create_board `Day N — <date>`; heading + document for the header block; create_table for scenes; create_table for cast; create_column for department notes; and **`unmet` entries naming every field it could not source** (hospital, weather, parking) rather than inventing them — which `planner.go:297-303` already mandates as correct behaviour for missing facts. Two of the eight header fields are Oman-specific (DF24).
- **Hard clause** — **probe.** Seeded schedule + cast boards; assert both tables compiled with the required headers and that hospital/weather appear in `unmet` rather than as invented strings.

#### DF5 · The breakdown sheet and its twenty element categories — the join between script and everything downstream
- **Evidence** — The twenty standard categories: Cast, Extras, Stunts, Vehicles, Props, Costumes, Makeup, Livestock, Animal Handlers, Music, Sound, Set Dressing, Greenery, Special Equipment, Security, Additional Labor, Optical Effects, Mechanical Effects, Miscellaneous, Scene Notes. Traditional colour marking: props violet, extras green, silent bits yellow, stunts orange, SFX blue. Header per sheet: production, scene #, page count in eighths, I/E, set, D/N, synopsis. Product side: `create_label` (`tools.go:290-296`) takes a **name only**.
- **Failure** — Breakdown is the pivot of the craft: schedule, budget and every department's prep derive from it. A domain-competent agent handed a scene list should propose the twenty buckets by name and tag what it can see. A generic one produces "Props", "People", "Stuff". IN4 gets adjacent — "mark every shot interior or exterior" as classification — but treats the axis as arbitrary and binary; the real axes are twenty, named, and standard.
- **Mechanism** — Ship the twenty as the seeded label vocabulary for a film-triggered board (LABELS already ride the digest, and `create_label` already warns "reuse before you coin"). Add the breakdown-sheet artefact spec. The verb is IN4's classification tool with a **domain-supplied** axis rather than a model-invented one — which also fixes IN4's own complaint that 40 individual `set_color` calls starve the step budget. Depends on DF10 for the colours to mean anything.
- **Hard clause** — **digest** (the seeded vocabulary must be visible as labels, not invented per run).

#### DF6 · The shot list — the agent is told to reach for a table and never told which columns
- **Evidence** — `planner.go:171-173` explicitly names it: "create_table when items share repeating attributes — budget lines, a shot list, a schedule. Six cards each saying 'Day 3 | Interior | 4 pages' is a table that has not been made yet." The real column set: Scene #, Shot #, Shot Size (VWS/WS/MS/MCU/CU/ECU), Angle, Movement, Lens, Subject, Description, Equipment, Est. time, Done. `create_table` (`tools.go:338-352`) takes only `rows` — untyped strings, header row first.
- **Failure** — The prompt gets within one inch of competence and stops. "Day 3 | Interior | 4 pages" is a **schedule** row, not a shot row, and the model is left to invent headers. Invented headers are the failure a DP notices in two seconds — a shot list without a movement or lens column is a list of sentences. The cheapest fix in the corner: it is literally a header row in a spec table.
- **Mechanism** — Attach canonical header rows to the artefact specs (DF1) for shot list, scene list, budget top sheet, DOOD, deliverables, cast list, crew list, location list. The prompt's create_table paragraph gains: "When the table is a known production document, use its standard columns — you will be given them." No schema change; a data change.
- **Hard clause** — **probe.** "Make a shot list for scene 4" → the compiled header contains size, angle, movement.

#### DF7 · Deliverables and QC — the one film artefact that is natively a checklist, and the agent has `create_todo`
- **Evidence** — Delivery structure: ProRes 422 HQ HD and 4K masters; textless elements; DCDM/DCP for a theatrical grade; audio at 48k/24-bit, mix normalised to −24 LKFS; a **fully-filled 5.1 M&E submix** with foley and ambience fill and no dialogue; stems; Dolby Atmos where applicable; IMSC1/TTML captions with `xml:lang`, `tts:origin`/`tts:extent` and a `ttp:frameRate` matching picture; DCP subtitles conforming to SMPTE ST 428-7:2014; manual QC pass. Product side: `create_todo` (`tools.go:196-201`), `add_tasks` (`:433-443`), `toggle_task` — all present.
- **Failure** — Delivery is where independent films die: a festival or platform bounce for a missing M&E or a wrong LKFS costs weeks and money. It is also the single best fit between this domain and this product — a delivery list is a to-do list with hard, externally-defined, checkable items, and QomraNote's TASK type plus the reminder sweep is exactly the right container. A generic agent writes "Export final file". A competent one writes forty items with the specs attached and knows M&E is the one people forget.
- **Mechanism** — A `film.deliverables` spec parameterised by destination (festival DCP / broadcaster / streamer / self-distribution) producing a real to-do list, with a document alongside carrying the numeric specs so the checklist stays scannable. This is also the highest-value target for A4's morning digest and A3's watcher: "three delivery items unticked, festival deadline in 11 days."
- **Hard clause** — none; data plus an existing verb. Blocked on DA4 (TASKs invisible) for the read-back half.

#### DF8 · Post-production is a dependency GRAPH and the product's best-built capability is aimed at nothing else
- **Evidence** — The turnover chain: offline edit → picture lock → turnover (AAF/XML/EDL out) → conform to full-res camera masters → online/VFX pulls → colour grade → sound: spot, edit, ADR, foley, pre-dub, final mix → M&E → layback → masters → QC → deliver. Product side: `design_as("flow")`, `connect` with `relation=blocks`/`depends_on`, LayoutFlow — all shipped and documented at `planner.go:258-263`, `tools.go:308-312`, `:424-430`.
- **Failure** — QomraNote built a genuinely good connector-and-flow engine (the most recent commit is literally "lines connect reliably on BOTH ends") and the agent has **no worked domain example that uses it**. Post is the canonical dependency graph of this trade: picture lock BLOCKS conform, conform BLOCKS grade, mix DEPENDS ON locked picture, M&E DEPENDS ON the mix. A filmmaker who sees the agent draw that correctly, with the right arrows in the right direction, will believe everything else it says — and getting the direction wrong is instantly visible to a professional.
- **Mechanism** — One worked example in the PLAYBOOK using the real chain and the real relation vocabulary, plus a `film.postGraph` spec listing nodes and typed edges so the model does not have to recall them. Entirely prompt + data over shipped capability. **Note the dependency: DA5 (LINE elements never enter scope) and CV3 (connect actions never render as ghosts) mean the agent draws this graph and can neither see nor preview it** — which makes this the strongest product argument for prioritising DA5 and CV3.
- **Hard clause** — none new; blocked on DA5 + CV3.

#### DF9 · The card swatch palette already IS the stripboard code and the WGA revision wheel — and nothing knows it 🔴
- **Evidence** — `tools.go:114-124` defines exactly: default (white), yellow, red, green, blue, purple, orange, pink, dark. Stripboard convention: INT/DAY = white, EXT/DAY = yellow, EXT/NIGHT = blue, INT/NIGHT = green. Script revision wheel, in order: White → Blue → Pink → Yellow → Green → Goldenrod. **The product's palette contains the stripboard's four exactly, and the revision wheel's first five exactly.**
- **Failure** — The most striking coincidence in this sweep. The product ships the trade's two colour codes by accident and treats colour as decoration — `planner.go:174-175` says only "add_color when the subject has a palette" and means mood boards. Colour-as-semantics is what makes a stripboard readable across a room: a producer glances and sees the night-exterior load without reading a word. It costs nothing to teach and it is the detail that makes a professional ask who built this.
- **Mechanism** — Two lines of vocabulary in the domain pack: the strip mapping and the revision order, stated as conventions the model applies when the material is scenes or script versions rather than as free choice. **But this is worthless until CV1 is fixed** — `set_color` writes a key no renderer reads — and conversely CV1 is worth considerably more than it looked when it was a generic bug, because it is the mechanism under the trade's own visual language.
- **Hard clause** — **compiler** — inherited from CV1.

#### DF14 · The table digest truncates at 6 rows × 5 columns, so every canonical film document is invisible on re-read 🔴 *(extends CV10, same function, twelve lines up)*
- **Evidence** — `digest.go:1213-1214` `maxTableRowsShown = 6`, `maxTableColsShown = 5`; applied at `:1158-1169`, which slices columns and appends "…N more row(s)". Re-verified. Real sizes: a call sheet cast table is 10–25 rows × 7 cols; a shot list 40–120 × 8–11; a DOOD is cast × 20–40 shooting days; a budget top sheet ~30 accounts; a stripboard 60–200 strips; a deliverables list 30–50 items.
- **Failure** — The most consequential thing in this corner, and invisible without the domain. `planner.go:171-173` tells the agent that budget lines, shot lists and schedules **want** to be tables — and then the read path can see six rows of them. So the agent writes a 40-row shot list and, on the next turn or the next run or the Continue, sees six rows and "…34 more row(s)". Ask it to add a shot and it cannot check for duplicates. Ask "is the budget over?" and it sees five of thirty accounts. Every register degrades on precisely the material this user's work consists of. CV10 diagnosed the formula-vs-value bug at `digest.go:1152` — the same function — and walked past the truncation; Part One's own thesis ("it is short of sight") is more true here than anywhere it looked.
- **Mechanism** — Intent-aware and shape-aware budgeting, not a bigger constant. (a) **Always render the full header row** regardless of width — the columns are the schema and cost almost nothing. (b) Raise the row budget for the table the request is about, in the spirit of IN17's foveated perception, keeping 6 for incidental tables. (c) A `read_table(elementId, fromRow, count)` paging read, riding the existing `maxToolOutputBytes` cap. (d) The elision note states **true dimensions** — "40 rows × 9 columns; showing 6×5" — so the model knows what it is missing rather than believing the table is small.
- **Hard clause** — **digest.** Probe: seed a 40×9 shot list, ask "which shots have no lens noted?" — must page or refuse, never answer from six rows.

#### DF18 · The worked example teaches an aesthetic that is right for a writer and wrong for a 1st AD 🔴 *(contradicts the prompt's flagship example)*
- **Evidence** — `planner.go:139-158` — 64 screenplay beats; one-column-per-scene is called "a wall"; the recommended answer is three act columns. `:129-131` generalises: "Three to six columns on a board reads well. Past about seven you are building a wall." Against: a shooting schedule for a modest feature is 18–30 day-columns; a DOOD is 20–40 columns; a stripboard is one strip per scene, 60–200 of them.
- **Failure** — The prompt's structural aesthetics were tuned on knowledge-work boards and are stated as universal law. For the writer they are correct — act structure IS the question. For the same film two weeks later, the 1st AD's board legitimately has thirty columns, and the agent will refuse to build it, build three and call the rest unmet, or restructure the AD's board into acts and **destroy a schedule**. Worse, the prompt's own critique machinery (`DetectDrift`, `MeasurePlan`, `hollow.go`) will flag a correct stripboard as bad. This is a case where the product's taste actively fights the domain.
- **Mechanism** — Make the aesthetic conditional on the **artefact**, not on the board: "Three to six containers is the default for a board of ideas. Some documents have a fixed shape the trade decided — a schedule has one column per day, a DOOD one per day, a stripboard one strip per scene. When you are building a known document, its shape wins over this guidance." Correspondingly the quality checks must exempt recognised artefacts, or they will keep punishing the right answer. Pairs with DF31: which aesthetic applies depends on who is asking.
- **Hard clause** — **handler** (the critique exemption), not the prompt line.

#### DF24 · The Oman permit chain is a known graph of authorities, documents and lead times — and it is the corpus's own probe, ungraded
- **Evidence** — Corpus probe D1 is exactly "set up the paperwork we need before we can shoot in a public place in Muscat" (`cli/agentcorpus.go:185`), graded by `authoringGrade` — structure only. The real chain: General Filming Permit from the Ministry of Information via gov.om (fee 0 OMR, stated service timeline 10 business days) — in practice 2–3 weeks, to 3–5 weeks by content; the GFP gates temporary import of equipment through customs with a **10% deposit on kit-list value**, released a month after exit; required documents are a Letter of Intent (synopsis, commissioning body, locations, contributors), crew passport copies, a full kit list with models, country of manufacture, serial numbers and values plus **photographs of serial numbers** on high-value items, and a signed letter authorising the local partner; drone filming needs Civil Aviation Authority **and** Defence approval, 3–4 to 6–8 weeks, commercial applicants only via a local production company, with a military observer required in some areas and confiscation at the airport for unpermitted drones; aerial 15–21 days; protected areas need Ministry of Heritage & Tourism; the Grand Mosque and Royal Opera House need their own; crew visas via evisa.rop.gov.om. **And an absence worth teaching: Oman has no film rebate or tax incentive as of this research** — an agent that offers to plan one is hallucinating, and every generic film-budgeting source assumes an incentive exists.
- **Failure** — This is the probe the product already believes matters, and the grade cannot tell a real answer from a beautiful fiction. The right answer names five authorities, three lead times and eight documents; the generic answer is a column called "Permits" with three cards. The kit list with serial numbers is the detail that decides whether the shoot happens — a genuinely hard, genuinely tedious artefact a filmmaker would be delighted to have generated as a table.
- **Mechanism** — An `oman.permits` fact pack: authority graph, document checklist, lead-time table, the customs deposit rule, the drone restrictions, the no-incentive fact — **each with a source and a stated 'as of' date** so the model cites rather than asserts and staleness is visible. It composes into DF20's backward planner (the lead times are the offsets) and produces the kit-list table directly. Upgrade probe D1's grade from structural to a domain rubric asserting the authorities and lead times appear.
- **Hard clause** — **digest**, with the provenance constraint (LP8's rule applied to domain knowledge). The finding that most directly answers "colleague or toy" for THIS user.

#### DF25 · No timezone anywhere in the product, and every call time this user writes is Asia/Muscat +04 🔴
- **Evidence** — `grep -rn "timezone|timeZone|TimeZone|Asia/Muscat|UTC+4"` across `backend/internal/` and `frontend/src/` returns **zero matches**. `set_reminder`'s schema hands the model a UTC instant: `"RFC3339 timestamp, e.g. 2026-09-01T09:00:00Z"` (`tools.go:620-627`). `formatTime` (`i18n/index.ts:567-573`) renders in the browser's local zone with no stored zone. Oman is UTC+04 year-round, no DST.
- **Failure** — A call sheet is a list of times and nothing else. "Crew call 05:30" written by an agent that produces `05:30Z` is **09:30 in Muscat** — the shoot is over. Because Oman has no DST the error is a constant four hours, which is worse than a variable one: consistent enough to look like a deliberate product decision and wrong on every row. It also silently poisons P5's due dates, A1's scheduled runs, A4's morning digest and the reminder sweep, all of which the prior documents specify without noticing there is no zone. A live wrong of the same class as CV1 — a value written that means something other than what it renders as.
- **Mechanism** — A workspace timezone in LocalizationSettings (defaulting from the browser), stored on the account; the digest states "times are Asia/Muscat (+04)" so the model reasons in local time; `set_reminder`/`set_due` accept a local wall-clock time and **the server converts**; every rendered time carries its zone when it is not the viewer's.
- **Hard clause** — **handler.** The conversion happens server-side once, not in three places. Prerequisite for DF4, DF20, DF22 and DF23 being true rather than approximately true.

#### DF33 · The template gallery ships empty, and eight film templates are the cheapest domain competence available 🔴 *(corrects P3's priority)*
- **Evidence** — `TemplatePicker.tsx:44`, the empty state, literally: `"No templates yet. Right-click a board → \"Convert to template\"."` (re-verified). `api.templates()` has no seeded system content; grep of `backend/` for `systemTemplate|seedTemplates|isSystemTemplate` returns nothing. Meanwhile P3 and DA12 both specify agent access to a template system whose gallery has nothing in it. (JN3 adds the other half: four English-only three-sticky-note templates exist behind a CLI seed command that a deployment may never have run.)
- **Failure** — P3 proposes `use_template` so the agent "instantiates rather than reinvents" — with an empty library it instantiates nothing, so P3 as specified is a **no-op for a new user**. And it inverts the priority: the expensive half (a verb, a handler, a compiler, a probe) is specified and the free half (eight boards of correct content) is not. Every artefact in this corner is a template: call sheet, breakdown, shot list, stripboard day, DOOD, budget top sheet, casting pipeline, deliverables, festival tracker, recce. A filmmaker who opens QomraNote and sees those ten names in the picker has learned everything about whether this tool is for them, before typing a word.
- **Mechanism** — Author the templates as board subtrees, ship them as system templates in both languages, and let the agent name them. Content work, not engineering, and **the one item here with no code dependency at all** — it can ship this week and it makes DF1's domain pack legible to a human as well as to the model. It also gives every probe in the corpus a real seed instead of a synthetic one, which is LP4's argument arriving by a different road.
- **Hard clause** — none.

#### DF36 · The eval corpus is film-flavoured and grades nothing about film 🔴 *(corrects the premise that the corpus is generic)*
- **Evidence** — **Fourteen of the thirty-three probes are film-shaped**, including D1 "the paperwork we need before we can shoot in a public place in Muscat", A1 "…for drama film from your imagination \"oman\"", A2 documentary scoping-to-delivery, B1 script draft-to-locked, B2 crew structure, B3 location comparison, D2 two-day shoot crew of six, C3 budget numbers by department, M2 "1200 rials", M6 casting cards, N2 "make a film — the whole production plan" (`cli/agentcorpus.go:26,32,69,83,94,185,186,189,310,422,483`). Every one routes to `authoringGrade` (`:692-722`), which asserts only: no undisclosed empty containers, content > 0, content ≥ containers, ≥ 10 actions, no duplicate titles, not truncated. **Nothing about correctness.**
- **Failure** — The measurement surface is already pointed at this domain and measures none of it. A run producing ten beautifully-shaped columns of fabricated Omani permit procedure scores identically to one naming the Ministry of Information, the CAA and the three-week lead time. So every domain improvement proposed here is currently unmeasurable — and worse, the corpus creates the impression the domain is covered. CG4 argues the harness cannot see distributions because it runs each probe once; the deeper problem on these fourteen probes is that it is **not measuring the right variable at all**. This is the fourth axis of the corpus's blindness, beside flatland (M5), single-player (MP11), the small (SC22) and the un-delegated (IL17).
- **Mechanism** — A `domainGrade` alongside `authoringGrade`: a per-probe rubric of required domain tokens (authorities, lead times, artefact names, canonical columns) checked mechanically, plus the existing structural grade. The corpus's own header concedes that "specificity and taste — could these titles have been written without knowing the domain? — are left to a person"; for a domain with published standards, much of it can be checked by machine. Add a fifteenth probe asserting an **artefact** — "make tomorrow's call sheet" with the required-field list as the rubric — and one Arabic film probe (DF35). **This is the item that makes every other item in this corner falsifiable.**
- **Hard clause** — **probe.** WAVE 0.

## T2 — collaborator

#### DF10 · Labels carry no colour, so the breakdown category system cannot be expressed at all
- **Evidence** — `tools.go:289-296` — `create_label`'s entire schema is `{"name": "Short label name, 24 characters or fewer."}`. No colour field. The breakdown system is **defined by** colour (props violet, extras green, stunts orange, SFX blue); DF5's twenty categories become twenty identical grey chips.
- **Failure** — Cross-cutting attributes are exactly what labels are for and exactly what a breakdown is. A card can have one background colour (its strip colour, DF9) and many labels (its breakdown elements) — the two systems are orthogonal in the trade and the product supports only one of them chromatically. DA26 notes labels have no rename/merge/retire and that `usageCount` is never read; it never notices there is no colour, which is the property the domain actually needs.
- **Mechanism** — Optional `color` on `create_label` drawn from the same `swatchNames()` enum (server resolves the name, identical pattern to cards); the digest renders `LABEL l3 "Props" (violet)`. Then the twenty categories seed with their traditional colours. Small schema + compiler change; the label chip already renders a colour in the picker, so this closes a write-without-read asymmetry of exactly the kind the thesis names.
- **Hard clause** — **compiler.**

#### DF11 · Day Out of Days — a matrix with a seven-token status alphabet, and the product has no matrix
- **Evidence** — Codes: SW = Start Work, W = Work, WF = Work Finish, SWF = Start-Work-Finish, H = Hold (not used, still paid), T = Travel, R = Rehearsal. Shape: cast down, shooting days across, a code per cell, totals per performer. Product side: `create_table` takes `rows` as arrays of strings, so a DOOD is expressible; but `tableDigest` caps at 5 columns, so a 20-day DOOD is 25% visible on re-read (DF14).
- **Failure** — DOOD is where scheduling meets money: it is what tells you an actor is being paid for eleven hold days because two of their scenes sit at opposite ends of the schedule. It is the report that justifies reordering the whole shoot. Its value is entirely in the arithmetic (I2's `compute`) and its shape is entirely a matrix — so it is the cleanest test of whether the agent can hold a two-dimensional production fact, and nothing in any prior document contemplates a matrix.
- **Mechanism** — DOOD as a first-class artefact spec: rows = cast, columns = shoot days, cells from the code alphabet, plus a derived totals column **the server computes** (I2's `compute` extended with a DOOD reducer — the server states "Layla: 4 work, 6 hold", the model interprets). Requires DF14. The reordering half — "cut Layla's hold days" → propose a schedule permutation — is DF21.
- **Hard clause** — **digest**, via DF14.

#### DF12 · Shooting order is not story order, and every ordering primitive the agent has assumes it is 🔴 *(corrects I3)*
- **Evidence** — `planner.go:273-274` "'Order these by date / priority / dependency' — stage the moves in that order; cards land in the order you stage them." `arrange()` offers grid/row/column/tidy/tree/flow. I3 proposes `arrange(ids,"timeline")` ordered by "the elements' own dates". Against: a shooting schedule is grouped by **location**, then by company move, then by day/night, then by cast availability — story order is the one order it is never in.
- **Failure** — A direct correction. I3 says "the film-production user asks for this constantly in disguise: shoot schedules ARE timelines" — they are not. A timeline lays items along their dates; a stripboard **assigns** dates by solving a constraint problem, and the dates are the **output**. Building I3 as specified gives this user a feature that looks right and answers the wrong question: it would arrange scenes 1..64 along an axis, which is the script. The distinction between story order and shooting order is the most basic fact of production scheduling, and the horizon document inverts it.
- **Mechanism** — Two capabilities, named differently. `arrange("timeline")` stays as I3 specified for genuinely dated material (deadlines, festival dates, delivery milestones). Scheduling is separate: `regroup(ids, by="location"|"dayNight"|"cast"|"set")` over scene-shaped material, producing day banners and strip order with the reasoning stated ("grouped by location to remove two company moves").
