- **Evidence** — `boardstate.go:63` `if active, err := s.runs.ActiveByBoard(ctx, boardID)` — where `boardID` is whatever board the client currently has open. `AgentObserving.tsx:21` `if (!active || active.mine) return null`. `ActiveByBoard` is keyed on `task.rootBoardId` while the run's blast radius is the whole subtree after W1.
- **Failure** — Deepens MP5 with the surface MP5's own mechanism does not reach. MP5 fixes the **lock** (one run per overlapping subtree) — but the observing pill is a separate read, and widening the lock does not make `BoardStateFor(nestedBoard)` return the parent's run. So the journey stays broken: the producer opens `Pre-Production` to review the schedule while the director runs the agent on the project root; thirty elements rearrange under the producer's cursor with no pill, no name, no count and no explanation. `AgentObserving`'s own header states exactly why it exists — "until now nothing said so: the run's journal was broadcast to everyone and consumed by nobody, so cards simply moved" — and after W1 that condition is restored for every board except the run's root, which is the board the agent's work has moved **away** from. This is the half of MP16 that is cheap and shippable today.
- **Mechanism** — Rides MP5's `ActiveOverlapping(boardID, ancestorIDs)` directly: `BoardStateFor` asks the same question the lock does, so the pill lights on any board inside a running subtree and names the board the run is rooted at — "Sara's assistant is working on Pre-Production, which contains this board." One call site, and it makes MP5's fix **visible** instead of merely correct. Second clause, nearly free: `agent.event` frames ride the ROOT board's room, so a viewer inside a nested board is not in that room and receives nothing — the broadcast must fan out to the subtree's rooms, which is the same multi-room broadcast DA2 and DA14 both need.
- **Hard clause** — **handler**, shared with MP5. Probe: a two-client test asserting the nested viewer's pill lights.

#### JN19 · The handoff artifact truncates every document at 500 characters and its images are dead links *(deepens PL11)*
- **Evidence** — `board_service.go:266-268` — `case TypeCard, TypeDocument: if txt, ok := el.Content["textPreview"].(string); ok && txt != "" { fmt.Fprintf(b, "%s\n\n", txt) }` — the 500-char preview (JN5), with no ellipsis and no marker. Images: `:291-294` emits `![title](url)` where `url` is the attachment's `PublicURL` — a storage URL the recipient of the .md file cannot fetch (and which expires in 7 days — DL3). The only entry point is an unlabelled icon disabled on Home, and no export verb exists in the agent's 49-tool registry.
- **Failure** — PL11 correctly identifies the **dropped types** (TABLE, SKETCH, ALIAS, CLONE, COMMENT_THREAD, LINE) and the geometric linearisation. The journey adds two failures PL11 does not reach and which are arguably worse, because they affect the types that DO export. **Truncation:** the deliverable of journey 6 for a filmmaker is the treatment, the brief, the schedule notes — all DOCUMENTs — and every one arrives as its first 500 characters with nothing indicating loss; PL11's fix leaves this untouched. **Assets:** a mood board exported to markdown is a list of dead links, so the one board type this product is best at is the one that survives export least. And the agent — which could compose a real one-page brief FROM the board — has no way to reach the export at all.
- **Mechanism** — Three clauses on top of PL11's. (1) Export reads the real text via JN5's `plainTextOf(doc)` walker — same call site, one line, and the change that makes PL11's other fixes worth having. (2) Assets: `format=zip` bundles the markdown with referenced attachments rewritten to relative paths; for the bare markdown, images render with a bracketed filename so a dead link at least names what is missing — **silence and absence must not be the same output**, PL11's own rule applied to the asset layer. (3) PL11's `export_board(format)` READ tool, whose journey value is specifically that `write_document` can then author a genuine brief FROM the export inside the staged-write discipline.
- **Hard clause** — **compiler**, with JN5's shared-walker constraint: export, search and digest must read one implementation or they will disagree about what a document says.

---

# Corner FR — Failure, recovery, and the states you cannot get out of

*Method: the run state machine, admission/execute/commit/steer/retry/continue,
revert and cancel, the tool loop's error arms, the provider plane, the
reconciler, the write path's atomicity, the HTTP error boundary, and every
frontend agent surface. Nothing here restates DA20 (fingerprint granularity),
DA21 (idempotency), MP15 (stale revert), MP17 (proposal lock), LP7 (steering),
LP10 (inbox), PL5 (`/readyz`), PL6 (spend) or PL12 (offline) — where the same
code is touched, the item says what those missed and gives new evidence.*

*The meta-question this corner was sent to answer — **are there states a person
cannot get out of?** — has an answer, and it is yes. FR22 states it.*

## T1 — parity and live wrongs

#### FR1 · The boot reconciler runs on a 5-minute ticker and kills every live run it finds — with a message that says the server restarted 🔴 *(two territories: FR and SC)*
- **Evidence** — `cli/serve.go:155` `tick := time.NewTicker(5 * time.Minute)` → `:162 agentSvc.Reconcile(ctx)` (re-verified). `agent/reads.go:51-86`: PROPOSED gets the expiry branch; **everything else** falls to `:84-85` `finishWithReason(ctx, run, StateFailed, "the server restarted while this run was in progress")`. `repository/mongo/agent.go:101-109 Unfinished` = `Find({"active": true})` — no age filter, no lease, no owner-process filter, and the Run struct carries no liveness field. Run deadline is **8 minutes** (`contracts.go:221`).
- **Failure** — This is not a crash path — it is the normal path. A run is sized for ~8 minutes of wall clock; the sweep fires every 5. **Any run still working when a tick lands is force-failed**, and a run started at a uniform point in the cycle has a mean 2.5 minutes to live. It directly contradicts the reasoning written into `contracts.go:216-221` — "The deadline must scale with MaxSteps or it becomes the real budget: at 3 minutes, a 24-step run over a nested-scope digest was killed by the clock long before the step budget it was nominally allowed." The deadline was raised 3→8 minutes so `MaxSteps` could bind; the real budget is neither, it is the interval to the next tick, and any run needing more than 5 minutes — exactly the big-workspace runs W4's starvation fix was built for — is killed with probability 1. The kill **succeeds**: `Update` is optimistic on `rev`, and between `transition(StateRunning)` and the planner's return the run document is never written (emit appends to the events collection), so Rev is stable for the entire tool loop and the reconciler's write matches. The run goroutine then finishes, fails the rev check twice, and logs "could not terminate run". The user paid for the tokens, watched the plan form live over the journal, and is told the server restarted. Nothing restarted. Three prior documents treat `Reconcile` as the crash-recovery story and one shipped test covers only the PROPOSED arm — **nothing anywhere asserts that a RUNNING run survives a sweep.**
- **Mechanism** — A run needs a **liveness lease**, not a boot-time heuristic. `Run.LeaseUntil` + `Run.Host`, stamped at `execute` entry and renewed at each turn boundary — the loop is already turn-structured, so the renewal is one line beside the existing steer drain, and that same write gives PL6 its mid-run spend checkpoint (SC15), DA22's subscriber bus a producer, and IL9 somewhere to persist the model. `Unfinished` filters to `active AND (leaseUntil == nil OR leaseUntil < now)`, so only genuinely abandoned runs are reclaimed — which is also the only version correct with two replicas, today's silent assumption. The reason string branches: an expired lease says "the server stopped while this run was working — nothing was written".
- **Hard clause** — **handler.** Probe: a scripted run held mid-loop across two `Reconcile` calls is still RUNNING after both.

#### FR2 · Stop does not stop — Cancel writes a state nothing reads; the run keeps spending, and its spend is never recorded 🔴
- **Evidence** — `revert.go:24-34 Cancel` calls only `finishWithReason(StateCancelled)`. The goroutine's context is independent: `service.go:237` `go s.execute(context.Background(), …)`, `:323` `ctx, cancel := context.WithTimeout(ctx, run.Task.Budget.Deadline)`. The loop's only exits are `planner.go:463 ctx.Err()` and `:466` the cost cap — **no read of run state**, no cancel channel; `drainSteers` reads Steers and ignores State. Frontend: `agentStore.ts:276-283 cancel()` calls the API and then sets `{run: null, events: [], adjustments: []}` unconditionally.
- **Failure** — The one control a person has while watching a run go wrong is a placebo. They press Stop, the dock goes idle, and the model keeps working for up to eight more minutes on their money. Worse, **the spend is lost**: when the goroutine finally tries to terminate, `finishWithReason` → `transition` → `s.runs.Update(ctx, run, run.Rev)` fails on the stale rev, the fallback branch fails on the same rev, and `run.Usage` — accumulated at `:354` — is never persisted. `spentToday` sums `r.Usage.CostUSD`, so **every cancelled run costs the tenant real money the daily cap cannot see** (compounding SC15). And because Cancel sets `Active=false` the board slot frees instantly, so the person can start a second run that writes the same board concurrently with the first. `contracts.go:353-357` says Stop "threw away the tokens along with the work" as the motivation for steering — it is worse: Stop throws away the work and keeps paying for the tokens.
- **Mechanism** — Cancellation must be a signal the loop can see. `Service` holds `cancels map[runID]context.CancelFunc` populated at `execute` entry and cleared in its defer; `Cancel` calls it before writing the terminal state, aborting the provider call at the transport layer. Belt and braces for the multi-replica case: the loop checks `s.runs.Get(runID).State.Terminal()` at the same turn boundary that already drains steers — one read per turn. On cancellation the accumulated Usage is persisted through a rev-refetching write, and the plan staged so far is **preserved and offered** exactly as the cost-cap path preserves it (FR7). Frontend: `cancel()` awaits the returned run so the outcome card renders CANCELLED with the spend — "Stopped after 3 steps · $0.09".
- **Hard clause** — **handler.**

#### FR3 · Stop pressed during an apply reports "Nothing was changed" over a board that was just rewritten, and the run is then unrevertible 🔴
- **Evidence** — `contracts.go:88` makes `StateApplying → StateCancelled` legal. `agentStore.ts:139` `WORKING` includes 'APPLYING' and 'VERIFYING', and `AgentBar.tsx:51` renders `<Working/>` for all of them — `AgentWorking.tsx:58` is the Stop button. Meanwhile `commit` (`service.go:447-602`) runs to completion regardless (FR2): `ApplyWithMeta` at `:564`, `run.TransactionIDs` appended at `:575`, then `transition(StateVerifying)` at `:579` — an **illegal edge from CANCELLED**, so it returns and `TransactionIDs` is never persisted. `i18n:199` `'agent.out.cancelledDetail': 'Nothing was changed'` (ar `:447` 'لم يتغيّر شيء'). `revert.go:45`/`:85` both require COMPLETED or PARTIAL.
- **Failure** — A person under AutonomyAuto — or anyone who presses Stop in the second the state flips from RUNNING to APPLYING — is told **in two languages** that their board is untouched while forty elements land on it. The run then has no transaction id recorded against it, so per-run revert, `RevertOne` and the audit view can none of them see the work. The only recovery left is Ctrl+Z, which dies on reload (FR22). The most direct contradiction of the product's central promise — "still one undo" — and it is reachable by pressing the one button the UI offers during a wait.
- **Mechanism** — Two clauses. (a) Once a commit has begun the run is no longer stoppable: `Cancel` on APPLYING/VERIFYING refuses with "this is already being written — you can undo it when it lands", and `AgentWorking` swaps Stop for a disabled "Writing…". The write path is already all-or-nothing in intent; making the UI agree costs one condition. (b) Independently, `TransactionIDs` must be persisted by its **own** write immediately after `ApplyWithMeta` returns, never as a side effect of a state transition (FR6).
- **Hard clause** — **handler.**

#### FR4 · A run that fails AFTER committing can never be reverted, and the outcome copy tells the person the board was restored 🔴
- **Evidence** — `contracts.go:90-91` — only `StateCompleted` and `StatePartial` have an edge to `StateReverted`; FAILED, CANCELLED and BUDGET_EXHAUSTED have none, and `revert.go:45`/`:85` enforce the same. Three commit paths reach FAILED with work already on the board: `service.go:569-572` (`ApplyWithMeta` errored after applying ops — FR5), `:589-598` (postconditions failed), and the expired-grant path (FR12). `:592-597` logs a failed auto-revert and then calls `finishWithReason(…, StateFailed, "the result did not verify; the board was restored")` **unconditionally** — the sentence does not branch on the revert's error. Frontend `AgentDone.tsx:14` `revertible = state === 'COMPLETED' || state === 'PARTIAL'`; `i18n:203` `'agent.out.failedDetail': 'The board was left as it was'` (ar `:449`).
- **Failure** — The sharpest version of the meta-question. **The state machine models failure as "nothing happened", and three real paths produce failure with things having happened.** The person is shown a card asserting, in their own language, that the board is untouched; there is no Undo button, the API refuses Revert with a bare conflict, and the audit view skips the run entirely. They are left to notice the damage by eye and repair it by hand. MP15 audits whether revert is *safe*; nothing in the three documents notices that for a whole class of runs it is not *offered*.
- **Mechanism** — Invariant: **any run that ever held a transaction id is revertible, regardless of why it stopped.** (a) Add `StateFailed → StateReverted` and `StateCancelled → StateReverted` to `allowedTransitions`, and change revert's guard from a state whitelist to `len(run.TransactionIDs) > 0 && !allReverted(run)`. (b) The postcondition message becomes conditional on the auto-revert's error: success → "the result did not verify; I put the board back"; failure → "the result did not verify and I could not put the board back — N changes are still standing", plus an Undo button that retries the same inverse. (c) `outcomeText`'s FAILED arm gains a `wrote` flag so the default detail stops claiming nothing changed.
- **Hard clause** — **schema** (the transition table). Probe: a scripted run whose postconditions fail and whose revert also fails renders an enabled Undo and never renders the word "restored".

#### FR5 · The write path applies ops one at a time with no rollback, so a mid-loop failure leaves elements with no transaction row — and therefore no inverse anywhere in the system 🔴
- **Evidence** — `service/transaction_service.go:126-134` — `for i := range ops { if err := s.applyOp(...); err != nil { return nil, err } }` (re-verified). The journal row is inserted only afterwards, at `:159`. The comment at `:96-100` claims the pre-validation loop is "a partial-write guard (a transaction is all-or-nothing in intent, so we never apply op 1 then fail on op 2)" — but pre-validation covers only `verifyOpScope` and `verifyDelegation`; it cannot pre-empt a duplicate key, a validation error inside `applyCreate`, or a Mongo failure. There is no session wrapper and no compensating loop.
- **Failure** — DA21 names the retried-commit case and prescribes journal-then-write. What it does not say is what the orphan looks like **to a person**: the elements exist, `run.TransactionIDs` is empty, no `transaction.applied` frame was broadcast (the broadcaster runs at `:162`, after the insert), so the initiating client's undo stack never receives the ops either. Per-run revert cannot reach it, Ctrl+Z cannot reach it, the audit view cannot see it, and the outcome card says "the changes were rejected" — the exact opposite of what happened. **This is the one place in the architecture where the "one transaction, one revert" guarantee is not merely stale but absent, and the code comment that would reassure a reader is the thing that hides it.**
- **Mechanism** — Three ordered clauses, cheapest first. (a) Insert the journal row in a `pending` state **before** the op loop (DA21's ordering) so the inverse always exists on disk even for a half-applied batch — the row is the recovery handle, and it is what makes (b) possible at all. (b) On any `applyOp` error, replay `InvertOps` over the ops already applied **in this call** before returning, and stamp the row `rolled_back` with the count — a compensating write, not a database transaction, so it needs no replica-set requirement (though SC12 argues for one anyway). (c) If the compensation fails, the row stays `pending` and the reconciler completes it; the run reports "N of M changes landed and could not be undone automatically — here they are" with a working Undo, composing with FR4.
- **Hard clause** — **handler.** The largest item in this corner and the one everything else leans on.

#### FR6 · The run's only link to the transaction it just committed is persisted as a side effect of a state transition that is allowed to fail 🔴
- **Evidence** — `service.go:575` `run.TransactionIDs = append(...)` — an in-memory mutation. The first write that persists it is `:579 s.transition(ctx, run, StateVerifying, "")`, and `transition` (`:896-927`) writes with optimistic concurrency: on failure it **rolls the in-memory State back** and returns the error, which `commit` propagates at `:580` without retry and without terminating the run.
- **Failure** — Any concurrent write to the run row between admission and the VERIFYING transition — a Steer landing at that instant, a Refine, a Cancel, a Reconcile sweep (FR1) — makes the rev stale, and **the committed transaction is orphaned from the run forever.** The run is then left non-terminal in APPLYING holding the board's slot, its work invisible to Revert, `RevertOne` and Audit. The same shape recurs at `:467-469` (`scope.Hydrate` error) and `:475-477` (`CheckFingerprint` error), which both `return nil, err` from APPLYING with **no `finishWithReason` at all** — those two branches wedge the run by construction, and the only thing that eventually frees the board is the reconciler, whose message will then be FR1's fiction.
- **Mechanism** — Separate the commit record from the state machine. Immediately after `ApplyWithMeta` returns, write the transaction id with a dedicated rev-refetching helper (`Get`, append, `Update`, retry once on conflict) — **durability of the revert handle must not depend on winning a rev race.** Then every early `return` inside `commit` goes through `finishWithReason`, so no path can exit APPLYING without reaching a terminal state.
- **Hard clause** — **probe.** A table-driven test over `commit`'s error branches asserting `run.State.Terminal()` in every case is what keeps the next branch from forgetting.

#### FR7 · Every hard failure throws away the plan the person already paid for; only the cost cap preserves it 🔴
- **Evidence** — `planner.go:463-465` `if err := ctx.Err(); err != nil { return nil, usage, err }` and `:502-504` (the provider call) both discard `stage.plan` with all N staged actions. Contrast `:466-470`: the cost cap `break`s out of the loop, **keeping** the plan, and `:632-634` then calls `discloseExhaustion` (`exhaustion.go:29-70`) which writes an honest summary and an unmet list. `cognition/resilient.go:11-15` states the problem verbatim — "Losing turn five to a 502 throws away every staged action AND the money already spent producing them — the user waits, pays, and gets nothing" — and solves only the ≤3-attempt case.
- **Failure** — The asymmetry is invisible and expensive. A run cut by the cost cap at step 18 returns eighteen steps of work with a truthful "this is a prefix" note and a Continue button. The identical run cut by the 8-minute deadline, or by a fourth consecutive 503, returns **nothing** — the person waited eight minutes, was charged up to $0.35, and gets a red card. The entire honest-exhaustion machinery W4 built, and the Continue affordance M2 built on top of it, are wired only to the two budgets that stop gracefully; the two that stop violently bypass both. It is also why FR14 bites: with no plan there is no unmet, so there is nothing to continue from and nothing for the next run's history to read.
- **Mechanism** — The loop's exit arms converge. Replace both `return nil, usage, err` sites with the same `break` the cost cap uses, recording a `stoppedBecause` enum (`cost | steps | deadline | provider`). After the loop, `discloseExhaustion` takes that enum and says the true thing per case — "the AI service stopped responding after step 9; what is here is a prefix" — and the planner returns `(plan, usage, err)` rather than `(nil, usage, err)`. `service.execute` keeps the plan on the run before calling `finishWithReason`, so a provider outage produces a **reviewable partial plan with a Continue button** instead of a void. The only case that must still return nil is zero staged actions.
- **Hard clause** — **handler.** Probe: a scripted provider erroring on turn 5 after four staged actions yields a run whose plan holds those four and whose summary names the provider.

#### FR8 · A hung provider and a deadline are the same event and are reported as two different, both-misleading outcomes 🔴
- **Evidence** — `service.go:989-998 terminalFor`: a bare `context.DeadlineExceeded` → `StateExhausted`, reason "the run ran out of time"; `cognition.ErrUnavailable` falls to `default` → `StateFailed`, "the AI service is unavailable right now". But `cognition/gemini.go:278` and `:283` wrap transport errors as `fmt.Errorf("%w: %v", ErrUnavailable, err)` — **`%v`, not `%w`** — so the context error is stringified and `errors.Is(err, context.DeadlineExceeded)` is false. Timing makes the collision routine: `gemini.go:48` sets a 3-minute HTTP client timeout and `resilient.go` retries 3× per provider, so **a single hung call can consume 9 minutes against an 8-minute run deadline**. Frontend renders BUDGET_EXHAUSTED as tone 'warn', title "Ran out of room".
- **Failure** — Whether a provider outage reads as "Ran out of room" or "The AI service is unavailable" depends on which side of a turn boundary the clock crossed. The first sends the person to raise a limit that was never reached — the exact error `exhaustion.go:29-31`'s own comment warns about ("naming the wrong one sends somebody to raise a limit that was never reached") — from a code path that comment never covered. The second is right by accident. PL5 fixes the readiness probe so the deployment knows; nothing makes the **run** know.
- **Mechanism** — Two corrections and one budget rule. (a) The providers wrap with `%w` on both sides so the cause chain survives; `terminalFor` then tests the chain in the right order — deadline first, unavailable second. (b) A new terminal reason distinct from both: when the deadline fires while a provider call is outstanding, the run reports "the AI service stopped responding" rather than a budget. (c) **The per-call HTTP timeout must be a fraction of the run deadline, not larger than a third of it** — derive it from `Budget.Deadline / maxSteps * 3` at construction, so raising MaxSteps can never make one call able to eat the whole run.
- **Hard clause** — **handler.**

#### FR9 · Three of the nine terminal states are unreachable dead code — with full bilingual copy and dedicated UI branches 🔴
- **Evidence** — `grep -rn 'StateDenied|StateQuarantine' backend/internal/agent --include=*.go` outside tests returns **only** `contracts.go:54-56`, `:63-64` and the transition table `:76-78` — **nothing ever transitions to either.** `StatePartial` is set at exactly one site (`service.go:374`, the "answered without changing anything" path) and by `terminalFor`. `ErrDestructiveNeedsReview` (`contracts.go:458`) is declared and referenced nowhere. Yet `i18n:204-205` and `:254-255` (ar `:451-452`, `:495-496`) ship copy for DENIED and SECURITY_QUARANTINED, `agentStore.ts:657-662` branches on both, and `AgentDone.tsx:18-19` lists DENIED as retryable.
- **Failure** — The failure vocabulary the design intended — denied authority, security halt, budget, partial — was built at **every layer except the one that assigns it**. In practice a provider outage, an 8-minute deadline, a malformed tool call, a precondition rejection, an expired grant, a rejected write and a half-committed transaction all arrive at the person as the identical card: a red dot, "Could not finish", and one server sentence. A person cannot learn which failures are theirs to act on and which are the system's, so they learn that the agent breaks at random — which is precisely the lesson `agentStore.ts:630-635`'s own comment says the FAILED/DENIED split was introduced to prevent. The split was introduced in the renderer and never in the producer.
- **Mechanism** — Assign the states that already exist before inventing more. `StateDenied` on any `ErrForbidden` out of `ApplyWithMeta` or the access resolver (which is what an expired grant and a delegation refusal are — FR12). `StateQuarantine` when `plan.Quarantined` and the person discards, or immediately when quarantine fires under AutonomyAuto, so the security halt has its own terminal record rather than hiding inside a PROPOSED plan (FR10) — and IL24 finally has something to count. Wire `ErrDestructiveNeedsReview` at the one site `verify.go:46` already detects.
- **Hard clause** — **probe.** A property test over `terminalFor` and every `finishWithReason` call site asserting that each terminal state in `allowedTransitions` has at least one producer — the same table-completeness discipline the transition machine already claims.

#### FR10 · A quarantined plan is presented with an amber sentence and a fully live Apply button, including the keyboard shortcut 🔴
- **Evidence** — `service.go:392-395` — quarantine suppresses only the AUTO-apply branch (`if run.Task.Autonomy == AutonomyAuto && !plan.Quarantined`); the run then falls through to `StateProposed` at `:404` like any other. `AgentDecide.tsx:86` renders `{plan.quarantined && <div className="ac-warn">…}` and **nothing else changes**: the Apply button is unstyled and unguarded, and `useAgentShell.ts:37` keeps `Ctrl/⌘+Enter` bound to `s.apply()` for any PROPOSED run. `planner.go:576-583` sets the flag only after `stage.quotas.lifted >= injectionHaltThreshold`.
- **Failure** — The one plan in the system the harness has positively identified as **steered by hostile board content** is offered with the same one-keystroke commit as a plan it trusts. The i18n copy is careful and correct — "check this plan before applying it" — and then the UI does nothing to make checking more likely than applying. LP3 argues review degrades under volume; this is the case where review has been explicitly flagged as load-bearing and the affordances were left identical. It compounds LP5: quarantine is exactly the peer/guest-authored-content attack, and the person's defence is a sentence they can dismiss with a reflex.
- **Mechanism** — Quarantine changes the **shape** of the decision, not just its colour. The Apply button renders secondary and requires the review list to have been opened (`details === true`) before it enables — the "you must look at it" gate the destructive banner also deserves; the Ctrl+Enter binding is suppressed; and the card leads with the **quoted offending text** rather than a paraphrase, because a quote is the only thing that makes an injection legible. Pair with FR9: discarding a quarantined plan lands in `StateQuarantine` so the security event has a terminal record.
- **Hard clause** — **handler.**

#### FR11 · A stale plan is permanently unappliable, the UI keeps offering Apply, and the list of what changed is computed and never shown 🔴 *(deepens DA20)*
- **Evidence** — `service.go:458-462` (membership) and `:474-483` (per-element fingerprint) both `_ = s.transition(ctx, run, StateProposed, "")` and return `ErrStalePlan`. **Nothing recomputes `run.Plan.Fingerprint`** — `:486` explicitly re-pins the old one — so the second Apply fails identically, and the third. `CheckFingerprint` returns a `stale []string` that `:481` puts into the event payload as `staleElements`; **no frontend component reads run events** (`AgentDecide.tsx` renders only `run.plan`). `agentStore.ts:234-239`: on 409 it toasts "The board changed while you were reviewing." and resyncs, which returns the same unappliable plan.
- **Failure** — DA20 supplies the mechanism (per-destination membership, drop-stale-keep-rest) and stops at the write path. The recovery **experience** is untouched by it and is the part a person meets: an Apply button that is enabled, that they have already paid for, that will fail every time they press it, and a message that names no element, no colleague and no next step. The only two exits are Discard (throw the paid plan away) and Refine (pay for a whole new run) and neither is suggested. On a shared board — where DA20 notes the hash covers the entire subtree after W1 — this is the **normal** outcome of reviewing for more than a few seconds.
- **Mechanism** — Staleness becomes a rendered state, not a toast. (a) The stale-id list is stored on the run, not only broadcast, and `Decide` renders it above the buttons in the digest's own voice: "3 items changed while you were reviewing — Sara edited 'Casting'". (b) Apply is disabled and replaced by two honest buttons: **Re-check** (free — recompute membership and fingerprints against current state and re-enable if the drift no longer touches the plan) and **Apply the rest** (DA20's per-action rebase, dropping stale actions into `unmet`). (c) Stale rows are struck through in the review list. **Nothing here needs a model call, which is what makes it the cheap half of DA20 and shippable before it.**
- **Hard clause** — **digest** — the read that makes the state legible.

#### FR12 · The delegation expires at 30 minutes and the proposal lives for 2 hours, so for 90 minutes the product shows an Apply button guaranteed to fail — and the code comment says so 🔴 *(two territories: FR and JN)*
- **Evidence** — `service.go:218` `ExpiresAt: now.Add(30 * time.Minute)`; `:835` `const proposalLifetime = 2 * time.Hour` (both re-verified). `transaction_service.go:83-85` `if d.Expired(now) { return nil, domain.ErrForbidden }` — reached only **after** `commit` has already run scope compile, membership, fingerprint, preconditions, op compilation **and inserted the run's new labels and comment bodies** (`service.go:509-545`). The failure then lands at `:569-572` as `StateFailed, "the changes were rejected"`. And `contracts.go:368-370` documents the mismatch **in prose, as a known defect**: "It also outlived its own authority: the delegation expires after 30 minutes, so a proposal older than that could not have been applied even if someone had found it."
- **Failure** — A known, written-down defect that shipped, and the most likely way a real working day destroys a real plan. Preview a plan before lunch, apply it after, and you get a red card reading "the changes were rejected" — no mention of expiry, no countdown beforehand, no way to re-authorise, and a run in FAILED which (FR4) cannot be reverted and (FR13) has already dirtied your label taxonomy on the way to failing. The only exit `AgentDone` offers is Retry, which pays for the entire run again from the bare intent and loses every refinement (FR15). MP17 examines `proposalLifetime` purely as a board-lock question and never notices that its value is **four times its own authority**; the 2-hour sweep it calls the mitigation does nothing for the 90 minutes in between.
- **Mechanism** — Authority and offer must expire together, and the offer must be able to renew. (a) `ProposalExpiresAt` is **derived** from the delegation, or — stricter — the grant is re-minted on every transition into PROPOSED, at the single place that already stamps the proposal deadline, so the two clocks cannot diverge by construction and Refine inherits correctly with no extra code. (b) The decide card renders the deadline once it is inside the last quarter, reusing the `humanAge` helper the busy-board refusal already has. (c) **Apply on an expired grant does not fail: it re-mints** from the human's live `RequireEdit`, which `service.go:437` already re-checks on every Apply — because the person is present and authorised at that moment, and the grant's purpose is to bound the **model**, which is no longer in the loop. Only if `RequireEdit` now fails does the run terminate, and then as `StateDenied` (FR9) with the honest reason.
- **Hard clause** — **handler.** Probe: applying a 45-minute-old proposal succeeds; applying one whose board access was revoked lands in DENIED naming the permission.

#### FR13 · A failed apply leaves behind the labels and comment bodies it inserted first — the exact leak the code claims to prevent 🔴
- **Evidence** — `service.go:509-525` inserts every `effective.NewLabels` and `:530-545` every `effective.NewComments` **before** `ApplyWithMeta` at `:564`. The comment at `:506-508` states the intent: "They are inserted BEFORE the ops, because the ops reference their ids — and only here, so a discarded or reverted preview never leaves a stray tag in the user's taxonomy." Every failure after that point — expired grant (FR12), delegation refusal, op failure (FR5), journal insert conflict (DA21) — returns at `:569-572` with labels and comments already written and **no cleanup anywhere**. `InvertOps` operates on `txn.Ops` only, so even a successful revert does not remove them.
- **Failure** — The reasoning that placed the insert here is right — the ops carry the label ids — and the conclusion drawn from it is wrong: "only here" protects against discard and revert, and **not against failure**, which is the case where cleanup matters most because the person is already confused. The residue is invisible and cumulative: a label that appears in the filter bar and on nothing, and a comment document with no thread element to anchor it, one set per failed apply. It also silently defeats the guidance the agent is given to reuse rather than coin labels — the taxonomy fills with orphans from runs that never landed.
- **Mechanism** — Make the pre-writes compensable. Track the ids inserted in this call; on any error before the journal row is durable, delete them in a deferred cleanup (labels by id with a `usageCount == 0` guard, comments by id) and journal one `EvError` naming what was rolled back. Better long-term and cheap once FR5's pending journal row exists: fold new-label creation into the same pending transaction so it is covered by the same compensation — which is also what DA13 needs when `applyCreate` starts accepting `labelIds`.
- **Hard clause** — **handler.**

#### FR14 · Recovery is wired only to graceful endings: the failure classes that most need memory are the ones excluded from it 🔴
- **Evidence** — `service.go:1115-1128 attachHistory` switches on run state and ends with `default: continue // in flight, or it failed for reasons that teach nothing` — dropping FAILED, CANCELLED and BUDGET_EXHAUSTED. `AgentDone.tsx:30` `continuable = state === 'COMPLETED' && unmet.length > 0`, and `service.go:787 Continue` requires `prior.Plan != nil && len(prior.Plan.Unmet) > 0`. Per FR7 the deadline and provider paths return **no plan at all**, so those runs have no unmet by construction.
- **Failure** — "It failed for reasons that teach nothing" is true of a validation refusal and false of a half-commit. A run that wrote sixteen elements and then failed (FR5), or one force-failed by the reconciler mid-write (FR1), is exactly the run whose successor must be told what is already standing — and it is precisely the one the digest hides. So the person's own recovery button, Retry, starts context-free and rebuilds the structure beside the partial one: **W6 and G2's duplicate-structure failure, arriving through the failure door instead of the exhaustion door, on a board already in a worse state.** MP7 found this hole along the tenancy axis; the outcome axis is untouched.
- **Mechanism** — History follows **effects**, not verdicts. `attachHistory`'s switch gains an arm for any run with a non-empty `TransactionIDs` regardless of state: "an hour ago they asked X → it stopped partway and left N changes standing: <the first few>" — derivable from `Plan.Actions` and the journal, both already loaded, by the same flattening `priorUnmet` performs. BUDGET_EXHAUSTED and CANCELLED render their partial plan the same way once FR7 preserves it. `Continue` widens from COMPLETED-with-unmet to "any terminal run with unmet **or** with standing effects and an unfinished intent".
- **Hard clause** — **digest.** Probe: seed a half-committed failed run, then "finish it" → fills the standing containers and creates no duplicates (the G2 assertion, one door over).

#### FR16 · There is no watchdog on the client: a run that has stopped emitting is indistinguishable from one that is thinking 🔴
- **Evidence** — `grep -rn 'setInterval|setTimeout|elapsed|Date.now' frontend/src/agent/` returns **exactly one** hit, an autofocus timer. `AgentWorking.tsx` renders the last event's message plus a CSS pulse and nothing else — no elapsed time, no step position, no cost. The server computes the step position (`stepSummary`, `planner.go:736-745`, "step 9 — create_column, move_element") and the client **discards it** into `phraseKeyFor`, which maps the whole message down to one of six generic phrases (AX38). The run's live spend exists on the run object and is rendered only on the Decide and Done cards. The only liveness source is the socket.
- **Failure** — An 8-minute deadline behind a spinner that says "Thinking…" is indistinguishable from a wedged run, a dead socket, or a provider hanging on a 3-minute timeout (FR8). The person has no basis to decide whether to wait or to stop — and, because the cost meter is hidden during exactly the window in which the money is being spent, no basis to decide whether stopping is worth it. IN12 argues for streaming ghosts and LP7 for a steering queue; **both presume the person can tell that the run is still alive.** Nothing today tells them, and the server is already emitting the facts that would.
- **Mechanism** — Pure composition over data already on the wire. (a) `Working` keeps `lastEventAt`; a 45-second gap swaps the pulse for "no word from the run for 45s" and offers Check (a `resync`) — a run already terminal is then adopted correctly instead of showing a phantom spinner. (b) Render the step position the server already sends ("step 9 of 24") and an elapsed timer against `Budget.Deadline`, so exhaustion is foreseeable rather than announced. (c) Emit the running `usage.costUsd` on the existing `step.finished` event data and show it beside the staged count — **the person watching a run approach its cost cap is the person best placed to steer or stop it, and today they are the only person who cannot see it.**
- **Hard clause** — none; composition. Composes with AX5's live regions, which need the same strings.

#### FR17 · Offline, the run freezes and Stop lies: the card clears while the run keeps going 🔴
- **Evidence** — `realtime/socket.ts:133-137` reconnects with backoff to 15s and resyncs an in-flight run only on a successful reopen. While disconnected nothing polls: `agentStore.ts:206-213 resync` swallows every error. `:276-283 cancel()` wraps the API call in `try { … } catch { /* idempotent */ }` and then sets `run: null` **outside** the try — so a Stop that never reached the server still clears the UI. On return, `loadBoardState` re-adopts the still-live run with no explanation of where it went.
- **Failure** — PL12 covers the offline **start** (a discarded typed intent, a cached board painting entry points that fail on submit) and the ops queue. It does not cover the in-flight run, which is the more alarming case for a filmmaker on location: the card freezes on its last known status forever, pressing Stop appears to work and does not, and the board silently rearranges itself minutes later when the connection returns — the exact "the board changed and nothing said why" failure `AgentObserving` was built to end, arriving through the network door.
- **Mechanism** — (a) `cancel()` and every other mutating agent call clear local state **only after** the request resolves; on network failure they surface "could not reach the server — the run may still be working" and leave the card in place. (b) A `navigator.onLine` + socket-state signal renders one line in the Working card so the frozen ticker has a stated cause (MO15). (c) While the socket is down and a run is in flight, poll `GET /agent/runs/:id` on the socket's own backoff schedule — the run object is already the complete answer, so this is a fetch, not a mechanism. Composes with PL8's `?wait=` long-poll if that lands.
- **Hard clause** — **handler** (client-side).

#### FR18 · A revert that fails partway loses the record of what it already undid, and the UI re-offers those rows 🔴
- **Evidence** — `revert.go:181-219 revertOps` accumulates `undone` across the run's transactions and, on any `ApplyWithMeta` error at `:206-208`, `return nil, err` — **discarding the set**. `:107` only appends to `run.RevertedElementIDs` after a fully successful call. The same shape in `revertTransactions` (`:249-266`). `AgentDone.tsx:145` builds its struck-through set from `run.revertedElementIds`, so rows already undone render live with a working ↩ button.
- **Failure** — Per-run revert is the product's central trust promise and its partial-failure mode is **silent forgetting**. The person presses Undo, sees an error toast, and the list still shows every row as standing — so they press the individual ↩ buttons on rows that have already been inverted, replaying deletes against deleted elements and restores against restored ones. MP15 asks whether revert should proceed when the board has moved; it does not ask what happens when revert itself half-completes — the same class of half-write as FR5 one layer up, on the path people reach for *because* something already went wrong.
- **Mechanism** — Record progress **before** returning the error. Both revert paths persist the ids undone so far — through the same rev-refetching write FR6 needs — and then return a partial result rather than a bare error. The card reports it honestly ("undid 18 of 24; 6 could not be undone") and leaves the remaining rows individually actionable, so a retry **resumes** rather than restarts. Probe: a scripted revert whose second transaction fails leaves `RevertedElementIDs` naming exactly the first transaction's elements, and those rows render struck through.
- **Hard clause** — **handler.**

#### FR19 · Revert is not the inverse of commit — the write path's post-commit side effects are outside the invertible set, while the copy promises the board is back as it was 🔴
- **Evidence** — `transaction_service.go:139` `s.rehomeConnectors(ctx, p, moved, now)` runs **after** the op loop and performs its own writes (moving or soft-deleting LINE elements, per W8's mechanism); those writes are never appended to `txn.Ops`, and `InvertOps(txn.Ops)` is the entire inverse. Same shape for `:165 fanOutCloneUpdates` and `:166 notifyAssignments`. `i18n:193` `'agent.out.revertedDetail': 'The board is back as it was'` (ar `:441`).
- **Failure** — Undo a grouping run and the arrows it silently retired do not come back; the person is told the board is back as it was, and it is not. The assignment notifications the run fired are not retracted either, so a colleague keeps a bell item for a task that no longer exists — which composes badly with MP8's finding that the notification already names the human rather than the agent. Same defect class as CV17 (label attach bypasses the transaction path) on the **server** side of the same promise: **a write that is real but not journalled is a write that undo cannot see.**
- **Mechanism** — Everything a commit does to the element graph belongs in the transaction it is committing. `rehomeConnectors` returns ops rather than writing them, appended to `txn.Ops` before the journal insert — so their inverses come free and the ghost preview can eventually show them (CV3's endpoint resolution needs the same data). `notifyAssignments` records the notification ids on the transaction so a revert can retract or supersede them, which is the same hook DA22's subscriber bus wants. **Until both land, the revert copy must be qualified rather than absolute**: "the changes are undone", not "the board is back as it was".
- **Hard clause** — **compiler.**

#### FR22 · The last escape hatch — the local undo stack — is wiped on every board load, so the unrevertible states become permanent on reload. This is the answer to the meta-question. 🔴
- **Evidence** — `boardStore.ts:103 loadBoard` sets `{loading:true, boardId, elements:{}, selection:new Set(), undoStack:[], redoStack:[], …}` — it clears both stacks. The stack is capped at 99 entries and lives only in the zustand store. Agent transactions reach it only through the socket echo path (`socket.ts:70-73`).
- **Failure** — Combine this with FR3, FR4 and FR5 and the answer is yes: **there are states a person cannot get out of.** A run that committed and then failed offers no Undo in the agent surface (FR4); a run cancelled mid-apply loses its transaction id entirely (FR3); a half-applied batch never produced a transaction row to broadcast, so it never reached the undo stack at all (FR5). For the first two, Ctrl+Z is the sole remaining recovery — and it is session-local, capped, and destroyed by a page reload or a navigation to another board and back (JN12). A person who hits one of these on Friday and returns on Monday has a board they can only repair by hand, with no record anywhere naming what to repair (FR21). Everything the three prior documents say about revert assumes the run-level path works; **the session-level fallback they implicitly lean on is the weakest link and nobody had looked at it.**
- **Mechanism** — The durable path must cover every case, which is what FR4 (widen the revert edges to any run with a transaction id), FR5 (journal-first, compensate on partial failure) and FR6 (persist the transaction id by its own write) jointly do — after which Ctrl+Z is a convenience rather than the last resort. One additional guard is worth its cost independently: **on board load, if the board has any agent transaction in the last N minutes that its run does not reference, surface it in the run ledger (JN8) as an orphan with a Revert built from the journal's stored inverse** — the ops carry their own `undoChanges`, so the repair needs no plan and no model.
- **Hard clause** — **handler.** Probe: after a forced mid-loop op failure, reloading the board still offers a working one-click undo of what landed.

#### FR23 · The HTTP error boundary discards six of the agent's eight hand-written refusal messages, replacing them with the single word "conflict" 🔴
- **Evidence** — `transport/http/server.go:164-171` — `case errors.Is(err, domain.ErrConflict): status, message = http.StatusConflict, "conflict"`; `ErrForbidden` → `"forbidden"`; `ErrUnavailable` → the generic sentinel text. **Only the `ErrValidation` arm (`:168`) passes `err.Error()` through.** Every agent sentinel in `contracts.go:440-459` wraps one of those: `ErrRunActive`, `ErrStalePlan`, `ErrNotProposed`, `ErrNotRunning` (conflict), `ErrDestructiveNeedsReview` (forbidden), `ErrDisabled` (unavailable). The most careful of them is composed at `service.go:163-165` — `fmt.Errorf("%w: %s started one %s — it will finish shortly", ErrRunActive, who, humanAge(active.CreatedAt))` — and reaches the browser as `"conflict"`. The frontend then hardcodes a generic string: `agentStore.ts:181`, `:339`, `:362` all do `e?.status === 409 ? t('agent.busy') : …`.
- **Failure** — **This is the root cause of a whole layer of unhelpfulness the three prior documents attribute to the agent.** The backend authors wrote genuinely good, specific, actionable refusals — who is holding the board and for how long, that the board changed while you were reviewing, that this run is not working right now — and the transport layer deletes them, so the frontend cannot do better than a guess and every distinct conflict reads as "Qomra is already working on this board" whether or not that is true. It also makes FR11's dead end unnavigable (the stale-plan message never arrives) and FR12's expiry invisible (an expired grant is `"forbidden"`). Worth noting the genuine plus alongside it: `ErrBudget`'s "daily AI budget reached" **does** survive, because it wraps `ErrValidation` — which is why the one refusal people find legible is the one that happened to take the right door.
- **Mechanism** — The conflict and forbidden arms carry the error's own text when it is a **typed application error** and fall back to the generic word otherwise — the distinction already exists in the tree as the `agentError` type, so the switch tests for it rather than for the sentinel: safe by construction, because `agentError` messages are authored, never derived from input. Then the frontend stops guessing: `start`/`retry`/`continueRun` render `e.message` on 409 and keep `t('agent.busy')` only as the fallback for an empty body.
- **Hard clause** — **probe.** A table test over the eight sentinels asserting each one's text survives a round trip is what keeps the next sentinel from being written into a void. Two switch arms; among the highest value-per-line in either document.

## T2 — collaborator

#### FR15 · Retry is unbounded, uninformed, silently truncates the conversation it replays, and drops the viewport
- **Evidence** — `service.go:736-762 Retry`: no cap, no comparison against the prior failure, and it composes `intent := prior.Task.Intent` + `"\nthen: " + note` per refinement — which `Create` then passes through `truncate(sanitizeBody(req.Intent), 1000)` at `:143`. It forwards BoardID, Intent, Scope, SelectionID, Autonomy, AttachmentIDs and **not `Viewport`**; `Continue` (`:790-799`) does forward it. The retry button is the primary action on every FAILED / BUDGET_EXHAUSTED / CANCELLED / DENIED card.
- **Failure** — When the provider is down (FR8) or a precondition deterministically rejects this board's shape, Retry is a loop: press, wait up to eight minutes, receive the identical sentence, press again — each pass charged at full price against the daily cap, with no signal anywhere that the same thing has now failed three times. The truncation is a quieter loss: a run refined five times replays as a >1000-character intent and **the later corrections — the ones the person cared about most — are the ones cut**. And the missing viewport means the retry places work somewhere other than where the original would have, so even a successful recovery lands in the wrong part of the canvas (the failure `contracts.go:161-167` was written to prevent).
- **Mechanism** — (a) Carry `Viewport` exactly as `Continue` does — one field. (b) Keep the refinements as `Refinements` on the new task rather than concatenating them into the intent, so the planner's existing replay path handles them and nothing is truncated (IL11 wants the same change for a different reason). (c) The new run carries `RetryOfRunID` (IL11); on the **second** consecutive failure with the same terminal reason the card stops offering Retry as the primary action and says what it knows — "this failed the same way twice; the AI service is unavailable" — with a Check-again that hits `GET /agent/capabilities` (PL5's `providerHealthy`) instead of spending a run. That is the client-side complement PL5 does not cover.
- **Hard clause** — **handler.**

#### FR20 · A watching collaborator is told only about success; every failure mode is silent
- **Evidence** — `agentStore.ts:436-446 observe()`: `const finished = ev.state && !WORKING.includes(ev.state) && ev.state !== 'PROPOSED'` → clears `active` and then `if (who && ev.state === 'COMPLETED') toast.info(...)`. **There is no branch for FAILED, CANCELLED, BUDGET_EXHAUSTED or REVERTED.** `AgentObserving.tsx:21-24` renders only WORKING/PROPOSED; on anything else the pill simply unmounts.
- **Failure** — The person looking at the shared board is the one MP10's notification design explicitly does not serve (it is about the absent). For them, a colleague's run that half-committed and failed (FR5) produces cards appearing and a pill vanishing with **no toast at all**; a colleague pressing Undo makes 24 elements disappear from under their cursor in silence. `AgentObserving.tsx:3-6` states its own purpose — "until now nothing said so: the run's journal was broadcast to everyone and consumed by nobody, so cards simply moved" — and the fix it shipped covers only the happy path.
- **Mechanism** — `observe` branches on the terminal state with the same redaction discipline the pill already applies: COMPLETED → today's toast; REVERTED → "Sara undid her assistant's 24 changes"; FAILED/CANCELLED with standing effects → "Sara's assistant stopped partway — some changes are still here", which is the honest thing to say to someone whose board just changed for a reason that no longer has an owner. All of it is derivable from the room-safe projection MP3 must build anyway (runId, state, actor, staged) — no new data crosses the redaction boundary. Route it through AX29's polite live region so it is audible too.
- **Hard clause** — **handler**, shared with MP3.

#### FR21 · The journal barely records failure, and the audit view excludes exactly the runs worth auditing
- **Evidence** — `EvError` is emitted at **three sites in the whole package**: `planner.go:416` (an unreadable attachment) and `service.go:460`/`:480` (the two staleness branches). It is emitted for no provider error, no deadline, no precondition failure, no rejected commit, no failed auto-revert, no reconciler kill. `contracts.go:401-402` declares the vocabulary's purpose ("Security events are named separately so alerting can key on the prefix"). `service.go:868 Audit` skips every run that is not COMPLETED/REVERTED/PARTIAL, and `:875` additionally skips any run with zero plan actions.
- **Failure** — "What has the AI changed here" — the view `service.go:850-851` calls the whole basis of trust in an agent — is structurally blind to the runs that changed the board **without finishing**: the half-commit (FR5), the cancelled apply (FR3), the postcondition failure whose revert failed (FR4). Those are the only runs whose effects a person cannot account for, and they are the ones the audit refuses to list. On the operations side, a monitor keying on `type: error` sees a quiet system through a total provider outage.
- **Mechanism** — (a) Every `finishWithReason` to a non-success state emits `EvError` alongside the state event, with a typed `cause` field (`provider | deadline | precondition | commit-rejected | reconciled | revert-failed`) — the same enum FR8 needs, so the two land together and IL22's content-free store has something to count. (b) `Audit`'s filter changes from a state whitelist to `len(r.TransactionIDs) > 0`, and each entry carries the terminal state, so a half-commit appears as "17 changes, run stopped partway, not undone" with the revert FR4 restores. **That single predicate change is what makes the audit view answer the question its own docstring asks.** (Note the deliberate opposition with IL19: the audit widens to include runs that *wrote*; the analytics read widens to include runs that *did not*. Two surfaces, two filters, both currently wrong in opposite directions.)
- **Hard clause** — **handler.**

#### FR24 · Correction to PL6: the daily-spend meter is not invisible — the composer already shows the remaining balance
- **Evidence** — `AgentAsk.tsx:301-311` renders `${Math.max(0, budget.dailyCapUsd - budget.spentTodayUsd).toFixed(2)} left`, with a `low` class at 80% of the cap; `api/types.ts:395` carries `budget?: {spentTodayUsd, dailyCapUsd}`, served from `service.go:273-275 SpentToday`. The composer also states the per-pass ceiling and the pass multiplier (`AgentAsk.tsx:40-41`, `:294-311`).
- **Failure** — PL6 states "an invisible meter that silently refuses is the worst possible failure mode, which is why every competitor converged on a visible credit balance." **The meter exists and is good.** A wave planned from PL6 as written would rebuild a balance display that already ships and miss the two places the meter genuinely is absent: mid-run spend is hidden for the entire duration (FR16), and the cap is admission-time only so a single run can overshoot it arbitrarily and K concurrent runs can bypass it by a factor of K (SC15). Getting this on record matters because it changes which half of PL6 is urgent.
- **Mechanism** — Keep the admission meter as shipped. Add the two missing checks against the same numbers: the running `usage.costUsd` on the Working card (FR16c), and `checkBudget` re-evaluated between model turns so a run crossing the daily ceiling stops through W4's forced-finish path **with a preserved plan** (FR7) rather than after the fact. The 500-document scan and the per-workspace dimension stay as PL6 describes them, and SC2's cache-write term must land first or every number is wrong.
- **Hard clause** — **handler.**

---

# Corner DL — Data lifecycle, privacy and trust

*Method: retention, deletion, portability, disclosure, and the surfaces that tell
(or fail to tell) a person where their work went — across the object store, the
element graph, the transaction journal, comments, notifications, agent runs and
events, the export, the share plane and the model provider.*

*Three findings contradict claims the code makes about itself, and they are the
sharpest in this document: `i18n:50` promises account deletion removes "all
uploaded files" and no code path removes a blob (DL1); `storage/r2.go:70-71` says
"the client re-resolves stale attachment URLs on 403" and no such route and no
such client code exist (DL3); `domain/models.go:32` frames comment immutability
as a product rule and `domain/repository.go:90-95` has made it a storage
absolute — comments cannot be deleted by any code path, including account
deletion (DL12).*

*One-line version of the corner: **QomraNote ships a Privacy tab with three
toggles, none of which are about AI, in a product whose central feature transmits
the user's film production to Anthropic or Google — and the word "Anthropic"
appears nowhere in `frontend/src`.** Meanwhile the four collections holding the
most content and the object store have no retention policy between them, and
"Delete forever" is true of none of them.*

## T1 — parity and live wrongs

#### DL1 · "This permanently deletes … all uploaded files" is false — no code path ever removes a blob 🔴
- **Evidence** — `frontend/src/i18n/index.ts:50` — "This permanently deletes your account, every board you own, and all uploaded files. This cannot be undone." (re-verified verbatim). The deletion path is `service/account_service.go:220-270`: hard-delete elements, then `labels.DeleteByOwner`, `attachments.DeleteByOwner`, `notifications.DeleteByUser`, TenantPurgers, `users.Delete`. And `repository/mongo/misc_repos.go:258-261` — `AttachmentRepo.DeleteByOwner` is `col.DeleteMany({ownerId: sub})`, **a Mongo row delete and nothing else** (re-verified). `storage.LocalPresigner.Remove` exists and is wired ONLY into `MaintenanceService`, which never runs on account deletion. `AccountService` has no `BlobRemover` field.
- **Failure** — The most consequential promise the product makes is made in the confirmation dialog for its most irreversible action, and it is not kept. After "Delete forever", every image, screenshot and PDF the filmmaker uploaded remains byte-for-byte on disk or in the R2 bucket — **and remains fetchable, because `GET /api/v1/blob/*` is unauthenticated** (`router.go:13-15`). The DB row that named the file is gone, so nothing in the product can even enumerate what was left behind. This is worse than not deleting: it is **unauditable** non-deletion.
- **Mechanism** — `AccountService` gains the same optional `BlobRemover` `MaintenanceService` already takes. `DeleteAccount` enumerates attachments by owner **before** deleting the rows (a `ListByOwner` the repo lacks though the index exists), removes each blob, then deletes the rows; failures are collected and the account deletion refuses to report success silently, because a partial purge reporting success is the current bug. The R2 driver must implement `Remove` (it has only PresignPut/PresignGet), or the deployment must be documented as requiring a bucket lifecycle rule **and the config must fail loudly at boot when neither exists.**
- **Hard clause** — **probe.** A repository contract test asserting zero blobs remain for that owner — the same clause DA15 asks for on transactions, extended to the store DA15 did not count.

#### DL2 · Attachment rows and blobs are never collected when the element that referenced them is purged — orphans accumulate forever and stay publicly readable 🔴
- **Evidence** — `service/maintenance_service.go:35-65` is the ONLY garbage collector, and it does two things: `elements.PurgeExpired` and `attachments.StalePresigned`, whose predicate is `{status:'presigned', createdAt < cutoff}` — it collects uploads that never **completed**. A COMPLETED attachment whose IMAGE element was trashed and then hard-purged matches no query anywhere in the codebase. `PurgeExpired` is `DeleteMany({deletedAt: {$ne:null, $lt:cutoff}})` and never reads `content.attachmentId`; same for `DeletePermanently` and `EmptyTrash`.
- **Failure** — Every "Delete forever" and every Empty Trash leaves the actual bytes behind, and the blob endpoint is unauthenticated with the URL still sitting in any board snapshot, export, or IndexedDB mirror that ever saw it. A filmmaker deleting a rejected casting photo has deleted the card, not the photograph. Storage cost is the trivial half; the real half is that **the product's delete gesture does not reach the content class most likely to be sensitive.**
- **Mechanism** — `PurgeExpired` changes shape: it returns the purged elements' `attachmentId`s rather than a count, and `SweepOnce` removes those blobs and rows — likewise for the ids returned by `DeletePermanently`/`EmptyTrash`, which must also return rather than swallow them. Guard against shared references (a duplicated card points at the same attachment): reference-count by querying elements for that attachmentId, or accept a one-sweep delay and collect attachments with zero live referrers. **The reference check must fail closed** — an attachment whose referrer query errors is kept, never removed.
- **Hard clause** — **handler.**

#### DL3 · On the production storage driver every uploaded image and PDF goes permanently dead after 7 days, and the recovery path the code promises does not exist 🔴
- **Evidence** — `backend/internal/storage/r2.go:66-80` — when `R2_PUBLIC_BASE_URL` is empty (the `.env.example:40` default), `publicURL` is a `PresignGetObject(..., 7*24*time.Hour)` signed URL. The comment at `:70-71` reads, verbatim: *"Private bucket: a week-long presigned GET keeps boards renderable; the client re-resolves stale attachment URLs on 403."* **That re-resolution does not exist.** `transport/http/router.go:72-73` registers exactly two attachment routes — presign and complete (re-verified). `service.UploadService.Get`, whose doc comment says "returns an attachment for URL re-resolution", has **no handler and no route**. On the client, `BoardCanvas.tsx:224-231` writes `content.url = presigned.publicUrl` permanently into the element; `api/client.ts:196-206` has no 403 path; `grep -rn '403' frontend/src` returns nothing.
- **Failure** — Silent, total, unrecoverable-by-the-user data loss on exactly the content a filmmaker's boards are made of. A mood board assembled in July is a grid of broken images in August. The bytes are still in the bucket — **the product simply forgot how to address them.** It takes the agent down with it: `vision.go:100-121` fetches through `att.PublicURL`, so `look_at` also goes blind at day 7 and reports "that image could not be read". The false comment is worse than no comment, because it is why nobody built the route.
- **Mechanism** — Two clauses. (1) Stop storing an expiring credential as durable content: `content.url` becomes a stable indirection — `/api/v1/attachments/{id}/blob` — that the API 302-redirects to a freshly signed GET **after an ACL check**, which is also the fix for DL4 and gives blob reads an authorization point they have never had. (2) Until that lands, ship the route the comment already promises: `GET /api/v1/attachments/:id` returning a fresh publicUrl, **ACL-checked** (`UploadService.Get` currently has no ownership check and must not be routed as written), plus a client-side `onError` handler on image elements that re-resolves once and patches `content.url`.
- **Hard clause** — **schema.** A migration must re-sign or re-point every existing IMAGE/FILE element, or the fix helps only new uploads and every board already made stays broken.

#### DL4 · Presigned bucket credentials are embedded in element content, broadcast to every viewer, written into exports, and cached on viewers' disks — outliving any revocation 🔴
- **Evidence** — The `publicUrl` from `r2.go:72-79` is an AWS SigV4 URL carrying `X-Amz-Credential` and `X-Amz-Signature`. It is written into `content.url` (`BoardCanvas.tsx:230-231`, `Toolbar.tsx:123-124`, `clipboard.ts:152-153`) and therefore travels: in every `GET /boards/:id/children` response, in every `BroadcastTransaction`, in `format=json` export, in `DataExport`, and into every viewer's IndexedDB via `lib/boardCache.ts:50-56` with a **14-day** max age.
- **Failure** — A signed URL is a **bearer credential for direct bucket access that bypasses the application entirely**. Revoking a share link removes the recipient's route into the board and does nothing at all to the signed URLs they already hold — in their browser cache, in their IndexedDB mirror for two weeks, in any JSON export they took. For a production board holding unreleased stills, "I revoked their access" is simply not true for the seven days those signatures remain valid.
- **Mechanism** — The same indirection as DL3 clause (1): element content holds an opaque, non-credential reference; signing happens per request behind an ACL check, so revocation is immediate **by construction**. Additionally: `DataExport` and `format=json` must resolve attachment references to a manifest of `{id, filename, contentType, size}` rather than emitting live signed URLs — an export is an archive, not a set of keys.
- **Hard clause** — **schema.** `content.url` must stop being a credential-bearing field; the type change is what makes every downstream leak impossible rather than individually patched.

#### DL5 · A read-only share-link holder can read the board's own edit token — view-to-edit escalation through the export and board-read endpoints 🔴 *(deepens MP2)*
- **Evidence** — `domain/element.go:89-104` (re-verified): `ACL.PublicEditLink` is `json:"publicEditLink,omitempty"` and `ViewLink.Token` is `json:"token"`. **Only `PasswordHash` is `json:"-"`, which proves the author knew how to withhold a field.** `transport/http/router.go:21-27` puts `GET /boards/:id` and `GET /boards/:id/export` in the **optional-auth** group, so an anonymous caller with `X-Share-Token` reaches them; `service/access.go:82-88` grants RoleView on a matching ViewLink token; `board_service.go:197-222` then returns, for `format=json`, `{board, elements: Descendants(...)}` — every document serialized whole, ACL included. `BoardService.Get` returns the raw Board element too. Grep for redact/sanitizeACL across `backend/internal` returns one unrelated hit.
- **Failure** — Send someone a read-only link to the pitch board and they can promote themselves to editor by reading one JSON response — **no exploit, just `?format=json`.** The same response hands them every collaborator's Keycloak subject id and every nested board's own share tokens. This is distinct from MP2, which is about what an edit-link holder can make the AGENT do; this is **how a viewer becomes an edit-link holder**. It also silently widens PL11: the export is not merely lossy, it is over-inclusive in the one field that must never leave the owner.
- **Mechanism** — One function applied at every serialization boundary: `ACL.For(role)` returns the ACL a caller of that role may see — Owner everything, Editor ownerId+editors, View/Feedback ownerId only and **never a token**. `BoardService.Get`, `Children`, `Unsorted`, `Export(json)`, `DataExport` and the realtime envelope all route through it. Simultaneously tag `ViewLink.Token` and `ACL.PublicEditLink` `json:"-"` and surface them ONLY through the owner-gated `ShareState` — so the default for a **new** field is withheld rather than exposed.
- **Hard clause** — **probe.** A server test asserting a View-role response contains neither token string, run against every endpoint that returns an Element. Same severity class as DA1.

#### DL6 · `agent_runs` and `agent_events` are a permanent, uncapped archive of every prompt, steer and drafted sentence — including plans the person explicitly discarded 🔴
- **Evidence** — `repository/mongo/indexes.go:37-53` — five indexes across the two collections and **no TTL index**; nothing anywhere calls `ExpireAfterSeconds`. The only delete is `DeleteByTenant` on account deletion. What is retained: `Task.Intent` (the person's own words), `Refinements`, `Steers`, and the whole Plan — `plan.go:294-316` with Actions carrying Title/Text/Rows and `NewComments` carrying full comment bodies. The state filter in the audit path proves DISCARDED runs are retained: they are excluded from the **view**, not from storage. `plan.go:314-316`'s own comment — "a discarded preview leaves no orphan threads behind" — is true of the board and false of this collection.
- **Failure** — Nothing in the product tells the person this exists, and nothing lets them clear it. Every request typed into the composer — including the ones about the film's budget, the ones about a difficult collaborator, the drafts rejected as wrong — is retained verbatim and indefinitely under their tenantSub. **The act of pressing Discard is the person saying "not this"; the system files it permanently.** DA15 identified transactions as the collection that ignores the privacy tab; this is the second, and it holds the user's **intent** rather than only their content, which is the more revealing artefact.
- **Mechanism** — Three clauses. (1) TTL: `{completedAt}` on runs and `{at}` on events at a configurable retention (90 days is the honest default — it matches TrashRetention and outlives any revert window), with a partial filter on `active: false` so an in-flight run can never be swept mid-run. (2) A DISCARDED or CANCELLED run keeps its Task, its state and its usage and **drops `Plan.Actions` text and `NewComments` at the moment of discard** — the audit value is "you asked X and rejected the answer", not the rejected sentences. (3) The Privacy tab gains "Agent history: kept for N days" with a "Clear my agent history" action, which is the `DeleteByTenant` already implemented for account deletion.
- **Hard clause** — **schema.** The retention window must be one config value read by both the TTL migration and the settings string, or the number shown to the user drifts from the number enforced.

#### DL7 · Logging out leaves 14 days of full board content on the device — and the function that would clear it is dead code 🔴
- **Evidence** — `frontend/src/lib/boardCache.ts:58-64` exports `clearBoardCache()`. `grep -rn "clearBoardCache" frontend/src` returns **exactly one hit: the definition itself** (re-verified). `auth/keycloak.ts:112-114` `export function logout() { keycloak.logout({redirectUri: window.location.origin}); }` — and nothing else. The cache holds `{view, children, unsorted}` — full element content including `textPreview`, document bodies and table cells — keyed by board id in IndexedDB `qomranote`, with a 14-day max age checked only on read, never enforced by eviction. `SettingsDialog.tsx:169-173` calls `api.deleteAccount()` then `logout()` — same path, same residue.
- **Failure** — A filmmaker in a shared edit suite, an agency machine, a rented workstation, a client's laptop — logs out, and the next person can open devtools and read the production boards, or simply re-open the PWA offline and have them paint from cache. The product deliberately built the mirror for instant paint (a good feature) and never built the other half. The account-deletion case is the sharpest: **the user just asked for everything to be destroyed and the most locally accessible copy is what survives.** PL12 reads `boardCache.ts` and calls it "a read-only IndexedDB mirror" in an argument about offline **writes**; the device-residue half is untouched.
- **Mechanism** — `logout()` awaits `clearBoardCache()` before redirecting, and does the same for any future local store — which argues for one `clearLocalData()` that owns the list, so a new store is added in one place rather than remembered in three. Two clauses beyond the obvious: (a) Keycloak can log the user out from another tab or device, and that path must clear too, or "sign me out everywhere" leaves the local copy; (b) namespace the cache by subject id, so switching accounts on one machine cannot paint the previous person's boards even for the instant before reconcile.
- **Hard clause** — **handler.** Clearing must complete **before** the redirect fires, or the navigation kills the IndexedDB transaction.

#### DL8 · Nowhere in the product does it say that board content is transmitted to a third-party model provider — and the personification actively conceals it 🔴
- **Evidence** — `grep -rn "anthropic|gemini|Anthropic|Gemini|provider" frontend/src` returns two files, both source comments, **neither rendered**. `grep -rn "privacy policy|terms|policy" frontend/src` returns only icon-catalogue string helpers. `frontend/index.html` has no policy link. The complete privacy vocabulary shown to a user is `i18n:101-104` — presence, email visibility, download-my-data — **none about AI**. The closest agent strings are `i18n:129` "What the agent can see — click to change", `:151` "The agent reads the most relevant part", `:162` "Attach an image or PDF to this request". The API's own honest catalogue (`handlers_agent.go:273-303`) lists nine can/cannot clauses and names no provider and no transmission. Meanwhile `cognition/select.go:26-46` sends the compiled digest to `api.anthropic.com` or the Gemini API.
- **Failure** — The product calls the model **"Qomra"** — a name, in the user's own language, presented as part of the app ("Ask Qomra to do anything on this board"). A reasonable person reads that as a feature of the software they installed. It is a courier to Anthropic or Google. For a filmmaker whose boards hold unreleased scripts, talent details and client budgets, the difference is the whole question — and it is one this UI is, if anything, structured to prevent them asking. **Every other trust mechanism in these four documents — preview, revert, delegation, quarantine — governs what the agent WRITES. Not one governs, or discloses, what it SENDS.**
- **Mechanism** — Three surfaces, smallest first. (1) `GET /agent/capabilities` gains `processing: {provider, model, region, retentionPolicyUrl}` from config — the API already answers "what can this deployment's agent do"; "and who processes it" is the same question. (2) The composer carries a persistent, quiet line — "Reads this board and sends it to {provider} to work out a plan" — with the name from that payload, **not hard-coded**, so a self-hosted deployment states its own truth. (3) A first-run consent the account records once (`agent.processingAcknowledgedAt`), because the honest version of this is asked before the first run, not discovered in a settings tab.
- **Hard clause** — **handler.** The provider name must come from the live provider (`cognition.Provider.Name()` already exists) and never from a client constant, or the disclosure becomes wrong on the first deployment that switches keys.

#### DL9 · Sending an image or PDF to the model renders in the UI as "Thinking…" 🔴 *(deepens LP8)*
- **Evidence** — `AgentWorking.tsx:18-26 phraseKeyFor` matches five substrings: 'read board', 'read_board', 'search', 'create', 'move'. **There is no case for `look_at` and none for `read_url`**; `:25` falls through to 'agent.work.thinking'. The server does emit the tool name (`planner.go:507` with `stepSummary` producing "step 3 — look_at"), so the information reaches the client and is discarded. What `look_at` does: `toolhandlers.go:194-225` fetches the attachment and appends `cognition.ImagePart{Data: bytes}` to the next turn; `provider.go:64-70` documents that images travel as **raw bytes** to the provider. Request-attached files take the same route unprompted at `planner.go:410-420`.
- **Failure** — The most privacy-consequential action the agent can take — a storyboard, a contract, a rushes still leaving the deployment as bytes — is displayed as the least informative string the component can produce. The person watching sees the same word whether the agent was reasoning about column titles or uploading their client's NDA. And because `look_at` is a READ it leaves no trace in the plan, no ghost on the canvas and no row in the audit view (DL15), so **this transient "Thinking…" is the only moment the event is ever visible to a human.** This deepens LP8 with an instance LP8's mechanism would not catch: LP8's manifest covers the digest blocks assembled before the loop; `look_at` happens mid-loop, per turn, and needs its own line.
- **Mechanism** — Two clauses. (1) `phraseKeyFor` gains `look_at` → "Reading {filename} — sending it to {provider}" and `read_url` → "Fetching {host}"; the filename is already in the server's outcome string, so promote it into `Event.Data` and let the client format rather than parse. Per AX38 the string should be a structured `announce`, not prose. (2) **The transmission must outlive the panel:** every `look_at` and `read_url` appends to a per-run `ContextReads []ContextRead{kind, ref, name, bytes, at}` on the Run, which is what DL15 renders and what makes the event answerable a month later.
- **Hard clause** — **digest.** The read record must be written by the tool handler itself, not by the UI layer, or a future caller bypasses it — which is exactly how the request-attachment path came to emit no event at all.

## T2 — collaborator

#### DL10 · Removing an editor or revoking a share link does not disconnect them — the live content stream keeps flowing to a revoked session 🔴
- **Evidence** — `service/share_service.go:104-120 RemoveEditor` mutates `ACL.Editors`, calls `elements.SetACL`, returns. No hub interaction; `ShareService` has no hub dependency. `RevokeLink` (`:171`) is the same shape. Authorization for a socket is checked **exactly once**, at handshake (`handlers.go:261`).
