After that, `realtime/client.go:59-60` sets the session lifetime purely from the token's own expiry, and room membership (`hub.go:83-113`) is keyed on the connection and never re-resolved. `Hub.BroadcastTransaction` fans the full `domain.Transaction` — every Op with its `Changes` content — to everyone in the room.
- **Failure** — "Remove" in a sharing dialog means **now**. Here it means "at some point up to the remaining lifetime of a token you cannot see" — and until then the removed person receives, live, the full text of every card anyone edits on the board they were just cut off from. On a production board that is precisely the window in which the removal happened for a reason. Combined with DL4, revocation currently reaches neither the live stream nor the already-issued asset credentials.
- **Mechanism** — The hub already has `NotifyUser(sub, …)` and rooms keyed by boardID, so eviction is a small addition: `Hub.Evict(boardID, sub)` closing matching clients, called from `RemoveEditor`; `Hub.EvictByShareToken(boardID, token)` for `RevokeLink`, which requires Client to retain the token it joined with (`Principal.ShareToken` already carries it). Second clause, because eviction alone is a race: clients re-authorize on reconnect, so a periodic re-resolve — cheap, since `Resolve` is a parent walk — closes the gap for sessions mid-flight.
- **Hard clause** — **handler.** Eviction must be driven by the ACL **write**, not by a sweeper, or the guarantee degrades to "eventually" and the dialog is still lying.

#### DL11 · Deleting your account destroys your collaborators' work, silently, and there is no way to hand a board over instead 🔴 *(two territories: DL and JN)*
- **Evidence** — `service/account_service.go:220-238` — `DeleteAccount` walks `OwnedBoards(sub, includeDeleted=true)` and **HardDeletes** each board with every descendant (re-verified). Descendants include elements other people created: `CreatedBy` is per-element and the ACL that decides ownership lives only on the BOARD. The doc comment at `:218-219` states the policy for the other direction — "Content the user contributed to other people's boards stays (their boards, their data); the departing user is only stripped from ACLs" — **and is silent on this one**; `RemoveEditorEverywhere` implements that half. Ownership transfer does not exist: `ACL.OwnerID` is assigned only at creation and at `element_service.go:146`; `ShareService`'s whole surface has no transfer verb, and a collaborator cannot leave either (RemoveEditor is owner-gated).
- **Failure** — The most severe finding in this corner, and a pure journey defect — it exists only at the seam between journey 5 and journey 6. The AD sets up the production board, invites the director, the producer and the DoP, and everyone works in it for four months. The AD then leaves the production and deletes their account. **Every board they own is hard-deleted with its entire subtree** — the director's shot lists, the producer's budget tables, the DoP's lookbook — not soft-deleted, not into the 90-day trash, not exportable afterwards, with no warning to the four people losing it and no notification after. And there is no ownership transfer, so the correct action — hand the board to the producer before leaving — cannot be performed. Three of the eighteen MP findings model contention between collaborators on a shared board; **none models the board's owner leaving, which on a freelance production crew is the normal ending.** The delete path even runs `RemoveEditorEverywhere` first, proving the author was thinking about collaborators — in exactly one direction.
- **Mechanism** — Three clauses, and the first is a one-day fix that should not wait for the second. (1) **`DeleteAccount` refuses** when any owned board has a non-empty `ACL.Editors`, with an enumerated list: "3 boards are shared with 6 people. Transfer or unshare them first." A refusal is correct here — silently destroying other people's work to satisfy a self-service delete is not defensible under any privacy reading, and the user's own data is still fully deletable once the shared boards are handed over. (2) `TransferOwnership(boardId, toSub)` (JN15's clause 3): owner-only, target must already be an editor, notifies both parties, refuses on Home, and re-stamps `ACL.OwnerID` on the board **and every nested board in the subtree** — since both `applyCreate` and the template path mint per-board ACLs, a subtree transfer is a walk, not a single write. (3) While there: DA15's missing journal deletion belongs in the same method, and the export offered before deletion must include the shared boards being handed over.
- **Hard clause** — **handler.** Probe: a repository test asserting a shared board survives its owner's account deletion.

#### DL12 · Comments cannot be deleted by any code path in the system — including account deletion 🔴
- **Evidence** — `domain/repository.go:89-95` — `CommentRepository` is Insert, Get, ListByThread, Update. **There is no Delete of any kind**: not by id, not by thread, not by owner. Consequently: no purger deletes comments (`cli/serve.go:148` registers exactly one TenantPurger, the agent service), `PurgeExpired` removing a COMMENT_THREAD leaves its messages behind, HardDelete of a whole board leaves them behind, and `DeleteAccount` never mentions them. `domain/models.go:31-32`'s comment frames this as a product rule — "messages cannot be removed from a thread once posted (§4.17)" — which is a rule about the **UI**, not about erasure. Comments also carry `Reactions map[string][]string` of user subs. `DataExport` does not include them either.
- **Failure** — Every word a person writes in a comment is, by construction, both **non-portable and non-erasable**. "Download my data" does not include it; "Delete forever" does not reach it; deleting the board it lived on does not reach it. After account deletion the `AuthorID` points at a user row that `users.Delete` just removed, so the messages persist as permanently orphaned, permanently un-attributable text — and their reaction maps still hold other live users' subject ids. DA15 named transactions as the collection in this blind spot; comments is a second, and unlike transactions it has **no delete method at the interface level**, so no purge path can even be wired without a schema change.
- **Mechanism** — `CommentRepository` gains `DeleteByThread` and `DeleteByAuthor`. Three wirings: `ElementService`'s permanent-delete paths call `DeleteByThread` for every COMMENT_THREAD in the purged id set; `MaintenanceService` does the same after `PurgeExpired`; `AccountService` registers a CommentPurger implementing `TenantPurger` — the interface the codebase already built for exactly this and then used once. **The product rule stays intact:** `DeleteByAuthor` is reachable only from account deletion, never from a UI verb, so "you cannot unsay a comment" remains true for users and stops being true for the storage layer. `ExportData` gains a Comments block.
- **Hard clause** — **probe.** A repository contract test asserting zero comments survive `DeleteAccount` — **the enumerate-by-hand pattern in `DeleteAccount` is what lost transactions, comments and blobs, and only a test that walks the collection list will stop it losing the fourth.**

#### DL13 · "Download my data" omits your trash, your files, your comments, your agent history, your settings, and everything you made on shared boards
- **Evidence** — `service/account_service.go:176-215` — `DataExport` is four fields: User, Boards, Elements, Labels. The walk is `OwnedBoards(sub, includeDeleted=**false**)` and `Descendants(b.ID, **false**)` — trashed content is excluded. Not present: attachments (no manifest, no bytes, no filenames), comments (no repository method exists — DL12), notifications, agent runs and events (IL27), transactions (DA15), and `UserSettings` — which is carried on the User document and so leaks in by accident rather than by design. Boards where the caller is an EDITOR rather than owner are structurally absent. The UI describes it as "Everything you own — boards, cards, labels — as a single JSON file".
- **Failure** — The asymmetry against deletion is the tell: `DeleteAccount` passes `includeDeleted=**true**`. So the system will **destroy** your trash and **refuses to export** it — for 90 days there is content you can restore, that Delete will erase, and that Download cannot give you. Beyond that, an export missing every uploaded file is not a portability artifact for a filmmaker at all; the boards are scaffolding around the images. And a person who spent a year contributing to a colleague's shared production board can export precisely none of it.
- **Mechanism** — Four clauses. (1) `includeDeleted=true` with a `deleted: true` marker per element, so the export is a faithful snapshot rather than a filtered view. (2) An `attachments` manifest of `{id, filename, contentType, size, sha}` plus, for a real export, the bytes — which means the export stops being a JSON response and becomes a **job producing a zip**, since streaming a production board's media through an HTTP handler is not viable. (3) That job is the natural home for comments, agent runs, notifications and settings as their own top-level blocks. (4) `contributions`: elements with `CreatedBy == sub` on boards the caller can still view but does not own, clearly separated so the archive never implies ownership it does not have.
- **Hard clause** — **schema.** The export document needs a version field and a manifest of which blocks are present, or DL14's restore cannot tell a partial archive from a complete one.

#### DL14 · Export is one-way: no import, no restore, no backup — the data can leave and can never come back
- **Evidence** — Grep across `transport/http` and `service` for an import route or handler returns only Go `import` statements. `router.go` registers no POST accepting an export document; the only structured write is `POST /transactions` taking `[]domain.Op`. There is no `POST /boards/import`, no `POST /me/import`, no restore-from-file anywhere. `SettingsDialog.tsx:546-560` downloads a blob and offers nothing in the other direction. The board-level JSON export is explicitly labelled "lossless, machine-readable" at `board_service.go:209` — a format built for round-tripping, **with no reader**.
- **Failure** — For a filmmaker this is the difference between a tool and a filing cabinet with a one-way slot. There is no way to archive a wrapped production and restore it next year, no way to duplicate a board structure into a new project from a saved file, no way to move to a self-hosted instance, and no way to recover from the deletion paths above — which matters enormously given that DL11 destroys a shared board irrecoverably and DL1/DL2 destroy files the export never captured in the first place. It is also the missing half of portability: the right to receive data is only meaningful alongside somewhere to put it. PL11 identifies the export as structurally lossy; it does not observe that even the lossless format has **no consumer**.
- **Mechanism** — Import must produce a **staged plan, never direct writes** — PL10's discipline applied to the archive format, and it composes: an import is read as a proposed subtree, previewed as ghosts, and applied as ONE transaction through the existing path, inheriting revert for free. Clauses: (a) ids are **remapped** on import, never reused, or an import into a live account collides and silently overwrites; (b) internal references — `location.parentId`, line from/to, alias target, clone source, `labelIds` — are rewritten through the remap table, and a reference that does not resolve becomes a named `unmet` rather than a dangling pointer; (c) ACLs and share tokens in the archive are **discarded** and the importer becomes owner, which is the only safe default and is DL5's fix arriving from the other side; (d) attachments are re-uploaded and re-keyed.
- **Hard clause** — **compiler.** Import emits Actions into the existing plan compiler and touches no new write path, or it becomes the second write path the whole architecture was built to avoid.

#### DL15 · The audit answers "what the agent changed", never "what the agent read" — a run that shipped your whole board to a model and was discarded leaves no record at all
- **Evidence** — `agent/service.go:852-888 Audit`: `:868` skips any run not in {COMPLETED, REVERTED, PARTIAL} — so DISCARDED, CANCELLED and FAILED runs never appear — and `:875-877` skips any run with zero plan actions. `AuditEntry` is `{RunID, Intent, At, Ops, Reverted, CostUSD, State}`: no element count, no board list, no attachment list, no model. The digest the run sent is not persisted anywhere: `digest.go` assembles ancestry, people, labels, account rules and the subtree walk into a string passed to the provider and dropped. The only durable trace that content was read is one event, "read board — N items in scope" — an agent_event, which DL6 shows has no retention policy and which the audit view never reads. **Attachment reads leave nothing at all** (DL9).
- **Failure** — Every retention and disclosure item above needs a record of what left, when, and to whom; there is none. A run that read 340 elements across four boards, transmitted two PDFs, and was discarded because the plan was wrong is — from the audit's perspective — something that never happened. The person cannot answer "was the client's contract ever sent to a model", and neither can the operator. This is the durable, queryable complement to LP8: **LP8's panel built without persistence would still leave this hole.**
- **Mechanism** — The Run gains a `Reads` block written by the scope compiler and the read tools — `{elementCount, boardIDs, attachmentIDs, urlsFetched, digestBytes}` plus DL9's per-read entries — and the audit's state filter is removed for a new READ tab: **every run that reached PLANNING is listed there whatever its outcome, because reading happened regardless of whether writing did.** The write tab keeps its current filter (its noise argument is correct for writes and wrong for reads; FR21 makes the separate case for widening it to runs that wrote and failed). This is also what the Privacy tab should link to.
- **Hard clause** — **digest.** The Reads block must be produced by `CompileScope` itself, not maintained alongside it — LP8 makes the identical argument for the same reason: a hand-maintained inventory of what was sent drifts within one wave and is worse than none, because it is believed.

#### DL16 · There is no way to tell the agent not to read something — no board, column or element opt-out exists at any layer
- **Evidence** — Grep for `locked|private|excludeFrom|agentOff|noAgent|optOut` across `agent/digest.go` and `domain/element.go` returns nothing. The digest's per-type renderer (`:620-690`) emits content for every element it walks — card textPreview, full table cells, document bodies, swatch values, folded column contents — with **no exclusion predicate anywhere in the walk**. The only agent-facing board field is `content.agentInstructions`, which is guidance TO the model and is itself rendered into the prompt. `UserSettings.Agent` is one field, Instructions. `PrivacySettings` is ShowPresence and ShowEmailToOthers. Scope narrowing exists but it is a per-**request** choice by whoever starts the run, not a property of the content — so it protects nothing from the next run, or from a collaborator's run.
- **Failure** — Real production boards are not uniformly shareable with a third party. Cast medical notes, a distributor's numbers, an unsigned contract, a private note about a crew member — these sit on the same board as the shot list, and today the only way to keep them out of a model context is to keep them out of QomraNote. **That is the product losing to a notebook for the material that matters most.** And because the choice is per-request rather than per-content, a board owner cannot make the decision at all: an editor's run (MP1) reads whatever the digest walks.
- **Mechanism** — One boolean on Content — `agentExclude` — honoured in exactly **one place**: the scope walk's admission predicate in `CompileScope`, dropping the element and, for containers, its whole subtree. Three supporting clauses. (a) The digest must **state** the exclusion rather than hide it — "3 items on this board are marked private and were not read" — since silence would let the model reason confidently over a hole, the same honesty rule DA19 applies to elided counts. (b) The flag is a property of the element, so it survives moves, is visible on the card, and applies to every run by every collaborator — **not a mode**. (c) `look_at` must re-check it, since an excluded IMAGE must not be readable by id.
- **Hard clause** — **digest.** Enforcement at scope compilation, one function, so no tool can route around it — enforcing it per-tool is how DA4 and DA5 ended up with element types some paths see and others do not.

#### DL17 · The trash clock is shown only when the trash is empty, and the irreversible confirmations are hard-coded English in a bilingual product
- **Evidence** — `TrashPanel.tsx`: the retention sentence "Deleted items are kept for 3 months." is inside the `items.length === 0` branch (`:77-82`) — visible only when there is nothing to lose. With items present each row shows an absolute date (`:56`) and never a purge date. The panel bypasses i18n entirely (JN4): 'Trash', 'Empty trash', 'Restore', 'Delete forever', 'Trash is empty', 'DELETED BY ME', 'DELETED BY OTHERS', and the Empty-trash confirmation at `:37`. Backend retention is 90 days, which the UI rounds to "3 months" — a difference of up to a day at the boundary.
- **Failure** — The person who most needs the retention window is the one looking at a deleted sequence they might want back; they are precisely the person the UI never tells (JN21). There is no per-item countdown, no warning as expiry approaches, and no notification — the content simply stops existing on a sweep, with no record, since `PurgeExpired` returns only a count. Separately: in an app built to be bilingual for an Arabic-speaking user, **the confirmation dialog for the single most destructive action in the product is untranslated English**, and a destructive confirm the reader cannot parse is not consent. CV9 argues the UI never goes RTL; this is the narrower and more urgent point that this panel was never translated at all.
- **Mechanism** — Four small clauses. (1) Each row states its own deadline — "deletes in 12 days" — computed from `deletedAt + TrashRetention`, **with the constant served by the API** rather than duplicated as a client string, so "3 months" and 90 days cannot drift. (2) The retention line moves out of the empty state onto the panel header. (3) The panel routes through `useT` like every other component (JN4). (4) A notification at T-7 days for anything trashed — the Notifier and NotificationKind vocabulary already exist; this needs one new kind and a predicate in the existing sweep.
- **Hard clause** — **handler.** The deadline must be computed from the server's constant, or a config change silently makes every countdown in the UI wrong.

#### DL18 · Notifications are a permanent, uncapped copy of your board titles and task text sitting in other people's accounts, surviving unshare and board deletion
- **Evidence** — `Notification` carries a free-text `Message` (`domain/models.go:96`) built from live content: `comment_label_service.go:76` `p.Name + " mentioned you on \"" + board.Title() + "\""`, `transaction_service.go:211` `p.Name + " assigned you a task: \"" + text + "\""` — **the task's own text, verbatim**. Rows are keyed by recipient and deleted only by `DeleteByUser`, called from exactly one place: `DeleteAccount:249`, for the **recipient**. `indexes.go:67-69` gives the collection one compound index and **no TTL**. Nothing deletes a notification when the board is deleted, when the element is purged, when the editor is removed, or when the share link is revoked. No cap, no read-and-forget sweep.
- **Failure** — Two problems. **Privacy:** "Ali assigned you a task: 'call the financier about the shortfall'" is a verbatim copy of production content that now lives permanently in Sara's account, and it survives every mechanism the owner has for taking it back — unsharing, deleting the board, deleting their whole account (which purges the OWNER's notifications, not the ones they caused). Deleting your account under DL11 destroys the board and leaves its title in six other people's notification lists forever, pointing at an id that no longer resolves. **Growth:** an active production board with reminders and assignments writes rows indefinitely with no ceiling.
- **Mechanism** — Three clauses. (1) A TTL on `createdAt` — 90 days is generous for an in-app badge — one migration, and the unbounded-growth half disappears. (2) **Notifications reference rather than embed**: `{kind, actorId, boardId, elementId}` are already stored; the `Message` becomes a rendering concern resolved at read time against live content **and the reader's own ACL**, so a notification about content you can no longer see shows "a board you no longer have access to" rather than its title. That single change makes unshare, board deletion and account deletion propagate for free, because there is no copy left to propagate to. (3) `DeleteByActor`, called from `DeleteAccount` for the departing user's outbound notifications.
- **Hard clause** — **schema.** `Message` must stop being a stored string, or every one of the three propagation paths has to be remembered separately — the same enumerate-by-hand failure as DL12.

#### DL19 · A departed user's name and identifier stay welded to everything they touched, and the row that would translate it is gone
- **Evidence** — `DeleteAccount` calls `users.Delete(ctx, p.Sub)` at `:257` and `RemoveEditorEverywhere` at `:239` (which only `$pull`s the sub from `acl.editors`). Untouched **by design** (`:218-219`): every `Element.CreatedBy` on someone else's board, every `Element.DeletedBy` (which the trash panel's two-bucket split renders from), every `Comment.AuthorID`, every `Comment.Reactions` value, every `Transaction.UserID`, every `Notification.ActorID`. The frontend resolves subs to names through `POST /users/resolve` — which, after `users.Delete`, has nothing to return.
- **Failure** — The wrong outcome in **both** directions at once. For the departed person: their Keycloak subject id remains a durable identifier scattered across other people's data, which the system will never remove and cannot even enumerate — the opposite of erasure. For the people who stay: "Created by" and comment attribution across a whole production board silently degrade to a blank or a raw id, so the history of who decided what becomes unreadable exactly when the person is no longer there to ask. Neither party got what deletion is supposed to give them.
- **Mechanism** — **Tombstone rather than delete:** `users.Delete` is replaced by an anonymize step keeping a minimal row — `{sub, displayName: "Deleted user", deleted: true, deletedAt}` — with email, avatar, settings and agent instructions cleared. Attribution keeps resolving and stops identifying, which is what both parties actually need. Second clause: reaction maps hold raw subs as **values**, so `$pull` them from `Comment.Reactions` on deletion — the one place the identifier is not needed for attribution at all and is therefore pure residue (requires DL12's methods).
- **Hard clause** — **schema.** The tombstone must be a first-class state on the user document, not a magic sub, or every resolve caller grows a special case.

#### DL20 · Board history exists on the server, is reachable over the API, and no UI has ever shown it — so "Empty trash" is not the end of the content 🔴 *(deepens DA15)*
- **Evidence** — `transport/http/router.go:46` registers `GET /boards/:id/transactions` → `handlers.go:161-168` → `TransactionService.History`, which requires only `RequireView` and returns full `domain.Transaction` documents. `grep -rn "/transactions" frontend/src` returns **exactly one line — the POST**. Nothing reads it. What those documents contain: `Op.UndoChanges`, the precomputed inverse — meaning a delete op carries the deleted element's prior content **verbatim, permanently** (the journal has no delete method at all; DA15). So the content of an element that was trashed, then purged or Emptied, remains readable through this endpoint by any current editor — **including one invited after the deletion**, since History gates on RequireView and nothing else.
- **Failure** — Two things at once. **The user-facing loss:** the product HAS a complete, ordered, per-board change history with inverses and actor attribution, and offers the person nothing — no version browser, no "what changed last Tuesday", no restore-to-a-point. For a filmmaker whose board is a living document across a shoot, that is the single most obviously missing feature in this corner. **The trust loss:** the same absence means the product's permanent-delete gesture is contradicted by a live, unredacted, editor-readable endpoint. DA15 established that this journal is never purged and framed it as privacy-at-rest plus growth; the **reachability** half — that it is a queryable content archive available to collaborators over HTTP, defeating Empty Trash — is what turns a hygiene debt into a user-visible broken promise, and it is what makes DA15's retention sweep urgent rather than tidy.
- **Mechanism** — Build the surface AND close the leak in one wave, because shipping the reader over an unredacted journal would make things worse. (a) A history panel per board rendering transactions as human sentences, grouped by session, with actor and `Origin` (which already distinguishes human from agent) — read-only first. (b) **Restore-a-version composed from existing parts:** select a point, compute the inverse chain, and stage it as a PLAN through the agent's existing preview/ghost/one-transaction path, so the riskiest operation in the product inherits review and revert instead of inventing a new write path. (c) **The redaction:** once an element is hard-deleted, its `UndoChanges` content must be stripped from the journal — the row stays for audit ("Ali deleted a card"), the content does not. That is the clause that makes Empty Trash true, and the natural place to also implement DA15's `DeleteOlderThan`.
- **Hard clause** — **handler.** Content stripping must be driven by the same code path that hard-deletes, not by a sweeper, or there is a window in which Empty Trash has visibly not emptied anything.

#### DL21 · Your content persists inside a collaborator's agent journal after you delete your account — tenant-scoped purges cannot reach it 🔴 *(deepens MP7's scoping argument, from the deletion side)*
- **Evidence** — Agent purge is strictly tenant-keyed: `agent/reads.go:41-46` calls `events.DeleteByTenant` then `runs.DeleteByTenant`; `repository/mongo/agent.go:111-114` filters `{tenantSub: tenant}`. **`Run.Tenant` is the human who STARTED the run, not the board owner.** A collaborator running the agent on your board produces a run whose `Plan.Actions` carry your cards' titles and text, whose `Task.Intent` may quote your content, and whose events carry staged-action summaries — all filed under **their** sub. `DeleteAccount` purges only `p.Sub`'s tenant.
- **Failure** — You own the board, you delete your account, the elements are hard-deleted — and a verbatim partial copy of what was on it lives on indefinitely in another user's agent history, reachable by them through `GET /agent/runs/:id` for as long as that account exists, which under DL6 is forever. It is the same structural blind spot MP7 identified for **memory** (tenant-scoped, so the agent has amnesia across collaborators), read from the **deletion** side: the scoping key that makes memory too narrow makes erasure too narrow in exactly the same way.
- **Mechanism** — Runs gain `BoardOwnerSub`, stamped at admission from the root board's ACL — the value is already resolved there, since admission calls `RequireEdit`. Purge then runs on both axes: `DeleteByTenant` OR `DeleteByBoardOwner`. This is also the index PL9's account-wide run query and MP6's owner-visible audit both need, so **one field settles three items** — and MP6 is worth landing together, since a purge key nobody can read is untestable from the product.
- **Hard clause** — **schema.** Runs written before the field exists must be backfilled from the board id, or the purge silently misses exactly the historical runs a deletion request is about.

## T3 — frontier

#### DL22 · Nothing in the deployment expresses a data-processing posture — no residency, no retention setting, no zero-retention endpoint, no policy the product can state
- **Evidence** — `.env.example:46-64` is the entire agent configuration surface: `AGENT_PROVIDER`, two API keys, `AGENT_MODEL`, `AGENT_DAILY_CAP_USD`, two price overrides. **No region, no retention flag, no data-processing endpoint variant, no policy URL.** `cognition/select.go:26-46` is the whole provider-selection plane and takes `{Provider, Model, AnthropicAPIKey, GeminiAPIKey}`. `config/config.go` has no privacy-shaped field. There is no deploy manifest beyond `deploy/allow-origin.sh`, and no R2 bucket lifecycle policy anywhere in the repo despite `maintenance_service.go:12-14` explicitly deferring blob lifecycle to one that does not exist (DL1, DL2).
- **Mechanism** — The user is working in Oman on client film production. The questions a real client will eventually ask — where is this processed, is it retained by the provider, is it trained on, who is the processor, can we get a DPA — cannot be answered from this codebase, cannot be configured, and cannot be shown in the product. That is not a compliance checkbox; it is the reason a production company would decline to use the tool for anything under NDA, which is most of the work. It also blocks self-hosting as a differentiator: a deployment that **can** answer these questions has no way to say so. So: a `processing` config block — `{providerDisplayName, region, retentionPolicyUrl, dataProcessingEndpoint, trainingOptOut}` — doing exactly two things and no more: served by `GET /agent/capabilities` so DL8's disclosure line and the Privacy tab render the deployment's real posture rather than a hard-coded sentence, and, where the provider offers a distinct zero-retention or regional endpoint, selecting it in `cognition.New`. Deliberately configuration, not product logic: the product's job is to **state the posture truthfully** and let the operator set it. Pair it with the R2 lifecycle rule `maintenance_service.go` already assumes exists, and with a documented retention table — one row per collection — that DL6, DL12, DL18 and DA15 each fill in. **And the boot must refuse to enable the agent when a provider key is set and no processing block is configured, exactly as it already warns loudly for an unpriced model** — an undisclosed processor is the same class of silent wrong as an unpriced one.

---

# Priority map, revised

*This replaces the map above rather than sitting beside it. The wave numbers are
the same waves; the new items are folded into them, two waves are inserted where
sweep two found work the original map had no home for, and the section
"**What sweep two changes about the ordering**" names every move and why.*

## The law, with the two clauses sweep two earned

HORIZON's five clauses stand — schema, handler, compiler, digest,
nested-workspace probe — as does Part One's amendment that **a probe must assert
a rendered result, not a compiled op**. Sweep two adds two more terms and one
standing rule.

**Sixth clause: a capability that stages an action must name that action in a
form a person can HEAR.** AX5 and AX38 are the argument: the product's entire
trust story — preview, honest outcome, unmet, per-action revert, cost, drift — is
delivered as a visual mutation, with zero `aria-live` regions in the whole
frontend. A rendered result no assistive technology can perceive is the same
class of lie as a write that lands on a key nothing reads. And the announcement
string belongs to the **plan contract**, not to React: `phraseKeyFor` currently
reverse-engineers intent from a substring of a tool name because the intent was
never sent.

**Seventh clause, on lifetimes: any promise with a lifetime must render its
lifetime at the surface that makes the promise.** The product holds four
independent recovery guarantees with four different clocks — the 30-minute
delegation, the 2-hour proposal, the six-run revert horizon, the 90-day trash —
and displays none of them to anyone. The digest holds itself to exactly this
rule for the model ("… 12 more, read_board for the rest") on the stated grounds
that silent elision makes an agent look like it ignores instructions. Nowhere in
the tree is that argument applied to the person. (JN21, JN8, FR12, DL17.)

**And a standing rule, from AX24: no gesture ships without a typed verb behind
it.** The set reachable by neither pointer-free input nor the agent — lock, text
direction, template conversion, sketch, group-into-column — contains the
product's protection primitive and its bilingual primitive. Closing that set is
the acceptance criterion for the agent as an alternative channel (AX34), and
keeping it closed is what stops the next context-menu item reopening it.

**One counting note.** Part One found the hard clause was *digest* 31 times,
*handler* 34, *compiler* 17, *probe* 9, *schema* 3 — "not short of verbs, short
of sight". Across sweep two's 214 items the distribution moves sharply: *handler*
is the most common by a wide margin, *schema* rises from 3 to roughly 30, and
*probe* roughly triples. That is what leaving the single-run frame does to the
shape of the work: sight was the binding constraint on one run reading one board,
and **durability, authority and lifecycle** are the binding constraints on
everything else.

## The ten highest-leverage NEW items, in order

These interleave with Part One's ten rather than replacing them; the combined
ordering is in the waves below.

1. **DL11** — deleting your account hard-deletes every collaborator's work on your boards, silently, with no ownership transfer and no warning to the people losing it. Found independently by two territories. Irreversible, not soft-deleted, not exportable afterwards. Clause 1 of the mechanism — refuse the delete when an owned board has editors — is roughly a day and should not wait for a wave.
2. **FR1** — a 5-minute reconciler ticker force-fails every run that lasts longer than a tick, with a message naming a server restart that never happened, while the goroutine keeps spending and its spend is never recorded. This is the normal path, not the crash path, and it directly contradicts the reasoning written into `contracts.go:216-221`.
3. **DL3** — on the production storage driver every uploaded image and PDF dies at 7 days, and the recovery route the code's own comment promises does not exist. Total, silent, unrecoverable-by-the-user data loss on the content a filmmaker's boards are made of, and it blinds `look_at` at the same moment.
4. **FR23** — the HTTP error boundary replaces six of the agent's eight hand-written refusal messages with the single word "conflict". Two switch arms. This is the root cause of a whole layer of unhelpfulness the prior documents attribute to the agent's judgement.
5. **DL5** — a read-only share-link holder can read the board's own edit token via `?format=json`, plus every collaborator's subject id and every nested board's share tokens. MP2 is about what an edit-link holder can make the agent do; this is how a viewer **becomes** one. Same severity class as DA1.
6. **JN5 + JN6** — `textPreview` has two writers with a 40× contract gap and is the sole input to search, export and the agent's perception; the corrupting writer is the user opening the agent's work to read it. And `set_note_text` overwrites a document from a 200-character read, which corrects M3 and is a live destruction path.
7. **AX1 + AX24** — `tabIndex` appears nowhere in the frontend, so no card can ever receive focus; and the capability set reachable by neither keyboard nor agent contains lock and text direction. Together these are the gate on the agent-as-channel inversion, and they argue for designing the keyboard path and the agent path as **one** path.
8. **IL1 + IL2** — the proposed plan is overwritten by the applied plan at commit, so the correction diff is destroyed and **cannot be backfilled**; and cascade drops are indistinguishable from human drops, so the first miner learns a 10× lie. LP1 is unbuildable retroactively and wrong on its commonest shape. Every hour this is not shipped is an hour of unrecoverable labels.
9. **SC3 + SC1** — at 9,331 elements the digest shows 230 items, all containers and no leaves, and its header reports the scope's size as the board's size; and the digest is never prompt-cached, so it is re-billed on every one of 24 turns and silently converts `MaxSteps=24` into `MaxSteps≈5` as the workspace grows.
10. **FR4 + FR5** — a run that fails after committing can never be reverted and its copy says the board was restored; and the write path applies ops one at a time with no rollback and journals afterwards, so a mid-loop failure leaves elements with **no transaction row and therefore no inverse anywhere in the system**. Together with FR22 these are the states a person cannot get out of.

**Next five, close behind:** DF33 (the template gallery ships empty; eight film
templates are the cheapest domain competence in either document and have zero
code dependencies), DL1 (the deletion dialog's central promise is false), FR12
(the grant expires at 30 minutes and the offer lives for 2 hours — documented in
prose as a known defect and shipped), SC5 (eleven no-selector store
subscriptions, so a collaborator's cursor re-renders every card on the board),
DF14 (the table digest truncates at 6×5, making every canonical film document
unreadable on re-read).

## What sweep two changes about the ordering

Six moves, each with its reason. Everything not named here keeps its place.

1. **WAVE 0 grows by four axes and becomes the largest single dependency in the
   plan.** M5 (flatland), MP11 (single-player) and CG4 (run twice) are joined by
   SC22 (never leaves the small), IL17 (never leaves the un-delegated), AX33
   (never leaves the sighted) and DF36 (never leaves the domain-ungraded). Seven
   axes on one instrument. IL17 is the sharpest: the corpus's one write path
   commits with a bare principal carrying **no delegation**, so the entire
   authorization envelope — the layer the product's safety architecture lives
   on — has never been exercised against model-built ops. That is why DA2 and
   DA11 needed throwaway Go probes to prove.
2. **The FR cluster moves ahead of WAVE 3 (sight).** You cannot usefully build
   sight for an agent whose runs are killed at five minutes (FR1), whose Stop is
   a placebo that keeps spending (FR2), whose failures after a commit cannot be
   undone (FR4), and whose write path can leave elements with no inverse (FR5).
   These become **WAVE 2b**, immediately after security.
3. **WAVE 2 absorbs the byte layer and the share plane.** Part One's security
   wave was about read/write boundaries inside Mongo. The object store was never
   in it (DL1, DL2, DL3, DL4), and DL5 is a privilege escalation of the same
   class as DA1 sitting in a `json` tag. Retention (DL6) belongs here too,
   because it is the second collection ignoring the privacy tab after DA15.
4. **LP1 moves later and IL1 moves to the front — the single largest ordering
   change.** LP1 was #10 in Part One's top ten. It is now blocked: the proposal
   is overwritten at commit, so the correction corpus for every run already
   applied is gone, and each further day destroys more. IL1 (split the field) and
   IL2 (drop provenance) must ship before LP1 is designed, not with it.
5. **PL4 drops out of T1.** It rests on "the PWA already exists"; MO10 shows it
   does not meaningfully exist on iOS, and MO18 shows its POST share target has
   no receiver in the shipped service worker. PL4 now sits behind MO10 and MO11.
6. **DF33 ships outside the wave structure entirely.** Authoring eight real film
   templates is content work with **no code dependency**, and it changes what the
   product *is* for this user more than any single engineering item in either
   document. It should be in the first week regardless of what else is.

## Product decisions added to the list

Part One's ten stand. Sweep two adds seven, each stated as the choice.

- **DL8 / DL22 · Disclosure and processing posture.** Does the product tell the user their board is sent to Anthropic or Google, where, and under what retention — and is that a config the operator sets or a sentence the product hard-codes? Everything in the DL corner's trust half hangs off this one record, and today the word "Anthropic" appears nowhere a user can see it.
- **DL11 · What happens when an owner leaves.** Refuse the account deletion, force a transfer, or orphan the board to a tombstone owner? The engineering is small; the policy is not, and the current answer — destroy everyone's work silently — is not a policy anyone chose.
- **DL16 · Whether content can opt out of the agent.** A per-element `agentExclude` is one boolean and one predicate. The decision is whether the product wants to be usable for material the user will not send to a third party — which for this user is a large fraction of the real work.
- **AX32 / LP9 · Whether preview mode is required to be operable without a pointer before autonomy is offered.** Today auto-apply is the only accessible path, so the product's own affordances push excluded users into the mode with no review. Making the review operable is two days; deciding it is a shipping gate is a product decision.
- **MO17 · Whether mobile gets a different projection or a shrunk desktop.** The agent-first mobile mode adds no capability and removes none — it is a renderer over the same run lifecycle. But it is a statement about what QomraNote is on a phone, and it is the only version that works on the device this user carries onto a set.
- **DF31 · Whether the product models ROLE.** The same 64 scenes are act structure to the writer and a stripboard to the AD, and the prompt's organising aesthetics currently assume one right shape per material. A role dimension is one field and one question; it is also the difference between a template library and a colleague.
- **SC22 / IL18 · Whether the eval sweep becomes a tracked artefact with a cost line.** Running the corpus at three workspace scales, N times, with a checked-in history, costs real money per sweep. The alternative is continuing to establish rates by hand and record them in prose.

---

# What is STILL unexplored after two sweeps

*Stated so nobody mistakes 214 items and four documents for coverage. The first
three exclusions are Part One's and stand: computer-use, generative-UI protocols,
and the deliberate parking of X5. Sweep two's researchers add: commercials and
branded-content workflow (agency approvals, PPM, versioning matrices — different
enough to be its own sweep), animation and VFX-heavy pipelines (asset tracking is
a different data model), episodic TV (writers' room, cross-boarding, episodic
DOOD), live and multicam, cognitive accessibility of the agent's prose register,
the Keycloak-hosted auth pages (outside this repo), and the WCAG conformance
statement — though the honest answer today is "does not meet Level A" on 1.3.1,
2.1.1, 2.1.2, 2.4.3, 2.4.4 and 4.1.2.*

**Nine territories have never been swept at all.** Named, so nobody assumes they
were:

1. **Security in the adversarial sense.** No sweep has attacked this product. No
   attempt to execute a prompt injection end to end; no fuzzing of tool-call
   JSON; no measurement of the quarantine threshold's false-negative rate; no
   SSRF probe against `LinkService`; no auth-bypass sweep of the sixty-plus HTTP
   routes. DA1 and DL5 were found by **reading**, not by attacking — which means
   the defects a reader would not notice are all still there, and IL24 says
   nobody would know if they were being exploited.
2. **The build, deploy and operational plane.** Nobody has looked at the
   Dockerfiles, the CI pipeline beyond noting what it lacks, secret handling,
   migration safety, or rollback with a schema change in flight. Most sharply:
   **is there any database backup at all?** Nothing in four documents establishes
   one, and `GAPS_AUDIT_2026-07.md` notes Mongo is a standalone node with no
   replica set — which SC12 now wants a session on. A product whose deletion
   paths are this leaky and whose backup posture is unknown has an unquantified
   worst case.
3. **Cost and pricing as a business.** SC2 shows the meter under-reports; SC1
   shows one big-board run can cost 4× its own cap. Nobody has modelled what a
   real user costs per month, what the margin is, or whether this can be sold at
   any price. PL6 governs spend; nothing values it.
4. **The frontend's own correctness.** 11,430 lines, and both sweeps read it only
   for agent-adjacent behaviour. No sweep of the canvas maths (zoom/pan/hit-test
   edge cases), the line engine's geometry, the Tiptap integration, the clipboard,
   or the undo stack's own invariants beyond JN12 and FR22.
5. **The realtime layer under adversarial conditions.** SC9 found a
   process-killing panic path by reading. Nobody has load-tested the hub, tested
   reconnection storms, or verified message ordering under concurrent commits.
6. **Internationalisation beyond the two dictionaries.** Number, date and
   currency formatting; Arabic shaping in exports and PDFs; font fallback; and —
   the one nobody in either sweep could assess — **whether the Arabic is
   idiomatic**. No native reader has judged the register, and DF35 argues the
   register is the whole question in this trade.
7. **Anything a real user actually did.** Every finding in all four documents
   comes from reading code or literature. There is no session recording, no
   usability test, no support-ticket corpus, and no telemetry — IL exists
   precisely because there is none. **The single largest unexplored territory is
   what happens when the filmmaker in Oman uses this on a Tuesday**, and after
   two sweeps it remains entirely unmeasured. That is why WAVE 0 and the IL
   corner matter more than their tier suggests.
8. **Legal and contractual posture.** DL22 names the config gap. Nobody has read
   whether the deployment has a DPA with its provider, what the provider's terms
   say about training, or what Omani and GCC data law require of a product
   holding client production material.
9. **The Keycloak realm configuration itself** — token lifetimes, refresh
   behaviour, session limits, brute-force policy — sits outside this repo and has
   never been examined. FR and DL both bump into it: DL10's revocation window is,
   in the end, a token-lifetime question, and nobody knows the number.

---

*This document now carries 359 items across two parts — 145 from sweep one, 214
from sweep two — and the honest summary of the second is one sentence: **the
agent is the best-built thing in this product, and almost
everything that will make it feel like a colleague is now outside the agent.**
The five clauses still govern every wave — schema, handler, compiler, digest,
probe — plus the amendment that a probe must assert a rendered result, the sixth
clause that it must assert a perceivable one, and the seventh that every promise
with a lifetime must show its clock. A capability whose write lands on a key
nothing reads is a lie the agent reads back and believes. A guarantee whose
expiry nothing displays is a lie the person believes instead.*
